Vimian · VIMIAN-SE · the new Memo conveyor's card of 10 November 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote behind each of the eleven ratings: Vimian

The Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions and its 13 group judgements, 57,796 words, set out under the eleven ratings: each rating, then each of its lettered sub-items, then the answers beneath.

Word for word. Every answer is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written; a script checked that every word is on this page, in order. What is changed is layout only: answers are grouped by rating rather than by layer; each answer is titled with the question as it was set (the Investment Analyst's own heading appears beneath only where it adds words); an answer that belongs under more than one sub-item is shown in full once, at its first place, and as a one-line link everywhere else.

Which answer sits where comes from the same table the ratings map uses. Each answer carries its layer and group, because "positive" means something different in each layer. Each layer's own material (what it was asked, what its scores mean, the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices) is at the end, so nothing is left out.

The ratings alone: the ratings map.

How to read the scores and letters

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning (listed just below).

Where the Rater has scored this stock (every memo from 3 November 2026), the number in each pill is the Rater's, set after the memo was written from the Investment Analyst's judgements against a key per group of questions; the Investment Analyst's own score is kept in the pill's tooltip. The letter beside each score is read from the one canonical source for ratings, the same file the eleven ratings page and the memo scorecards read; nothing is graded on this page. The letter follows the item's rule: for most items this stock's rank among the stocks scored on the same scale on that same question or group judgement, re-cut each night (Richard's ruling of 4-Nov-26: every letter by rank; in the file's words: A above 90%; B+ above 82.5% to 90%; B above 75% to 82.5%; C+ above 66.67% to 75%; C above 58.33% to 66.67%; C- above 50% to 58.33%; D above 25% to 50%; F up to 25%); on the External change forces and Internal change forces items the number carries a sign (50 = neither positive nor negative) and the letter is the rank, so read the two together. “57th percentile” means the stock beats 57% of them; a dashed outline marks a provisional grade (fewer than 30 stocks scored).

What the scores mean in each layer

Layer 1

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 2

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 3 and Layer 4

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

Inputs

1

External headwinds/tailwinds

F4122nd percentile

Memo topic 1, Tailwinds in the operating environment: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

External change forces

Layer 1 · External change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

The environment neither carries nor sinks the case. The overall picture is a mild net headwind: end-market volume has contracted for eighteen quarters, the broad market has turned promotional, online disintermediation is forming, and currency and rates erode reported growth and EPS. Against it stand slow structural positives in Vimian's own niches (dental, allergy, diagnostics, stewardship) and specific openings such as the DePuy Synthes separation.

The decisive observation is the gap: Vimian grew organically 11% to 12% in mid-2026 while US visits fell 2%. The company is doing the work, not the market.

Three implications for the Consumer's model:

  1. Do not add a market recovery before 2028. Base organic growth on niche penetration, price and self-help at about the company's two-year rate (about 8%).
  2. Haircut reported growth for currency. Model a negative translation effect in 2027 on the consensus EUR/USD path, and treat it as unhedged.
  3. Carry the elective orthopaedic line as the cyclical swing variable in downside cases. Carry the immunotherapy line as the one exposed to industry price-war spill-over.

The external picture is stable to slightly worsening over the next six months, with the skew to the downside if the consumer squeeze deepens.

1a

External headwinds/threats

6 answers
C-0243C-0547C-0738C-0950C-3545C-3942
C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

C-4354th percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: competitive intensity is rising sharply in the broad companion-animal therapeutics market and is stable and benign in Vimian's niches. Net, a mild negative. The one real spill-over risk is to Nextmune's dermatology and allergy franchise, through cheaper symptomatic drugs.

  • The broad market has turned promotional.
  • Zoetis recorded a 1% organic revenue decline in Q3 2026 on flat volumes and lower net price, and is "giving margin back" to clinics and owners (Jefferies, 5 November 2026).
  • Morgan Stanley estimates Zoetis' gross-to-net adjustments imply an 8% fall in companion-animal net price realisation in H2 2026.
  • Elanco grew 9% organically in Q3 on new launches. Zenrelia is in over 50% of US practices and Credelio Quattro in over 40%, at lower prices than incumbents.
  • Merck's Numelvi is challenging Apoquel. Branded generics offer up to 50% discounts.
  • Why the spill-over risk matters. Nextmune's allergen-specific immunotherapy (diagnosed by PAX tests) is a disease-modifying alternative to lifelong symptomatic drugs (Apoquel, Cytopoint, Zenrelia, Numelvi). If those drugs get cheaper through promotions, the relative cost of immunotherapy rises. Lower-income owners may then choose the cheaper symptomatic route.
  • No evidence of this yet: Specialty Pharma grew 11% underlying in Q3 with double-digit allergy and dermatology growth, and its margin rose to 30.3%.
  • It is the most plausible channel through which the industry price war reaches Vimian.
  • The niches are benign.
  • Veterinary orthopaedics: Movora is the only full-suite offer, against the small veterinary units of DePuy Synthes and Arthrex. Johnson & Johnson is separating DePuy Synthes (USD 9.2bn of sales, 18 to 24 months), and a former Zimmer Biomet director says smaller players win share during such transitions. Low-cost orthopaedic copycats at the edges are an early-warning item in the Risks report, not an observed trend.
  • Dental: fragmented, with iM3 the global leader.
  • Veterinary buying groups: VetFamily is described as the only global platform.
  • Diagnostics: intense, and against Vimian. IDEXX holds 72% of point-of-care equipment and 56% of reference labs, and Asian AI analysers are entering. Indical is marginal there.
  • The weakest listed rival in Vimian's nearest space is getting weaker. Swedencare grew 2% organically in Q3 2026 with North America at -4%, and its CEO is departing. That lowers pressure in over-the-counter dental and supplements.
  • Direction: tightening at the blockbuster level, neutral in the niches, adverse in diagnostics.
C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

C+4767th percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: input costs are rising roughly in line with revenue and are being passed through, so the net effect is a mild headwind to absolute profit, neutral to the gross margin. Supplier concentration (Taiwan for MedTech) is the real exposure: a tail risk, not a trend.

  • Cost growth has tracked revenue.
  • Raw materials and merchandise were EUR 132.2m in 2025 (+13.5% on EUR 116.5m), and EUR 73.2m in H1 2026 (+13% on EUR 64.8m). Revenue grew 13.4% in 2025 and about 12.5% in H1 2026.
  • Gross margin has been held at 68.9% in 2024 and 2025 (69.2% in 2023).
  • Personnel costs rose 14% to EUR 108.2m in 2025, slightly faster than revenue, reflecting commercial investment (the US field force, new countries).
  • Pass-through is working. Average price increases of about 5% were implemented, and wage inflation was passed to veterinary customers (Business Foundations). This is nominal pass-through, not a real margin gain (see C-17).
  • The concentration and availability risks:
  • About 20% of cost of goods is linked to Taiwan suppliers.
  • Titanium and stainless steel prices drive implant costs.
  • Florida MedTech operations are exposed to hurricanes.
  • Active pharmaceutical ingredients come from China and India (sector-wide).
  • Mitigants: 92% of key suppliers audited (2025), right-shoring, and Diagnostics production consolidated from four sites to two (cutting fixed costs).
  • Sensitivity: a combined 10% rise in raw material and external costs would cost about EUR 10m of operating profit, about 9% of adjusted EBITA, on the historical structure (prospectus). The business can absorb a gradual rise through price, not a sudden one.
  • For the model: hold gross margin at 68.5% to 69.5%. Treat MedTech's cost of goods as the line exposed to a tariff or supply shock.
C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

D3840th percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: macro acts on Vimian mainly through three channels: household affordability (industry level), currency translation, and floating-rate debt (company level). It bites hardest at company level, on reported (not organic) growth and on EPS. Vimian reports in euros, sells 38% in US dollars, hedges nothing, carries floating-rate debt, and is priced in krona.

  • Industry level (demand). Household squeeze, rates and veterinary price inflation depress clinic volumes (C-01). This bites the elective US orthopaedic business most. The FfF regime (6 November 2026) is risk-off beneath index highs, with US ten-year yields above 5% and no in-window evidence of rate cuts. The relief DNB Carnegie expected is not in the data.
  • Company level (currency).
  • The US dollar was 38.19% of 2025 revenue. Translation was -2% in FY2025, -5% in Q1 2026 and -1% in Q2 2026. The Q3 effect is undisclosed, but reported growth of 15% against organic 11% plus acquisitions suggests a small negative.
  • A 10% USD move against the euro shifts revenue by about EUR 15.7m and EBIT by about EUR 2.3m. The margin effect is muted because about 30% of costs are in dollars against 35% of sales.
  • Vimian uses no currency derivatives. Peers hedge: IDEXX 75% to 85%, Demant 70% to 100%.
  • Bank forecasts of EUR/USD at 1.20 to 1.23 (Deutsche Bank, Morgan Stanley) imply continued translation drag in 2027.
  • The share price is in SEK. Krona strength against the euro (EUR/SEK forecast down towards 10.20 to 10.40) lowers the SEK value of the same euro earnings.
  • Company level (rates).
  • Average interest cost was 4.1% (Q1 2026). Only half of the EUR 150m bond is swapped to fixed. A 1-point rate rise costs about EUR 2.0m of pre-tax profit, about 3% of FY2026 consensus pre-tax profit.
  • Net financial items were EUR 20.6m in FY2025, about 20% of adjusted EBITA. Consensus assumes a sharp fall (C-76), which a higher-for-longer regime makes harder.
  • Sector level: healthcare is being bought as a defensive (relative strength to A over two months; Pharma/biotech Overweight in the FfF table). The market is choosing defensives and selling leveraged ones. Fragility is Double Underweight, a negative for a small, leveraged, thin-float stock like Vimian.
  • Where it bites hardest: reported revenue growth (2 to 5 points of currency drag in bad quarters) and EPS (interest and tax). The organic operating line is affected only through the US orthopaedic volume channel.
C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

–50

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: four other external dynamics matter. One is clearly positive (the DePuy Synthes separation as a share-gain window). Two are negative (sector sentiment and capital flows against small roll-ups; clinic consolidation eroding VetFamily's addressable pool in mature markets). One is a small dated positive (the VOI litigation recovery).

  • DePuy Synthes separation (positive, 18 to 36 months). Johnson & Johnson's separation of its USD 9.2bn orthopaedics unit, which includes the veterinary business Movora competes with, creates distraction at a rival. A former Zimmer Biomet director says smaller European players win share in such transitions. Undated and unquantified, but a plausible 2027-2028 tailwind to US orthopaedics.
  • Sector and style flows (negative).
  • "Sector capital outflows" took Vimian down 17% year to date to February 2026 with nothing company-specific.
  • The Risks report describes the climate as "hostile to complex roll-ups" (VusionGroup, Eurofins, FTAI Aviation).
  • The FfF regime has Fragility at Double Underweight: small, leveraged and complex names get sold first in a risk-off tape.
  • Vimian has run +44.5% in twelve months and is in the 94th relative-strength percentile. That makes it a candidate for profit-taking if the healthcare rotation reverses.
  • Clinic consolidation (slow negative).
  • Corporate groups control about 30% of US general practices and 80% of specialty clinics, and 60% to 80% of UK practices.
  • Each consolidation removes an independent from VetFamily's natural pool and moves purchasing to central tenders that squeeze supplier margins.
  • VetFamily's response is geographic: Japan, Portugal, Ireland (Vetsave, 150 clinics), now 14 markets and 12,400 members. Its mature-market growth therefore depends increasingly on taking share among a shrinking pool of independents.
  • VOI litigation (small positive).
  • The Delaware court awarded USD 40.2m plus USD 8.6m of interest against the last VOI seller, who paid USD 31.9m in Q4 2025.
  • A USD 6.2m receivable is classed as doubtful. Final judgment on appeal was expected by September 2026; the Q3 files do not report the outcome.
  • This is a one-off cash item worth up to about EUR 5m. Do not model it as recurring.
  • FfF environment fit. Healthcare is the defensive the market is paying for (relative strength to A). But Vimian is a leveraged, small-cap, acquisition-led name inside it. On the FfF's own question ("which kind of defensive is it?"), it is not the clean kind. The rotation helps the group, not necessarily this stock.
C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

D4530th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: all three kinds of new entrant are present. Adjacent-market entrants are the most important threat, through ecosystem platforms aimed at the independent-clinic economics VetFamily serves. Orthogonal entrants (non-surgical orthopaedics) threaten Movora's demand but are being slowed by safety concerns. Standard low-cost entrants are a threat at the price end of diagnostics and generics, not yet in Vimian's product lines.

  • Adjacent entrants:
  • Covetrus-MWI: practice software plus distribution.
  • Chewy-Modern Animal: online pharmacy plus 47 clinics.
  • Mars: clinics, Antech labs, Heska point-of-care.
  • IDEXX: CoVet AI assistant.
  • Each owns a software or clinic control point that can route purchasing and fulfilment around an independent buying group.
  • Threat to VetFamily: moderate and slow. Threat to Specialty Pharma's clinic channel: moderate.
  • Standard entrants:
  • Viyash's 66-product companion pipeline targets USD 150m to 200m by FY2033.
  • Branded generics with 50% discounts.
  • Mindray and other Asian AI analysers in diagnostics.
  • Orthopaedic copycats.
  • Threat: low for Movora and Nextmune today, moderate for Indical.
  • Orthogonal or innovative entrants:
  • Monoclonal pain therapies (Librela, Solensia) and PetVivo's Spryng injection against orthopaedic surgery.
  • Liquid biopsy (VolitionRx Nu.Q, licensed to IDEXX and Antech) in diagnostics.
  • VBX-1000, a cathepsin K inhibitor targeted for 2030, against periodontal surgery (its sponsor claims up to 60% of a USD 2bn market).
  • AI diagnostics.
  • Threat: moderate for Movora, eased by Librela's safety controversy. Long-dated for iM3.
  • Barriers that slow all of them:
  • Regulatory registration (FDA CVM, EMA; separate USDA pathway for biologics).
  • Clinical training infrastructure.
  • Direct-to-clinic commercial reach.
  • These are well established in the research.
C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

D4239th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: suppliers are a source of moderate risk rather than support. Vimian outsources most manufacturing to contract manufacturers. About 20% of cost of goods is concentrated in Taiwan. MedTech in the US is effectively a distributor of imported metal implants. Supplier costs are rising about as fast as revenue. The mitigants are real but standard.

  • Exposure:
  • Outsourced manufacturing (asset-light: capex was 3.55% of sales in 2025).
  • About 20% of consolidated cost of goods linked to Taiwan suppliers.
  • Titanium and stainless steel for implants.
  • Hurricane exposure at the Florida operations.
  • Raw material and merchandise costs up 13% in H1 2026.
  • DNB Carnegie names "responsible supply chain management" a key sustainability risk because of the outsourced model (23 October 2026).
  • Mitigants:
  • 92% of key suppliers audited against the code of conduct (2025; 94% in 2024).
  • Right-shoring and renegotiated terms. Jefferies says these limit the tariff impact to a low single-digit point of EBITA.
  • Diagnostics' own plants consolidated to two.
  • Price increases of about 5%.
  • Bargaining power: Vimian's scale with contract manufacturers is modest. VetFamily's buying power (rebates from major suppliers' marketing budgets) is the one place where supplier relationships are a support.
  • For the model: keep MedTech cost of goods as a sensitivity. A 10% input shock is worth about EUR 10m of operating profit. A Taiwan disruption is a low-probability, high-impact scenario for MedTech supply, not a base-case item.
1b

External tailwinds

7 answers
C-0140C-0345C-0454C-0850C-3255C-3760C-4366
C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

F4017th percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: end-market volume is in a prolonged slump that is part structural and part cyclical, with no visible turn in the next 6 to 12 months. Vimian's own demand has decoupled from that volume through its chronic, consumable and niche exposures. The one exposed piece, elective orthopaedics, is cyclical, and its recovery has no two-year visibility.

  • The volume trend is negative and not yet inflecting.
  • US clinic visits fell 2.2% year on year in Q3 2026 (Vetsource), the eighteenth consecutive negative quarter, after -1.9% in Q2 (Morgan Stanley AlphaWise).
  • Discretionary procedures fell 2.5% sequentially and wellness visits fell 1.7% (Morgan Stanley, October 2026). Practice revenue was flat because price offset volume.
  • Only 48% of veterinarians expect positive traffic in the next twelve months. 93% say macro conditions hurt volumes.
  • Our own sector digest (7 October 2026) expects European clinic volumes to keep contracting through 2027.
  • The cause is mostly structural.
  • Veterinary prices are rising 10% to 12% a year against weak income growth (Aon expert, October 2026). Morgan Stanley forecasts real income growth of 1.2% in 2026 and 1.7% in 2027.
  • Ownership among 18 to 34 year-olds is back at pre-pandemic levels.
  • That is post-pandemic normalisation plus an affordability squeeze created by the clinics' own pricing. Lower interest rates alone will not reverse it.
  • The cyclical element is household budgets and rates. The FfF regime reads rates as higher for longer (US ten-year above 5%), so DNB Carnegie's expected relief from rate cuts in late 2026 is a hope, not a forecast.
  • Value of care is holding better than volume. Acute visits rose 2.2%, and pet spending grew 6% to 13% a year in past US downturns. This is why Vimian's mix matters:
  • Allergy immunotherapy and dermatology (chronic, diagnosis-led, customised): low exposure to volume.
  • Dental equipment and consumables: 80% of adult pets have periodontal disease, and private-practice dental consumable spending is rising 10% to 20% a year (expert). Under-penetrated.
  • Orthopaedic implants: elective and deferrable, the most exposed. North American volumes have been soft since summer 2024. Management describes US surgical market conditions as "unchanged" while its own US orthopaedic sales grow at low single digits through self-help.
  • Size of the decoupling. Group organic growth was 9%, 12% and 11% in Q1 to Q3 2026, against visit volumes of about -2%. Vimian is outgrowing volume by 11 to 14 points. That gap is product mix, share gain and self-help, not market demand.
  • Skew over the next 12 months: two-sided on volume, negative at the tail. A further leg down in discretionary spending would hit US orthopaedics first. MedTech is 52% North American, and the segment's recovery is what lifted group organic growth from 5% to 11-12%.
  • For the model:
  • Assume no market-volume tailwind before 2028.
  • Treat Vimian's organic growth as niche penetration plus price, not market growth.
  • Haircut the orthopaedic line in any downside case.
C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

D4528th percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: three disruption forces act on Vimian. Online pharmacy and software-led disintermediation is strengthening and acts against VetFamily and the clinic channel. Injectable pain therapies as a substitute for orthopaedic surgery are being partly disproved by safety concerns. AI in practice workflows is strengthening but is closer to a tailwind than a threat for Vimian. Net: a slow, mild headwind, smaller than the memo's D implied.

  • Online pharmacy and software are strengthening.
  • Chewy fills about 12% of written prescriptions (Morgan Stanley). Owners planning online fulfilment are projected to reach 10%.
  • Chewy now has 47 Modern Animal clinics. Covetrus-MWI combines 40% practice-management software share with distribution.
  • This erodes clinic pharmacy margins and the economics of the independent clinics VetFamily serves. It also threatens VetFamily's role as the purchasing intermediary if software routes clinics directly to fulfilment.
  • Offsets:
    • 79% of practices now use revenue-sharing digital platforms to retain prescriptions.
    • Long-acting injectables (Zoetis' Convenia RTU, October 2026) keep spend in the clinic.
    • California's out-of-state pharmacy licensing (full enforcement 2026) raises barriers for online sellers.
  • VetFamily membership kept growing through this (10,948 in 2025, 11,900 in Q2, 12,400 in Q3 2026). The force is real but has not yet bitten the buying group's volumes.
  • Injectables versus surgery: weakened.
  • Monoclonal antibody pain therapies (Librela, Solensia) let owners defer orthopaedic surgery.
  • But the FDA issued a warning letter on Librela's neurological adverse events. 48% of practitioners became more cautious prescribing it. Zoetis' shares fell 21.5% in one day partly on this narrative.
  • The substitute is thus losing credibility at the margin, and the strongest version of this risk is weaker than a year ago.
  • Slower threats: regenerative products (8.8% a year growth) and a cathepsin K inhibitor for dental disease targeting a 2030 launch.
  • AI: more opportunity than threat for Vimian.
  • Veterinary AI use rose from 39.2% in 2024 to 83.7% in 2026 (Digitail and AAHA). IDEXX bought CoVet, an AI clinical assistant, in September 2026.
  • Vimian participates rather than being disrupted: Clarity XR dental imaging (David AI), OvaCyte AI parasitology, and the Pet Allergy Explorer platform launched at Q3.
  • The risk is in diagnostics, where IDEXX's software-plus-instrument ecosystem deepens lock-in that Indical cannot match.
  • Direction on Vimian:
  • VetFamily faces a slow headwind.
  • Movora's substitution risk is easing.
  • Diagnostics faces a relative headwind.
  • Dental and allergy see a small tailwind from AI tools.
C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

C-5453rd percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: political and regulatory change is under way and on balance neutral to mildly helpful. The largest feared item, US pharmaceutical tariffs, has been resolved in animal health's favour. The residual risk is device and component tariffs on MedTech.

  • Section 232 tariffs. The US imposed 100% tariffs on imported patented drugs and explicitly excluded animal health and orphan products (Bernstein, June 2026). This removes a tail risk for Specialty Pharma.
  • The sector digest notes tariff exemptions end on 20 January 2029, a dated item for the outer years.
  • Device and component tariffs remain the live risk.
  • About half of US sales are exposed, with a "low-single-digit percentage point" hit to EBITA before mitigation (Jefferies).
  • About 20% of cost of goods comes from Taiwan suppliers. The US orthopaedic business operates largely as a distributor of imported plates, screws and tools.
  • Mitigation: right-shoring, supplier renegotiation, price.
  • UK CMA. The final remedies (March and September 2026) imposed transparency, not price caps. Vimian, as a supplier, is outside scope. The indirect effect is that UK clinic margins are squeezed (published prices, the prescription fee cap of £21, National Insurance), so UK clinics will press suppliers harder on price.
  • Antimicrobial stewardship is a structural tailwind. EU Farm to Fork targets a 50% cut in farm antimicrobial sales by 2030, pushing demand to vaccines, diagnostics and preventive care. This helps Indical's livestock diagnostics and the allergy and dermatology franchise.
  • Compliance costs are rising but small: the EU Veterinary Medicinal Products Regulation, CSRD, Union Product Database and US state pharmacy licensing.
  • ESG standing: MSCI upgraded Vimian to AAA in May 2026, a small visibility positive for institutional ownership.
  • Fragility: low for Specialty Pharma, moderate for MedTech, because the device tariff decision is political and not yet made.
C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

F5016th percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: no industry or sector-level driver is accelerating Vimian's demand now. The acceleration is company-driven (self-help in MedTech, dental and cross-selling). Two slow structural drivers could add from 2027 to 2029: the ageing of pets adopted in 2020 to 2022 into chronic-care ages, and antimicrobial stewardship pushing diagnostics and prevention. Neither is visible in the data yet.

  • Pet humanisation and premiumisation are intact in value but fading in volume. The animal-health and pet-care theme de-rated in 2026: Zoetis' guidance cuts, Swedencare's misses, and IDEXX cutting its clinic-visit outlook to -1.5%.
  • The pandemic cohort ageing. About two million UK lockdown pets, and their US equivalents, are now four to six years old. Peer research on CVS Group (October 2026) says the cohort is "starting to drive higher clinical care intensity and diagnostic testing volumes". This lands in exactly Vimian's categories (allergy, dental, orthopaedics), but its timing is not measured. Treat it as an upside option for 2028 onwards, not a base-case driver.
  • Growth in the diagnostics market. The veterinary diagnostics market is projected to grow 9.6% a year to 2031 (USD 8.73bn to 15.05bn), and only 20% of diagnostic opportunities are captured. Vimian's Indical is too small and livestock-heavy to ride it fully. The I-Vet companion pivot is the attempt.
  • Antimicrobial stewardship (EU Farm to Fork) supports vaccines, diagnostics and preventive dermatology. Structural, slow, small for Vimian.
  • Dental under-penetration: 80% of adult pets have periodontal disease, and practice dental consumables spend is rising 10% to 20% a year (expert). This is the closest thing to an industry driver actually accelerating Vimian's demand, through iM3, AllAccem's SANOS sealant and Clarity XR. Its scale at industry level is not measured in the research.
  • Clinic consolidation is accelerating, but it is a mixed driver. It strengthens buyer power against suppliers and shrinks the pool of independents VetFamily serves in mature markets. It increases the value of a buying group to the independents that remain.
C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

D5538th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: customer demand supports the thesis in Vimian's chronic, consumable and buying-group categories, which make up most of revenue. It supports it less in elective orthopaedics. The support is moderately fragile, because the clinic customer is under margin pressure and the end owner is under budget pressure. Neither has turned.

  • Who the customer is.
  • The clinic is the purchaser: over 17,000 clinics and laboratories, no customer above 10% of revenue.
  • The owner is the payer: cash-pay, about 94% companion animals.
  • Specialty Pharma also sells nutrition through a leading US retailer through campaigns.
  • Support:
  • Organic growth above the market in every segment in 2026.
  • VetFamily membership up from 10,948 (2025) to 12,400 (Q3 2026).
  • Cross-selling uptake.
  • Dental demand "robust" in North America and Asia-Pacific.
  • Allergy and dermatology growing double digit.
  • Movora educated 1,300 veterinarians in one quarter, which drives adoption.
  • Fragility:
  • Clinics face falling visits, rising wages, UK National Insurance and CMA costs, and pharmacy leakage of up to 50% for some VetFamily partners. They are cost-sensitive buyers.
  • Owners are trading down, extending dosage intervals and deferring elective surgery. 37% use credit to fund procedures. Insurance penetration is about 15% in Europe and higher in the UK.
  • Specialty Pharma's retail campaigns make quarterly demand lumpy and depend on one retailer relationship.
  • A two-sided effect: clinic margin pressure raises the value of a buying group to independents (VetFamily benefits) while raising price pressure on suppliers (Specialty Pharma and MedTech face it).
  • For the model: demand supports 8% to 10% organic growth in Specialty Pharma (underlying) and Veterinary Services. It supports 6% to 9% in MedTech after lapping the recovery. The downside sensitivity sits in orthopaedics and in campaign-driven nutrition.
C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

C+6069th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: regulators and political actors are on balance helping. Animal health was excluded from US Section 232 drug tariffs. The UK CMA's remedies avoid suppliers. Stewardship rules push demand to prevention and diagnostics. State pharmacy licensing slows online sellers. The fragility is moderate: device tariffs are undecided, and UK and US clinic-level regulation compresses Vimian's customers' margins.

  • Helping:
  • The Section 232 exemption for animal health (2026).
  • CMA final decision (24 March 2026) and binding order (22 September 2026): transparency, a £21 prescription fee cap, no divestments, outside Vimian's scope.
  • EU Farm to Fork's 50% antimicrobial reduction target by 2030.
  • California and multi-state pharmacist-in-charge licensing for out-of-state pharmacies (full enforcement in 2026).
  • FDA CVM and EMA approval barriers protecting registered products.
  • Hurting:
  • Possible US tariffs on medical device imports, affecting about half of US sales at a low-single-digit-point EBITA hit before mitigation.
  • UK transparency and prescription disclosure pushing medicines online and squeezing clinic margins.
  • EU Veterinary Medicinal Products Regulation and CSRD compliance costs.
  • The separate FDA and USDA pathways that make biologics development (Nextmune Bio) slower and costlier.
  • Tariff exemptions are dated to end on 20 January 2029 (sector digest).
  • Fragility: low for the drug side through 2028. Moderate for the device side, where a single political decision could add cost.
C-43

How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

C6663rd percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: the realistic addressable market is large relative to Vimian and is not the constraint on growth for the next five years. The constraint is the customer's budget and Vimian's execution. The niches' under-penetration plus market growth of about 5% to 7% in value supports organic growth of about 7% to 9% a year over the mid term, plus 2 to 4 points from acquisitions. That is total growth of about 10% to 12%. Beyond the next two to three years, faster growth would need a demand turn or a step-up in M&A.

  • Market sizes in the research:
  • Global animal health: about USD 50bn in 2024, rising to USD 65bn to 75bn by 2030 (about 5% to 7% a year).
  • Veterinary diagnostics: USD 8.73bn in 2025 to USD 15.05bn by 2031 (9.57% a year).
  • The European pet care market is forecast to grow near 4% (Jefferies).
  • Global animal diagnostics: USD 8bn to USD 17bn by 2032 (expert).
  • Penetration headroom in Vimian's niches:
  • 80% of adult pets have periodontal disease.
  • Only 20% of diagnostic opportunities are captured.
  • Only one in a hundred dogs needing hip surgery is treated.
  • Allergy immunotherapy competes against lifelong symptomatic therapy across a large chronic population.
  • Veterinary buying groups are absent in many markets (Japan and Portugal are new entries).
  • Vimian's share: low single digits of its addressable niches, on my inference. The research does not size the niche market combined. Revenue of about EUR 0.5bn against niche markets that are plausibly several billion euros.
  • Growth that this allows:
  • Market growth of 4% to 7% plus penetration and share gains of 2 to 3 points gives about 7% to 9% organic.
  • The company's own organic record since 2021 (16.5%, 3.7%, around 9% in 2023, 9%, 6%, 9-12% in 2026) averages about 8% to 9%. That supports the range.
  • What limits it: owner budgets (elective categories), clinic consolidation (buying group), and the need to fund penetration with commercial spending (field sales, training), which caps margin expansion.
1c

Overall external picture

2 answers
C-0662C-1042
C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

C6263rd percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: Vimian's own revenue cycle has clearly turned up. Organic growth went from 5% (Q2 and Q3 2025) to 9%, 12% and 11% in Q1 to Q3 2026, led by a MedTech swing from -4% to +15%. But a large part of the 2026 acceleration is base effect and campaign phasing. On a two-year view the underlying rate is about 8% a year, the company's normal rate, not a new higher one. Expect deceleration in 2027 as MedTech laps its recovery.

  • The turn is real and broad. In Q3 2026 three of four segments grew organically at double digits:
  • MedTech 15%
  • Veterinary Services 10%
  • Diagnostics 13%
  • Specialty Pharma 7% reported, 11% excluding the prior-year US nutrition campaign

Q3 group organic growth of 11% beat the 8% consensus. That makes three consecutive beats.

  • The base effect is large, by my two-year arithmetic:
  • Group organic growth was 5% in Q2 2025 and 12% in Q2 2026, about 8.2% a year over two years. Q3 was 5% then 11%, about 7.9% a year.
  • MedTech was -4% in Q2 2025 and +11% in Q2 2026, only about 3.4% a year over two years. FY2025 MedTech organic was -1%.
  • MedTech's 15% in Q3 2026 is therefore mostly recovery from a depressed base plus dental growth and the field-force reorganisation. It is not a market that is growing 15%.
  • The volatility drivers to model:
  • Specialty Pharma campaigns: a US nutrition campaign with a leading retailer flattered Q2 2026 (14% reported against 9% underlying) and depressed the Q3 comparison (7% against 11%).
  • Diagnostics: swings with livestock PCR extraction demand during disease outbreaks (Q3's 13% was livestock-led).
  • MedTech: the annual order programme was ended to align distributor shipments with demand, which removes one source of shipment volatility.
  • The cycle position for 2027:
  • MedTech laps +11% (Q2) and +15% (Q3), so its growth should fall towards 6% to 9%.
  • Specialty Pharma's underlying rate has held near 9% to 11% for three quarters.
  • Veterinary Services has held 10% to 11% for four quarters with Japan, Portugal and Ireland entries adding.
  • Group organic growth of about 8% to 9% in 2027 is my base. 11% to 12% repeating would need US orthopaedic volumes to recover, which management says has not happened.
  • Direction: turned up, peaking in the second half of 2026, normalising in 2027.
C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

F4223rd percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the external picture is a mild net headwind of about one to two points of organic growth against a neutral environment. Vimian's niche positioning and self-help have more than overcome it for four quarters. The headwinds are at the end customer and in the broad market. The tailwinds are slow, structural and in Vimian's niches. Nothing external is driving the case. The case depends on Vimian continuing to outrun its environment, so a model should not assume external help before 2028.

Thematic drivers one by one (direction for Vimian, with the level at which each acts):

  1. Pet humanisation and premiumisation (value chain / industry): stable in value of care, fading in visit volume. A mild headwind to elective lines, neutral for chronic care.
  2. Affordability and rates (value chain, end customer): headwind, strongest on US elective orthopaedics. No dated relief in the data.
  3. Clinic consolidation (value chain): headwind to pricing and to VetFamily's mature-market pool. A mild tailwind to direct-to-clinic suppliers with training relationships.
  4. Online and omnichannel prescription migration (value chain): slow headwind to the clinic channel and VetFamily members. Partly offset by long-acting injectables and state licensing barriers.
  5. Industry price war at blockbuster level (industry): neutral in the niches. A latent headwind to immunotherapy's relative economics if symptomatic drugs get cheaper.
  6. AI and digitalisation (industry): neutral to a mild tailwind for Vimian (dental imaging AI, OvaCyte, Pet Allergy Explorer). A headwind only in diagnostics against IDEXX's ecosystem.
  7. Antimicrobial stewardship and prevention (sector / regulation): mild tailwind.
  8. Tariffs and supply chain (sector): headwind on MedTech cost of goods, a tail risk on Taiwan. A tailwind for Specialty Pharma after the Section 232 exemption.
  9. Currency (company): persistent headwind to reported growth and to the SEK share price from a weaker dollar and stronger krona. Unhedged.
  10. Sector rotation (stock market): healthcare bought as a defensive is a tailwind, but small leveraged roll-ups are de-rated first. Net neutral to negative for this stock.

Headwinds by level:

  • Value chain (end customer and clinic): moderate and persistent.
  • Industry: mild for Vimian (strong for Zoetis).
  • Sector: mild, mixed.
  • Company-specific external items: currency and rates are moderate. DePuy Synthes and VOI are small tailwinds.

Overall: a mild net headwind with a negative tail (a further consumer leg down, a device tariff, a sharp dollar fall). Mid-term structural tailwinds (cohort ageing, dental and diagnostic penetration) are real but undated.

Inputs

2

Internal change forces/risks

C6459th percentile

Memo topic 2, Internal change forces: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Internal change forces

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

Vimian is a genuine 'Major Corporate Change' case at the level of people, commercial model and execution. The board removed an under-delivering chief executive. It promoted the operator who built the best segment. It rebuilt the leadership layer within a year. MedTech was reorganised and is growing 15% organically. Three consecutive quarters beat consensus.

The change is a sharpening of the same strategy, not a new one. It has not reached the three things that turn growth into shareholder return:

  • the group margin
  • cash conversion
  • capital allocation, meaning returns, incentives and the 2030 target

The negative developments cluster exactly there: working capital that did not normalise, an unexplained related-party licensing stream in the most profitable segment, incentives without a return test, perimeter creep, and a mid-term target that cannot be met on stated funding.

For the Consumer's model:

  • Treat the internal change as reliable for organic growth of about 8% to 9% in 2027 and for continued bolt-ons.
  • Treat it as unproven for margin expansion and cash conversion. Model these from the segment mix and an explicit net working capital ratio, not from management ambition.

The Capital Markets Day on 24 November 2026 is the next decisive internal event. It will show whether the new team links its execution to a modellable margin, cash and capital plan, or re-states an ambition the numbers cannot carry.

Internal risks

Group judgement

Risks

C-4251st percentile

Layer 1 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The fundamental risk profile is moderate. The growth engine is accelerating, customers are diversified, and the sudden fundamental shocks are low probability. The share-price risk profile is high:

  • valuation at the 75th percentile of its own five-year range after a 40% to 45% run
  • a controlling holder over a thin float
  • a style the market de-rates first in risk-off phases
  • a dated perception event (the Capital Markets Day) two weeks away
  • a downside to a fair trough of about 27%, and about 40% in a company-specific problem

The two places where fundamental and perception risk meet are:

  1. Cash conversion and working capital: a second weak cash quarter would validate the roll-up bear thesis.
  2. The adjusted earnings base, including the unexplained related-party licensing income.

For the Consumer's model:

  • Build explicit downside scenarios around:
  • Specialty Pharma underlying growth below 6%
  • US orthopaedic decline
  • net working capital rising another 2 to 3 points of revenue
  • the below-EBITA relief not arriving (EPS about 10% lower by FY2028)
  • Pair each with the multiple bands above. The earnings risk alone is modest. Its combination with the multiple is not.
C-14

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

C6265th percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: operating standards have visibly risen in the weakest unit and in group processes. The leading edge is MedTech (field sales, SKU cull, ending the annual order programme), Diagnostics' footprint, and leadership programmes. The improvement shows in MedTech's growth and margin. It does not yet show in group margin, cash conversion or governance depth.

  • Execution standards:
  • The US orthopaedic model moved from inside sales to a field sales force in January 2026.
  • More than 4,000 of 22,000 MedTech SKUs were discontinued in Q4 2025.
  • The annual order programme was ended so shipments match demand.
  • Diagnostics production went from four sites to two.
  • Movora trained 1,300 veterinarians in Q3 2026 alone, and the group trained 65,000 professionals in 2025.
  • Output: MedTech organic growth -4% (Q2 2025) to +15% (Q3 2026). MedTech adjusted EBITA margin 23.7% to 24.4% (Q3 2026) and 25.8% (Q2 2026).
  • Operational measures and culture:
  • KPIs standardised across segments.
  • More than 80 senior managers through new leadership programmes, which marks a shift from a hands-off holding model to active cross-segment management.
  • The CFO drove operating cash conversion above 70% in Q1 2026. Q2 was 13% on a receivables build. Q3 was 66.7% of adjusted EBITDA (71% on the company's measure).
  • Cost discipline: mixed.
  • Q3 group margin slipped to 24.3% from 24.5%, attributed to "central cost increases" and growth investments.
  • Q3 revenue grew 15% and adjusted EBITA 14%: no operating leverage.
  • Diagnostics investment cut its margin to 7.2% (13.8% in Q1).
  • Standards improved operations, not overheads.
  • Governance standards:
  • No internal audit function; the board relies on self-assessment across about 80 countries.
  • 12 whistleblower complaints in 2025 (one substantiated discrimination case).
  • MSCI AAA (May 2026).
  • Undisclosed LTIP hurdles.
  • No improvement here; it caps the attribute.
  • Net: a real improvement in execution where it was most needed. Unproven in cost discipline and cash, and flat in governance.
C-86

What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

F3325th percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: there are several negative corporate developments, present and plausible. The four that matter most for the model:

  1. Cash conversion and net working capital have drifted up structurally, and the Q2 build did not reverse in Q3.
  2. A material, unexplained related-party licensing stream sits in the largest segment.
  3. Incentives reward volume over returns, while the 2030 target is unreachable on stated funding.
  4. The earnings-quality gap between adjusted and reported profit.
  • Net working capital.
  • The company stated the Q2 receivables build would "normalise during the third quarter".
  • It did not reverse. The Q3 net working capital cash flow was only +EUR 0.3m. FactSet's net working capital was EUR 137m (Q2) and EUR 138m (Q3): 30.4% then 29.5% of trailing revenue, against 27.6% at Q4 2025.
  • Q3 conversion "improved" to 66.7% only because there was no further build.
  • The year-to-date operating cash flow of EUR 49.4m is about 49% of adjusted EBITDA (EUR 100.8m) and about 56% of adjusted EBITA (my arithmetic).
  • The Q3 test was "conversion above 60%". It passed narrowly, on the flow, not the stock.
  • A plausible structural cause is the strategy itself. Direct sales to thousands of clinics, in place of a few distributors, raise receivables and inventory per euro of revenue. The FY2025 days-sales-outstanding rose from 49 to 51 days.
  • Related-party licensing income.
  • Specialty Pharma invoiced licensing fees to an entity fully owned by a Specialty Pharma manager (Nick Bova): EUR 9.4m in 2024, EUR 9.7m in 2025, and EUR 4.9m in an interim 2026 period.
  • If this income carries little cost, as licensing income normally does, it could be a mid-teens share of the segment's roughly EUR 55m of adjusted EBITA (my arithmetic; the cost against it is not disclosed).
  • The research does not explain what is licensed, why to a manager's entity, or whether it is at arm's length.
  • It coincides with the departure of the segment head of ten years.
  • This is the single most important unexplained item in the earnings base.
  • Incentives:
  • LTIP 2026/2029 vests 70% on FY2028 adjusted EBITA and 30% on revenue.
  • No ROIC metric, no clawback, undisclosed hurdles. Adjusted EBITA excludes acquisition costs, so it rewards buying EBITA.
  • The outgoing CEO received an EUR 85k bonus despite a 0% payout on the EBITA target, plus EUR 446k of "other remuneration".
  • Earnings quality. Adjusted EBITA was EUR 105.3m against reported EBIT of EUR 66.8m in 2025. The gap:
  • EUR 23.8m amortisation of acquired intangibles
  • EUR 14.7m items affecting comparability
  • EUR 6.0m capitalised R&D and software (Jefferies: about 250 basis points of adjusted margin)
  • FY2025 operating cash flow including USD 28.7m of litigation receipts (27% of the total)
  • Other negatives:
  • Central costs rising (Q3).
  • Diagnostics margin dilution while investing.
  • Perimeter creep (cremation).
  • Staff turnover of 19% in 2025 (12.6% in 2021).
  • No internal audit.
  • A controlling-holder overhang.
  • Possible selling by the former Specialty Pharma head (2.6m shares).
  • The plausible Capital Markets Day credibility event (C-13).
C-59

How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system (as at Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair)? Such that the goals, strategy, culture, execution, decisions are congruent, holistic and symbiotic?

F4424th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: the culture is decentralised and entrepreneurial, congruent with the acquisition model (founders join and stay; about 80% of deals bilateral) and with local commercial speed. It is not congruent with the financial goals shareholders need: cash conversion, return on capital, margin leverage. Incentives reward absolute EBITA and revenue, and the centre is too small to impose cash discipline. Structurally it resembles Lifco. It does not have Lifco's return-on-capital and cash culture. The system is aligned for growth, not for returns.

  • Congruent elements:
  • "A natural home for ambitious entrepreneurs" (chief executive). Founder retention (I-Vet).
  • Autonomy scores 83/100 and inclusion 78/100.
  • Cross-segment leadership programmes for 80 managers have been added to reduce silos.
  • KPIs standardised.
  • The goals (growth, niche leadership), the strategy (bolt-ons, cross-selling), and the execution (local selling, training) fit together.
  • Incongruent elements:
  • Clarity scores 59/100 and work-life balance 61/100.
  • Staff turnover is 19% (12.6% in 2021). Voluntary departures are 13%, concentrated in production units.
  • The employee net promoter score is disputed (+30 against a target of 50, or -36).
  • Incentives: 70% adjusted EBITA plus 30% revenue, no ROIC, no clawback. The STI includes 20% to 30% employee NPS and sustainability. The outgoing chief executive was paid a bonus despite missing the EBITA target.
  • The ending of the annual order programme was presented as working-capital smoothing, but a former controller says it removed visibility just as the market slowed.
  • The Lifco comparison: Lifco's decentralisation is paired with an explicit EBITA-to-capital employed discipline that drives acquisitions and subsidiaries. Vimian's has no such return metric anywhere in the incentive system.
  • Read: a congruent growth culture with a missing return-and-cash spine. The CFO's release to "capital allocation and cash conversion" is the first sign of one.
C-81

What fundamental risks are there that are sudden, high-magnitude and low-probability, and how plausible and how large is each?

B5879th percentile

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: the sudden, high-magnitude, low-probability fundamental risks are few and mostly concentrated in MedTech supply, currency and governance. None is likely in the next year. The most plausible large one is a disclosure or governance event around the related-party licensing income or acquisition accounting, because the earnings base depends on adjusted measures.

Ranked by plausibility multiplied by size (my judgement):

  1. Governance or accounting revelation. - The licensing income from a Specialty Pharma manager's entity (EUR 9.7m in 2025), adjustments of EUR 14.7m, capitalised development and earn-out accounting. - Plausibility: low to moderate. - Size: potentially large for the multiple. It is the profile short-sellers target in roll-ups (the Risks report notes Vimian fits screening criteria, with no short campaign yet).
  2. Currency shock. - A 20% USD fall with SEK strength would cut revenue by over EUR 31m, compress the operating margin by more than 150 basis points and push leverage past 3.0x (Risks report). Vimian is unhedged. - Plausibility: low for 20%. Moderate for 10% (EUR 15.7m revenue, EUR 2.3m EBIT).
  3. US device tariffs or Taiwan supply severance. - About half of US sales are exposed to tariffs (a low single-digit point EBITA hit). About 20% of cost of goods is Taiwan-linked. - Plausibility: low to moderate for tariffs, low for severance. - Size: moderate for tariffs, high for supply loss.
  4. Acquisition failure. - A VOI-type liability (USD 70m in 2023) from a newly acquired company, at a stepped-up deal pace with a new Head of M&A. - Plausibility: low. Size: high.
  5. Loss of the US retail nutrition partner, whose campaigns swing Specialty Pharma's quarters. - Plausibility: low. Size: moderate. The memo noted the forward EPS story would "collapse on high leverage" if retail contracts ended, which overstates it.
  6. A competitor or substitute shock to dermatology or immunotherapy pricing from the Zoetis/Elanco/Merck war. - Plausibility: moderate over 18 months. Size: moderate.
  7. The VOI appeal loss. Small, with USD 6.2m already doubtful.
  8. Product recall or regulatory action. None from 2023 to 2025. - Plausibility: low. Size: moderate.
D-03

In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

D4237th percentile

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the drivers of the level of the forecasts are diffuse, and the drivers of the difference from the past are concentrated. Revenue and EBITA growth rest on four segments, thousands of customers and many small products, which is good for predictability. But what makes the next four years differ from the last four to six is not growth. It is conversion: a margin that stops falling and far less profit lost below EBITA. That difference rests on a small cluster of drivers, most of which have a common root in the accounting and funding of acquisitions, and one of which, the dental business, pulls EBITA and EPS in opposite directions.

What actually differs between the forecast years and 2021 to 2025. Organic growth does not differ (about 8% then and now). Total growth is lower (11% to 12% a year against 13% to 18%, and 62% in 2022). The differences are these:

Difference from 2021 to 2025 Size in the base (FY2026 to FY2028) Drivers behind it Concentrated or diffuse
The adjusted EBITA margin rises instead of falling +62 basis points (24.5% to 25.1%), worth about EUR 3m of the EUR 33m rise in EBITA Diagnostics margin (+29 basis points), MedTech (+27), central costs growing more slowly than revenue (+20), less Veterinary Services normalising and mix Concentrated in two segments and one cost line, but small in euros
Less is lost between EBITA and net income Reported net income rises from about 31% of adjusted EBITA to about 42% (after my change to financial items) Non-cash earn-out and currency charges falling from about EUR 10m to EUR 6.5m; the reported tax rate falling from about 34% to about 30%; IAC falling from about EUR 11m to EUR 8.5m Concentrated: three lines with one root, the acquisition model
No litigation and no equity issue 2023 carried the USD 70m settlement; 2024 the SEK 1.6bn rights issue; 2025 USD 28.7m of receipts The absence of a second liability of the VOI kind; leverage staying near 2.0x A single condition
Free cash flow becomes positive and steady EUR 38.5m rising to EUR 65m EBITDA growth, less working capital held at 28.5% of revenue Concentrated in one balance: MedTech inventory

The growth itself is diffuse, which is the reassuring half of the answer:

  • About 60% of the growth in EBITA from FY2026 to FY2028 is organic volume across four segments (Specialty Pharma about 42% of revenue, MedTech 36%, Veterinary Services 16%, Diagnostics 6%), and about 30% is bolt-ons.
  • No customer exceeds 10% of revenue. There are more than 17,000 clinics and 12,400 buying-group members.
  • In 2024 and 2025, when US orthopaedics shrank, Specialty Pharma and Veterinary Services carried the group. In 2026 MedTech led. The segments have covered for each other.

Four concentrations sit inside that diffuse picture. They are the ones the Consumer should name in the memo.

1. Dental: one driver, five lines, two directions. Dental is about 30% of MedTech revenue (DNB Carnegie, March 2025), or roughly a tenth of the group. Its influence on the forecasts is far larger than its size:

  • MedTech growth and margin (positive). Dental is growing at double digits and carries a margin above the group's. It is the reason MedTech's margin rose while orthopaedics was weak.
  • Inventory (negative for cash). The organic inventory build of about EUR 17m in 2026 is attributed to dental stocking (D-01).
  • Earn-out charges (negative for reported and model-basis EPS). The probability adjustments of 2025 and 2026 reflect dental businesses beating their targets. iM3 alone has an optional earn-out tranche of up to EUR 50m.
  • The tax rate (negative). Those charges are not deductible; the tax rate was 35% in Q1 and 31% in Q3 2026.
  • Net effect. In the 18-month window, a better dental outcome raises EBITA and, at the same time, lowers free cash flow and reported EPS. Over three to four years it is clearly positive, because the earn-outs end and the stock turns. This is why a quarterly "beat" on EBITA and a disappointing EPS and cash print can come from the same good news. It is also why EBITA, not EPS, is the line on which to judge the operating case.

2. One counterparty in the most profitable segment. Licensing income from the Bova entity (about EUR 10m a year) may be up to a sixth of Specialty Pharma's adjusted EBITA (EUR 53.9m in 2025) if it carries little cost. Specialty Pharma is itself about 47% of segment EBITA. Losing the income would cost about 8% of EPS. A second single relationship, a US retailer's nutrition campaigns, moves the segment's quarterly growth by four to five points but not its annual level.

3. US orthopaedics as the only cyclical swing. It is roughly an eighth of group revenue (my estimate: MedTech is 36% of revenue, about 70% of it orthopaedic, about half of that North American). It is the one line tied to the clinic-visit cycle. The base assumes no recovery, so the concentration is on the upside (EUR 2m to 3m of EBITA a year if volumes return).

4. The below-EBITA cluster. Between FY2026 and FY2027, model-basis EPS grows about 25% (0.113 to 0.141 after my changes) while adjusted EBITA grows about 13%. The twelve points between them come from only three lines: non-cash financial charges, the tax rate and the fading of 2026's unusually heavy items. These are not independent. All three are functions of how well acquired businesses perform against their earn-outs and of how many new deals are signed.

What this means for predictability.

  • The diffuse part (volume) is where our forecast puts most of its weight. That is a reason to trust the EBITA line.
  • Consensus puts more weight on the concentrated part. About 22% of consensus EBITDA growth to FY2028 is margin, against about 10% of ours, and consensus assumes the below-EBITA cluster normalises almost completely.
  • The four concentrations are all visible in quarterly disclosure (D-05), so they are trackable even where they are not predictable.

Where I differ from the second set. The second set (P-09) said its forecast was "concentrated in two drivers: organic volume and the bolt-on pace". That is right for the level of EBITA. It understates how far the EPS growth rate depends on the below-EBITA cluster, which the second set treated line by line rather than as one driver with one cause.

C-11

To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders (e.g. finance director, operations director, board, division heads, etc.) or other key actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) changed, and what does the change signal?

B+7388th percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: almost the entire leadership layer has changed in sixteen months. The controlling shareholder's board forced the change, and the appointments signal a shift to commercial execution and urgency, not to a new strategy. The change is a net positive and is already visible in outputs. Its cost is a team whose group-level record is four quarters long, and a new Head of Specialty Pharma from outside animal health in the segment that carries the governance question.

  • What changed, and when:
  • Chief executive. Patrik Eriksson was removed by the board on 17 July 2025 after eighteen months; the CFO served as interim. Alireza Tajbakhsh, who built VetFamily to over 10,000 members with double-digit growth and margin expansion, became permanent chief executive on 24 November 2025. He also ran MedTech on an interim basis from July 2025 to April 2026.
  • MedTech. Guy Spörri left at the end of 2025. Lotta Lundaas, an external hire from the Kinnevik sphere with US and European scaling experience, started on 13 April 2026.
  • Specialty Pharma, the largest segment (EUR 182.4m, about 43% of revenue, the highest margin). Magnus Kjellberg stepped down in early 2026 after about ten years; he holds 2.6m shares. The CFO covered as interim. Hans Lord Skarplöth started on 1 October 2026, after thirteen years as chief executive of ATG, described as international expansion and brand transformation. He is not an animal-health operator.
  • Veterinary Services. Michael Thunell, the segment's COO since 2018, was promoted in November 2025.
  • M&A. Martin Bengtson (46 transactions, about EUR 170m of acquired revenue) was replaced on 5 October 2026 by Andrea Tidholm, an internal hire with twenty years of M&A experience. She reports to the CFO.
  • Board. Cut to five members, with Magnus Welander chair since 2024 and three of five independent. Fidelio's Theodor Bonnier chairs the M&A committee.
  • What the change signals: 1. The controlling shareholder acts. Fidelio (58.4% of capital) removed an under-delivering chief executive within eighteen months. That is governance by a strong owner, not by a passive one. 2. The board mandate was "greater urgency", not a strategy change (the chair, September 2025). That matches what happened: the same strategy, executed harder (C-12). 3. The new hires are commercial builders, not scientists. The bet is on sales execution, channel conversion and M&A pace.
  • What it does not yet signal:
  • Capital discipline. No ROIC metric was added to incentives, and the controlling shareholder still chairs the M&A committee.
  • Bench depth. The CFO carried two jobs for most of 2026. The ending of that dual role from October 2026 releases him to focus on cash conversion, which is the case's weakest output.
  • Evidence it is working:
  • MedTech organic growth went from -4% to +6%, +11% and +15% across the four quarters after the change.
  • The group made three consecutive organic beats.
  • The February 2026 employee survey showed improved scores.
  • Risks:
  • A whole new team learning at once.
  • The largest segment under an outsider while the related-party licensing arrangement with a segment manager (C-86) remains unexplained.
  • Kjellberg's 2.6m shares as a possible seller in a thin float.
C-12

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

D5732nd percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the strategy has been sharpened, not changed. It is selective bilateral bolt-ons, dental as the MedTech anchor, direct sales replacing distributors, Diagnostics pivoting to companion animals, cross-selling across segments, and geographic expansion of the buying group. It is coherent, adequately resourced for its bolt-on pace, and well articulated at the segment level. It will be articulated at group level for the first time at the Capital Markets Day on 24 November 2026. The weakness is unchanged: it is a growth strategy without a capital-return discipline, and its margin path relies on mix, not operating leverage.

  • The strategic choices, each with evidence of action:
  • Bolt-ons, mostly bilateral. About 80% of deals are bilateral, at a historical average of 7.5x EV/EBITDA (Jefferies). The CFO calls it "about 9x" for Norfolk. 235 targets have been screened. The chief executive says the "M&A engine is entering a more active phase" (17 September 2026).
  • Dental platform: iM3 (2024), Dental Focus, AllAccem (SANOS sealant), Clarity XR AI imaging. It lifted MedTech's margin.
  • Direct sales. Movora is 95% direct. Specialty Pharma has terminated distributors in France, Belgium and the Netherlands. iM3 is converting distributors to direct.
  • Diagnostics pivot to companion testing: I-Vet (EUR 5.6m revenue, Italy), OvaCyte AI, site consolidation from four to two.
  • Entry into soft-tissue surgery: Norfolk Medical Products (USD 6.9m trailing revenue, about 9x EBITDA, closed about 2 November 2026, funded from own resources).
  • VetFamily expansion: Ireland, Japan, Portugal. Membership is 12,400.
  • Cross-selling: about 18% of group sales and about a third of Specialty Pharma's organic growth.
  • Resourcing:
  • Leverage is 2.0x pro-forma adjusted EBITDA (Q3 2026) against a 3.0x ceiling. That is about EUR 70m of headroom to 2.5x and about EUR 135m to 3.0x on about EUR 135m of pro-forma EBITDA (my arithmetic).
  • Consensus free cash flow is EUR 58m in FY2027 and EUR 78m in FY2028.
  • Fidelio backs M&A funding lines.
  • Enough for EUR 60m to 120m a year of bolt-ons. Not enough for the 2030 target (C-13).
  • Articulation. The segment-level strategy is clear. The group-level transmission (the margin bridge, the capital allocation framework, what "over half organic" means) has never been laid out. The 24 November Capital Markets Day is the first opportunity.
  • What has not changed and should have:
  • The incentive plan (70% absolute adjusted EBITA, 30% revenue, no return test, no clawback) still rewards acquired volume.
  • The decentralised model limits cost synergies by design. The former group controller says it "limits large-scale cost takeouts". Margin gains must come from mix and commercial efficiency.
  • Perimeter creep continues (C-15).
  • For the model: expect EUR 60m to 120m a year of acquisitions at 8x to 10x EBITDA. They add 2 to 4 points of revenue growth at margins at or slightly below the group's.
C-13

To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised? (choices)

F4222nd percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: no guidance has been raised. The company gives no annual guidance. Its single mid-term ambition, adjusted EBITA above EUR 300m by 2030 (set in March 2024 when the EUR 200m by 2025 target was abandoned), was reaffirmed at Q3. It sits far above what the sell-side models. On my arithmetic it cannot be reached on the stated funding terms. The Capital Markets Day on 24 November 2026 is therefore more likely to re-frame or re-date the ambition than to raise it, and its credibility cost is the main risk on this item.

  • The gap to consensus:
  • The target implies adjusted EBITA growth of about 26% a year from a likely FY2026 level of about EUR 118m to 120m (my estimate: nine months EUR 88.1m plus a Q4 near EUR 30m to 31m). It implies about 23% a year from 2025's EUR 105.3m.
  • Historical delivery was about 10% a year: about EUR 87m in 2023 to EUR 105.3m in 2025. DNB Carnegie cited 10%.
  • Consensus FY2029 EBITDA is EUR 181m on one broker, which implies adjusted EBITA in the EUR 160m to 170m range. The target is about 75% above the consensus trajectory.
  • Why it does not add up on the company's own terms ("over half organic", "no further capital injections"). My arithmetic:
  • Organic revenue growth of about 8% a year plus 40 basis points of margin a year gives EBITA of about EUR 170m to 175m in 2030.
  • The remaining EUR 125m to 130m of EBITA would need roughly EUR 1.1bn to 1.3bn of acquisitions at 9x to 10x EBITA, about EUR 300m a year.
  • The 2024 to 2026 run-rate is about EUR 80m to 95m a year of acquisition cash.
  • Self-funded capacity (consensus free cash flow plus leverage to 3.0x) is about EUR 700m to 800m over four years. That reaches roughly EUR 240m to 250m of EBITA at maximum leverage.
  • Reaching EUR 300m needs equity, organic EBITA growth near 15% a year (never delivered), or a large platform deal. The memo flagged the platform deal as an alarm.
  • History of the guidance behaviour. Three mid-term ambitions were set and dropped: a 35% adjusted EBITA margin, a 30% sales CAGR, and EUR 200m adjusted EBITA by 2025 (2025 actual: EUR 105.3m). Management is characterised as an "over-promiser archetype historically".
  • The Capital Markets Day outcomes, in my order of likelihood: 1. The 2030 ambition is kept but surrounded by segment targets and an M&A envelope that make it look reachable, with an implicit reliance on acquisitions. 2. The ambition is re-dated or re-based to something the sell-side can model. 3. The ambition is kept with a large-deal or equity-funding signal. This is negative for minorities given two prior raises at falling prices.

Outcome 2 would be the healthiest and probably a short-term negative for the shares. Outcome 3 is the main downside risk.

  • For the model: ignore the 2030 target as a forecast input. Use the Capital Markets Day building blocks (organic growth, segment margins, M&A envelope) as testable inputs if they are given.

C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

C-5058th percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: price rises are real in nominal terms and applied, but they do not exceed cost inflation. Gross margin has been flat at 68.9% for two years while input costs rose about as fast as revenue. Real pricing power is latent and plausible in the chronic and consumable niches but not demonstrated. Management's references to pricing have not increased. The price element of organic growth is undisclosed, which is itself a gap for the model.

  • Nominal:
  • Average price increases of about 5% were implemented to offset cost inflation, and wage inflation was "fully passed" to veterinary customers (Business Foundations).
  • Raw material costs rose 13% to 13.5% in 2025 and H1 2026 against revenue growth of about 12.5% to 13.4%. Gross margin was 68.9% in 2024 and 2025, against 69.2% in 2023.
  • That is nominal pass-through, not real gain.
  • Context: the clinics' own pricing outruns Vimian's. Veterinary service costs are rising 10% to 12% a year. The clinic is taking the real price, not the supplier. In a squeeze, the clinic's own price rises are what drive owners to defer (C-01). Pricing power at clinic level is being tested ("clinics are out-pricing consumer demand", Morgan Stanley).
  • Where real pricing power is plausible:
  • Customised allergen immunotherapy with proprietary PAX diagnostics (high switching costs, lifelong patient lock-in).
  • Dental consumables (SANOS reorders).
  • Integrated orthopaedic systems (instrument lock-in).
  • Segment evidence: Specialty Pharma's margin rose to 30.3% from 29.5% in Q3, and MedTech's to 24.4% from 23.7%, on dental gross margins. These are mix and pricing combined and cannot be separated from the disclosure.
  • Where it is under pressure:
  • Corporate clinic groups centralise purchasing and run RFPs.
  • The Risks report's failure mode 2 is consolidator pressure on dental consumables pricing.
  • Industry promotions in dermatology.
  • For the model:
  • Assume price of about 3% to 5% a year within organic growth, matched by cost inflation. Gross margin flat.
  • Treat any gross margin expansion as mix (dental, direct sales, Specialty Pharma), not price.
C-18

To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

C+5873rd percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: M&A is stepping up. It is strategically sensible (niche bolt-ons that feed the cross-selling network), disciplined on entry price relative to listed peers, and integrated through incentives rather than operating integration. The weakness is that the returns on the acquired capital are modest (group ROIC 9.4% on EUR 590m of goodwill), entry multiples appear to be rising from about 7.5x towards about 9x, and the integration model gives up cost synergies by design.

  • The step-up:
  • Acquisition cash outflow (FactSet) was EUR 80.9m in 2024, EUR 93.3m in 2025 and EUR 63.2m in the first nine months of 2026.
  • 2025 had five deals (three in Q4, around the chief executive's appointment). 2026 so far: Favna, Vetsave, I-Vet, Verona Pet, Norfolk.
  • The chief executive says the engine is "entering a more active phase". There is a new Head of M&A and 235 screened targets. The Q2 review's own test (two to three quality deals by year-end) is met with Norfolk and the 2026 bolt-ons.
  • Strategic sense: high. Each deal adds a product to an existing channel (Norfolk into Movora), a geography to a playbook (Vetsave), or a capability to a pivot (I-Vet). Dental is the proof case: it offset orthopaedic decline and lifted MedTech margins.
  • Discipline:
  • Historical average 7.5x EV/EBITDA pre-synergy against core peer multiples near 16x (Jefferies). About 80% bilateral, which avoids auctions.
  • The CFO's own reference point for Norfolk is "in line with the group's historical average of about 9x EBITDA". Either the definition differs or recent multiples are higher. Model 8x to 10x, not 7.5x.
  • Earn-outs and stay-on bonuses (EUR 2.9m to 3.9m adjusted out in Specialty Pharma) are part of the real price. They sit in items affecting comparability and investing cash flow, not in the headline multiple.
  • Integration:
  • Decentralised, through aligned incentives (founders retained, as at I-Vet).
  • Revenue synergies come through cross-selling. Cost synergies are limited.
  • There is no record of failed integrations after VOI. VOI itself was a due-diligence failure that cost USD 70m, recovered in full through litigation over three years.
  • Returns: my arithmetic.
  • At 9x EBITDA, the pre-tax EBITDA yield is about 11% and the post-tax EBITA yield about 8%, roughly the cost of capital before synergies.
  • Group ROIC is 9.4% (2025). Jefferies has 10.8% for 2026e. DNB Carnegie's after-tax adjusted ROCE is 5.4% in 2025, rising to 10.2% by 2028e.
  • Value creation therefore depends on cross-selling synergies and organic growth in acquired units. The company does not disclose either.
  • For the model: each EUR 100m of acquisitions at 9x adds about EUR 11m of EBITDA, about EUR 30m to 35m of revenue at about 30% EBITDA margins, and about 0.4x of leverage before cash generation.

At about 25x 24-month-forward earnings, a buyback would not be value-accretive, so the absence is rational today. It is still a structural F for a return-focused framework.
  • Watch: any Capital Markets Day signal that the 2030 target needs a "transformational" acquisition. That would be balance-sheet use above 2.5x or equity, both negative for minorities.

What staircase of novel positive developments lies ahead: which new, dated things can the market look forward to within the next 6 months, in months 6 to 18, and in months 18 to 36; how novel is each; and how well-founded?

Most of the other steps are well known to the market and only partly novel: the FY2026 cash print, the Diagnostics margin turn, MedTech holding double digits against tough comparisons, below-EBITA normalisation, the integration and cross-selling of Norfolk. The staircase is reasonably well founded for months 0 to 18 and thin and narrative-based for months 18 to 36. Because the price has already run 40% to 45%, the staircase now has to deliver positive surprises rather than merely confirm.

Next 6 months (to May 2027):

  1. Capital Markets Day, 24 November 2026. Novelty: high (first ever; segment targets, a margin bridge, an M&A envelope, the status of the 2030 target). Foundation: dated and certain. Direction uncertain; downside if the target is re-dated or equity-dependent, upside if a credible bridge appears.
