  3. Integration and early cross-selling of Norfolk (closed about 2 November 2026). Novelty: low to moderate.
  4. Commercial launch and uptake of PAX2 / Pet Allergy Explorer; VetFamily Japan and Portugal ramp. Novelty: low.
  5. Further bolt-ons under the new Head of M&A. Novelty: low (expected), but a large deal would be novel and contentious.
  6. The Delaware VOI appeal outcome (expected by September 2026, not reported in the files). Novelty: low; small cash.
  7. Q1 2027 results (late April 2027): the Diagnostics margin "back toward the mid-teens" checkpoint in the Q3 review. Novelty: moderate.

Months 6 to 18 (May 2027 to May 2028):

  1. Q2 2027 results (late July 2027): MedTech double-digit for four quarters against +11% and +15% comparisons. This is the durability test for the turnaround. Novelty: moderate. Probability of passing: below even, on my view (C-06).
  2. FY2027 margin expansion. The Q3 review's checkpoint is +100 basis points of adjusted EBITA margin in FY2027, reported in February 2028. Consensus expects about +90 basis points of EBITDA margin. Novelty: high if delivered, because it would break a three-year trend.
  3. Below-EBITA normalisation visible in FY2027 EPS (financial items, tax, items affecting comparability). Novelty: moderate.
  4. The LTIP 2025 FY2027 hurdle year; the dental platform's software and consumables scaling (Clarity XR, SANOS).
  5. A possible return to US orthopaedic market growth if the consumer squeeze eases, and the DePuy Synthes separation's share-gain window. Novelty: moderate. Foundation: low (exogenous).

Months 18 to 36 (May 2028 to November 2029):

  1. The FY2028 LTIP hurdle (70% adjusted EBITA) and the halfway point to 2030 against the EUR 300m ambition. Novelty: low. Foundation: weak, given the arithmetic.
  2. Nextmune Bio's first biologics candidates. Novelty: high. Foundation: low (no revenue attached).
  3. The ageing pandemic pet cohort lifting chronic-care volumes. Novelty: moderate. Foundation: plausible, undated.
  4. The research's non-consensus call: the dental platform taking 15% to 20% of the global companion dental market by 2028. Novelty: high. Foundation: unsized.
  5. The expiry of US animal-health tariff exemptions (20 January 2029), a step down, not up.

Overall: a well-dated near staircase, led by an event of uncertain sign, followed by a middle staircase of confirmations (cash, margin, MedTech durability) that the market is already watching for, and a thin far staircase.

3

Value chain dynamics and industry structure

D5134th percentile

The Investment Analyst's group judgement: Industry and Value Chain

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Industry and Value Chain

D5134th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The industry and value chain support Vimian's model structurally and work against it cyclically.

Structurally:

  • Vimian sits in the value chain's highest-margin stages: specialty IP and group purchasing.
  • Its niches are fragmented and under-penetrated, with Vimian number one or two in four of them.
  • Demand is cash-pay and chronic.
  • Regulation is benign to helpful.
  • The concentrated manufacturer oligopoly has historically priced with discipline and ignored Vimian.

Cyclically and at the edges:

  • The clinic customer is consolidating and squeezed.
  • The owner is deferring elective care.
  • Online and software disintermediation is forming against the clinic channel and the buying group.
  • The industry leader has started a price war in adjacent categories that can spill into dermatology.

The most important insight for the model is that Vimian's position rests on obscurity and specialisation, not on dominance. It gains share by acquisition and cross-selling, not by gravitational pull. Industry share flows to ecosystem players (IDEXX, Covetrus, Chewy, Mars) and corporate clinic networks, none of which is Vimian. The historical precedents (C-84) say the market rewards this kind of company only once growth converts into earnings and cash.

For 2027-2029, the structure supports:

  • gross margins around 69%
  • segment margins of 25% to 30%
  • organic growth of about 8% to 9%, above a market growing about 4% to 6% in value

It does not support margin expansion through price or through operating leverage. The downside is concentrated in dermatology pricing, US orthopaedic volumes and VetFamily's long-term relevance.

C-32How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?D5538th percentilefull answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

D6049th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the route to market is mainly helping. Vimian is moving to direct sales, taking the distributor's margin and controlling the clinic relationship, while traditional distributors are squeezed from both sides. The cost is higher selling costs and, I judge, structurally higher receivables and inventory. That cost is part of the cash-conversion problem. The fragility is the clinic's own channel leaking to online pharmacies and practice software.

  • Direct conversion:
  • Movora is 95% direct.
  • Specialty Pharma has gone direct in France, Belgium and the Netherlands.
  • iM3 is converting distributors to direct. DNB Carnegie raised EBITA estimates 3% to 4% for iM3's distributor-to-direct conversion.
  • heiland.com serves 1,700 German clinics monthly with over 40,000 products.
  • The research's value-chain map puts distribution at about a 4% EBIT stage. Capturing it is margin-accretive.
  • Intermediaries:
  • Covetrus-MWI's merger creates a stronger distributor for the sales that still go through distribution, with 40% of practice software.
  • Vetcove and online portals raise price transparency.
  • Wholesalers' margins are squeezed (expert calls).
  • For Vimian, the distributor layer matters less each year.
  • The hidden cost, my judgement from the evidence:
  • Selling directly to thousands of clinics (in place of a few distributors) increases receivables (days sales outstanding 49 to 51 days), inventory in the field (implant sets, a broad SKU range) and fixed selling costs.
  • The US field-force switch in January 2026 adds fixed cost ahead of volume.
  • This partly explains why net working capital has drifted from about 21% to 26% of revenue on the company's basis, and to about 29.5% to 30.4% of trailing revenue on FactSet's, while margins have not risen.
  • The Consumer should model a higher steady-state net working capital ratio than management's 21% aspiration.
  • Fragility:
  • Online pharmacies (Chewy about 12% of scripts) and software-led fulfilment erode the clinic channel Vimian sells through. VetFamily's members lose pharmacy margin.
  • Long-acting injectables and in-clinic diagnostics and consumables (iM3, Indical) are the categories that stay in the clinic.
C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

B6281st percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: existing competitors are behaving in ways that mostly help Vimian. Giants fight each other in blockbusters and ignore the niches. DePuy Synthes' separation distracts the main orthopaedic rival. Swedencare is weakened. The position is moderately fragile in two places: dermatology (spill-over from the Zoetis/Elanco/Merck price war) and diagnostics (IDEXX's ecosystem dominance).

  • Helping:
  • Zoetis, Elanco and Merck compete for parasiticides and dermatology share, not for implants or immunotherapy.
  • Johnson & Johnson's DePuy Synthes separation takes 18 to 24 months.
  • Swedencare grew only 2% organically in Q3 2026 (North America -4%) and its CEO is leaving. It cut multi-year EPS projections 9% to 11% (Jefferies).
  • Vimian's relative growth (12% organic in Q2 and 11% in Q3, against peers about 7%; Zoetis -1%) is a share-gain signal.
  • Hurting, or potentially:
  • Zoetis' gross-to-net cuts on Apoquel and Cytopoint, Elanco's Zenrelia and Merck's Numelvi could make symptomatic dermatology cheaper relative to immunotherapy.
  • IDEXX's installed base plus software (CoVet) and Mars' Antech dominate diagnostics, where Indical is investing at a 7.2% margin.
  • Low-cost orthopaedic copycats are a Risks-report early-warning item.
  • Fragility:
  • Low in orthopaedics and dental for 18 months.
  • Moderate in dermatology and immunotherapy.
  • High in diagnostics.

Weighted by profit, about 98% of segment EBITA sits outside the highly fragile Diagnostics segment. About 47% sits in the moderately fragile Specialty Pharma segment. Q3 2026 shares of segment EBITA: Specialty Pharma about 47%, MedTech about 33%, Veterinary Services about 18%, Diagnostics about 2% (my arithmetic).

C-37What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?C+6069th percentilefull answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

D4228th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: disruption forces act on almost every actor in Vimian's value chain. The net effect leaves Vimian moderately exposed in two businesses (VetFamily and US orthopaedics), lightly exposed in two (dental and allergy, which gain from AI and in-clinic anchoring) and relatively exposed in a third (Diagnostics, against ecosystem incumbents).

  • On demand (owners):
  • Online fulfilment intent is rising, with credit and insurance tools at checkout (CareCredit and Clarus) and price transparency.
  • This shifts chronic-medicine purchasing out of the clinic and makes owners more price-aware.
  • Effect on Vimian: indirect, through clinics' economics. Elective deferral hits Movora.
  • On competition:
  • AI-enabled ecosystems (IDEXX-CoVet, Zoetis-VitalRADS teleradiology, Covetrus software lock-in) raise switching costs towards the incumbents.
  • Effect: negative for Indical and VetFamily, neutral for implants and immunotherapy.
  • On regulators: transparency regimes (UK CMA) and pharmacy licensing (California) act in opposite directions on online fulfilment. Effect: small.
  • On suppliers: right-shoring and tariff avoidance raise costs temporarily (relocation, bottlenecks, quality). Effect: a modest cost headwind for MedTech.
  • On clinics:
  • AI scribes (about 30% of professionals; 83.7% using AI) raise productivity.
  • Consolidation continues; consolidators generate 65% to 70% of industry revenue.
  • Clinics outsource marketing and scheduling.
  • Effect: corporate buyers squeeze supplier prices, while productive independents may buy more diagnostics and dental equipment.
  • Net fragility:
  • VetFamily: moderate.
  • US orthopaedics: moderate.
  • Diagnostics: high relative exposure, small in profit.
  • Dental and allergy: low, with AI tools as a tailwind.

Industry structure

Judgement51C-2452C-2540C-2666C-2745C-2860C-2945C-3151C-3545C-3646

C-24

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

D5226th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Vimian is number one or two in four of its niches but a minor player in the overall animal-health industry. It is under 1% of a roughly USD 50bn market and far from the leaders. It is not a leader in diagnostics. The relevant ranking for the model is the niche one; the industry ranking explains why the giants ignore Vimian.

  • Niche positions, by segment:
  • Veterinary orthopaedics (Movora, within MedTech). Number one or two, with the only fully integrated, full-suite offer (over 6,000 products from fracture plates to hip systems). The small veterinary units of DePuy Synthes and Arthrex are the scaled rivals. An expert expects Movora to keep taking share from them.
  • Veterinary dentistry (iM3 plus AllAccem and Dental Focus, within MedTech). Global leader in dental equipment, imaging and consumables, in a fragmented niche.
  • Veterinary buying groups (VetFamily). Described as the only global platform, with 12,400 member clinics in 14 markets. Mars' Antech and corporate networks are different models.
  • Allergy diagnostics and immunotherapy (Nextmune, within Specialty Pharma). "Globally leading position in veterinary allergology".
  • Diagnostics (Indical, I-Vet). Marginal. IDEXX holds 72% of point-of-care equipment and 56% of reference labs, Mars' Antech 33%, Zoetis 6% of reference labs.
  • Industry position:
  • Group revenue of EUR 425m (2025) against a market of about USD 50bn (2024).
  • The top five are Zoetis 17%, IDEXX 16%, Boehringer Ingelheim 14%, Merck 13%, Elanco 9%.
  • Vimian is outside the top ten by revenue. Vetoquinol, at about EUR 526m, is described as the eleventh largest.
  • The Leadership digest notes a "profound silence": no mention of Vimian in Zoetis', IDEXX's or Swedencare's transcripts.
  • Distance to number one or two: zero in the four niches, very large in the industry. The niche leadership protects margins. The industry smallness means no pricing umbrella of its own and vulnerability if a giant chooses to enter a niche.
C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

D4027th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the overall animal-health market is highly concentrated, with the top five holding about 70%, well above the 30% test. Share drops steeply from the leader to number five and then to a long tail. Vimian's niches sit in that fragmented tail, where concentration is not measured. That is good for consolidation and share gain, and means there is no protective oligopoly pricing within the niches.

  • Industry: five firms hold about 70%. Diagnostics is even more concentrated: IDEXX 72% of point-of-care equipment; reference labs a duopoly of IDEXX 56% and Antech 33%.
  • Steepness: steep from Zoetis (17%) to Elanco (9%). The research does not give positions six to ten (Virbac, Vetoquinol, Dechra and others, each a low single-digit share). The top five probably hold more than five times the share of numbers six to ten. That is my inference from the ranks; it is not stated.
  • Niches (not measured in the research):
  • Veterinary orthopaedics: few scaled players (Movora, DePuy Synthes Vet, Arthrex Vet) plus small regional and copycat suppliers.
  • Dental: fragmented, iM3 the leader.
  • Buying groups: fragmented locally, VetFamily the only global player.
  • Allergy immunotherapy: Nextmune leading, others unnamed in the files.
  • Customer side (clinics), concentrating fast: corporate groups own about 30% of US general practices, 80% of US specialty clinics and 60% to 80% of UK practices. Consolidated networks generate 60% to 70% of diagnostic and pharmaceutical spend.
  • Implication: Vimian sells concentrated-customer-facing niche products into a market whose giants compete with each other on blockbusters. Its niches are fragmented enough to consolidate by acquisition. Its customers are consolidating faster than its suppliers' competitors, which is the structural pressure point.
C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

C6663rd percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: shares in Vimian's niches have been stable over three to five years, and new entrants have taken no measurable share there. In the adjacent therapeutics market, new entrants have taken share fast (Elanco's Zenrelia and Credelio Quattro; Merck's Numelvi). That shows how quickly position can move once a well-funded rival chooses a category.

  • Niche stability:
  • Orthopaedics: stable, held by instrument and training lock-in. Movora is "expected to continue winning share".
  • Allergy immunotherapy: Nextmune's position is stable, anchored by PAX diagnostics and patient-level lock-in.
  • Buying groups: VetFamily grew from about 10,000 to 12,400 members, partly organically and partly through Vetsave.
  • Dental: iM3 is consolidating a fragmented niche by acquisition.
  • Entrant gains in adjacent categories:
  • Zenrelia penetrated over 50% of US practices and reached blockbuster status within about four quarters.
  • Credelio Quattro gained 3 points of broad-spectrum share in Q1 2026 (over 40% of practices).
  • Zoetis' US dermatology share fell 5 points to 86%.
  • Generic entrants offer 50% discounts.
  • In diagnostics, Mindray AI analysers and Fujifilm's IDS i10 are entering at the price end.
  • Implication: share stability in Vimian's niches reflects the giants' disinterest as much as Vimian's moats. The adjacent evidence shows a determined entrant can move share by several points a year. That is the fragility.
C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

D4526th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: market share consolidates over time towards two kinds of company. One is the integrated ecosystem players: IDEXX (instruments, software, consumables), Covetrus-MWI (practice software plus distribution), Chewy (online pharmacy plus clinics) and Mars (clinics, labs, diagnostics). The other is the corporate clinic networks. Vimian is neither. It consolidates its niches by acquisition, not by gravitational pull. It is among the advantaged within its niches (clinical training, instrument and patient lock-in) but not among the ecosystem players towards whom industry share is flowing.

  • Towards the largest: strongly in diagnostics and distribution. IDEXX's share holds above 50% of companion diagnostics. Covetrus-MWI combines 40% practice-management software share with distribution after Cencora sold MWI for USD 1.25bn plus USD 800m of preferred equity.
  • Towards the most advantaged: towards those with software, data and autoship lock-in. Chewy expects Modern Animal technology to lift net sales per customer 15% to 20%, and IDEXX bought CoVet. Vimian's advantages are clinical (training, instruments, immunotherapy). They hold share but do not pull it.
  • Vimian's own consolidation:
  • By acquisition: 59 deals since 2015.
  • By cross-selling: 18% of sales.
  • By geographic expansion of VetFamily.
  • The research's own lesson ("fragmentation is usually structural, not a roll-up opportunity") applies to veterinary buying groups and to clinic-facing niches with local relationships. A roll-up gains share only as fast as it buys.
  • Implication: Vimian's market share growth is management-driven and capital-dependent. It will not compound passively. Vimian is "among the advantaged" in its niches, which protects the base, but it is not riding the industry's consolidation current.
C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

C-6053rd percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: competitors' attitude is margin- and return-first in Vimian's niches and has historically been benign and cooperative across the industry. Innovators priced above inflation and industry prices did not fall when clinic volumes fell. That changed at the blockbuster level in 2026. Zoetis, the leader, is now willing to give margin back to defend volume. The price war is in parasiticides and dermatology, not in implants, dental consumables or immunotherapy.

  • Historically:
  • Innovators implemented price rises well above core inflation.
  • Competitors avoided aggressive cuts.
  • Branded animal generics erode only 30% to 40% at patent expiry (Sushil Finance), far less than human generics.
  • Clinics raised prices even as visits fell for eighteen quarters.
  • 2026 change at the top:
  • Zoetis triggered "an aggressive companion animal pricing reset with targeted promotions and gross-to-net rebates". Q3 organic was -1% on lower net price.
  • Morgan Stanley estimates an implied 8% fall in net price realisation in H2 2026.
  • Elanco took its largest US price increase in five years and is now facing promotional responses from Zoetis and Merck.
  • Do industry prices fall when volume falls? Historically no; in 2026, yes in contested blockbuster categories.
  • In Vimian's niches: no evidence of losses accepted for share or of price cuts. Competitors there are smaller units of large groups (DePuy, Arthrex) or fragmented private firms.
  • Implication: the cooperative pricing regime is intact where Vimian sells. It is breaking at the industry's centre, where it can spill into Specialty Pharma's dermatology through cheaper symptomatic alternatives (C-02).
C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

D4542nd percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: competitor attitude has become less stable over the last eighteen months at the industry's centre. Zoetis' 2026 promotional reset is a reactive, situation-dependent move by a leader losing share: a real-world "shark attack" on pricing in its own categories. Vimian is too small and too niche to provoke one against itself today. The risk is spill-over from the blockbuster price war, plus a large player deciding to enter a niche, not a direct attack.

  • The last eighteen months to three years:
  • Elanco's launches (Zenrelia, Credelio Quattro) took share.
  • Zoetis responded with gross-to-net rebates and promotions, cut 2026 guidance (August), and lost 21.5% of its market value in one day (May 2026).
  • Merck launched Numelvi against Apoquel.
  • The leader went from price-setter to price-defender within about a year. That is the "Michael O'Leary" pattern: reactive and hard to predict.
  • Would they shark-attack Vimian? Not observed and not likely in implants, sealants or immunotherapy, where no giant has a franchise to defend.
  • The plausible channels: 1. Promotions in dermatology making symptomatic drugs cheaper than immunotherapy. 2. Ecosystem players (IDEXX, Covetrus, Mars) using software and reference-lab lock-in against independents and their buying groups (the Business Foundations counter-case). 3. Low-cost orthopaedic copycats being used by corporate groups in tenders.
  • Trend: adverse at the margin. Stability in the niches is a function of the giants' focus elsewhere. That focus could shift if growth in their core categories stalls, which it is doing at Zoetis.
C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

D5134th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the industry structure is supportive of Vimian's model but the support is fragile.

Supportive:

  • Cash-pay demand with no reimbursement risk.
  • Regulatory barriers (FDA CVM, EMA registration).
  • A concentrated manufacturer oligopoly that has priced with discipline and ignores Vimian's niches.
  • Fragmented, under-penetrated niches in which Vimian leads.
  • A value chain whose highest-margin stages (specialty product IP and group purchasing, each at about 28% EBIT in the research's mapping) are exactly where Vimian sits.

Fragile:

  • Buyer power is consolidating into corporate networks.
  • The leader has started a price war in adjacent categories.
  • Ecosystem players are integrating software, distribution and clinics.
  • Online disintermediation is forming.

Vimian's position rests on being small and specialised enough not to attract a giant's attention, which is a defence by obscurity, not by strength.

  • Value-chain economics (Business Foundations stage map):
  • Raw inputs and APIs: 45% gross, 18% EBIT (absent)
  • Contract manufacturing: 35% / 15% (absent)
  • Specialty product IP: 70% / 28% (present)
  • Distribution: 12% / 4% (present, moving direct)
  • Veterinary buying group: 85% / 28% (present)
  • End clinics: 75% gross / 18% EBITDA (minor)

Vimian has chosen the right stages. Its 68.9% gross margin compares with Swedencare's 54.6%.

  • Fragility, ranked: 1. Customer consolidation shifting bargaining power (slow, certain). 2. Online and software disintermediation (slow, forming). 3. Blockbuster price war spill-over (possible, moderate). 4. A giant entering a niche (low probability, high impact).
  • For the model:
  • Industry structure supports Vimian's gross margin at about 69% and segment margins of 25% to 30% for 2027-2029.
  • It does not support expansion of the gross margin through price.
  • The downside is a 100 to 200 basis point compression in Specialty Pharma or dental if consolidators or promotions bite.
C-35To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?D4530th percentilefull answer under 1a External headwinds/threats
C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

D4634th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: substitutes are a moderate and mixed threat:

  • Rising in dermatology and allergy: symptomatic drugs are becoming cheaper through promotions and competition.
  • Steady to falling in orthopaedics: injectables are slowed by safety concerns, though conservative management by cost-constrained owners is rising.
  • Long-dated in dental: a pharmacological alternative to periodontal surgery around 2030.

  • Rising for the clinic pharmacy channel: online fulfilment.

  • Orthopaedics.

  • Owners substitute conservative management (pain medication, weight control) and injectables for surgery when budgets tighten. Only one in a hundred dogs needing hip surgery is treated.
  • The Librela FDA warning letter and the 48% of veterinarians now more cautious reduce the injectable substitute's momentum.
  • Net: the substitute is mostly "do nothing or medicate", which is cyclical with household budgets.
  • Allergy and dermatology.
  • Immunotherapy (disease-modifying, customised) competes with lifelong symptomatic drugs.
  • Zoetis' Apoquel and Cytopoint now face Zenrelia and Numelvi and a promotional reset. The substitute is getting cheaper.
  • Mitigant: veterinary dermatologists' reluctance to switch away from trusted, proven protocols.
  • Dental. VBX-1000 (licensed from Medivir, targeted 2030) could reduce equipment-heavy dental surgery. A 2030 question, but it touches iM3's equipment, not SANOS consumables.
  • Clinic channel. Online pharmacies price chronic medicines 50% to 60% below clinics (UK) and fill about 12% of US scripts. This erodes VetFamily members' economics more than Vimian's own products.
  • Direction of change: worsening in dermatology, stable in orthopaedics, distant in dental.

Strong business SRCA and BTE; management; financial profile

D5234th percentile

Memo topic 3, Value chain, structure, model, financials, management: the Rater's score

4a

Business quality

D5338th percentile8 answers
Judgement53C-4155C-4240C-4366C-4460C-4552C-4652C-4752
Group judgement

Company - Competitive advantage

D5338th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Vimian's competitive advantages are real, niche-specific and holding.

  • Instrument and training lock-in in orthopaedics.
  • Lifelong patient lock-in in allergy immunotherapy.
  • A razor-and-blade dental platform.
  • A rebate-sharing buying group with the only global footprint.
  • An under-appreciated proprietary acquisition-sourcing capability rooted in its decentralised culture.

Together they produce demonstrable customer utility (organic growth above market every year since 2021) and pricing that defends a 69% gross margin.

What they do not produce is the financial signature of a compounding advantage:

  • returns well above the cost of capital (ROIC 9.4%)
  • margins that rise with scale (the adjusted EBITA margin is down 150 basis points over three years)
  • real pricing gains (gross margin flat)

The advantages are confined to small niches, with no scale advantage against the industry giants and no group operating leverage by design. The most valuable advantage, cheap bilateral deal sourcing, is also the one most dependent on culture, which shows signs of strain.

For the model:

  • The moats justify holding segment margins (Specialty Pharma about 30%, MedTech 24% to 27%, Veterinary Services about 28% to 30%) and gross margin of about 69%, against competitive and customer pressure.
  • They justify organic growth of 7% to 9%.
  • They do not justify modelling margin expansion from scale or price. Any expansion must come from mix and the recovery of the weak units (Diagnostics, US orthopaedics).
C-41

Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

D5537th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Vimian holds real, demonstrable moats inside its niches, mostly customer-related switching costs, plus one less-noticed advantage: proprietary acquisition sourcing. Each moat is confined to its niche, and the moats sit in the segments with the best economics. None gives scale or cost advantage against the industry giants. They are holding, and widening only in dental and in the buying group's geography.

Strength and direction by moat type:

1. Customer-related (strong; holding to widening).

  • Orthopaedics (Movora): instrument and hardware lock-in. Switching implant brand means replacing the surgical instrument set, and Movora carries over 6,000 products. Clinical training entrenches the workflows: 65,000 professionals trained in 2025, 1,300 veterinarians in Q3 2026 alone. Strong. Holding; widening through soft tissue (Norfolk) into the same surgeons.
  • Allergy (Nextmune): customised allergen immunotherapy creates lifelong patient-level lock-in, anchored by proprietary PAX tests that feed the prescription. The Pet Allergy Explorer adds 25 allergens. Strong. Widening modestly through test innovation.
  • Dental (iM3, AllAccem): equipment plus consumables plus software (SANOS sealant reorders, Clarity XR AI imaging). A razor-and-blade structure. Moderate to strong. Widening, because the platform is being built.
  • Diagnostics (Indical): SmartLab subscription reagent pull-through (industry retention near 90%). But contracts run under one year, with annual re-tender. Weak to moderate. Not widening.
  • Buying group (VetFamily): a tiered rebate flywheel. Members get better rebates as commitment rises, and more members extract more rebates. Moderate. Widening in geography (14 markets, 12,400 members), eroding slowly in mature markets through consolidation and software disintermediation.

2. Production-related (weak). Most manufacturing is outsourced. Indical makes PCR and ELISA products in Germany and the Netherlands. The production moat is product registration, not process or cost.

3. Scale-based (moderate inside niches, absent against giants).

  • Inside its niches: the only global buying group, the only full-suite veterinary orthopaedic offer, and cross-selling at 18% of sales.
  • Against Zoetis or IDEXX, none.

4. Regulatory (moderate, durable). FDA CVM and EMA registrations protect the incumbent's registered portfolio and slow entrants.

5. Culture and knowledge (moderate, and the most under-appreciated).

  • The decentralised "natural home for ambitious entrepreneurs" culture is what produces the M&A sourcing advantage: about 80% bilateral deals at historically about 7.5x (CFO: about 9x) against listed peers near 16x, and retained founders (I-Vet).
  • That advantage lets Vimian buy growth below its own multiple, which is accretive by construction.
  • It is fragile to culture erosion: 19% staff turnover in 2025, a disputed employee net promoter score, Clarity 59/100.

Overall: strong in two niches (orthopaedics, allergy), moderate in two (dental, buying group), weak in diagnostics. The moats are real and do protect gross margin (68.9%). They do not produce high group returns (ROIC 9.4%), because the acquisition model pays away part of their value (C-51).

C-42

How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

F4023rd percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Vimian is small relative to the industry, at about EUR 425m of revenue against a roughly USD 50bn market and leaders a multiple of its size. It is large relative to its niche rivals. Its scale gives it niche leadership, a cross-selling network, training infrastructure and an acquisition platform. It gives no cost advantage or operating leverage at group level, by design.

  • Size markers:
  • Revenue EUR 425m in 2025; consensus EUR 484m for 2026.
  • About 80 markets served (some sources say 60).
  • Over 17,000 clinics and laboratories as customers.
  • 12,400 VetFamily members.
  • Group headquarters of only thirteen people.
  • Market capitalisation about EUR 2.0bn.
  • Zoetis and IDEXX are many times larger. Zoetis' R&D budget alone (USD 698m in 2025) exceeds Vimian's revenue.
  • What scale confers: 1. Niche leadership and the right to set standards in training (orthopaedics, dental). 2. A distribution network across 17,000 clinics into which every acquired product is cross-sold. The research's evidence: about a third of Specialty Pharma's organic growth. 3. Buying-group rebate power for members. 4. An M&A platform able to absorb two to five bolt-ons a year at below-market multiples. 5. Enough size for index inclusion and broker coverage (six visible brokers).
  • What it does not confer:
  • Purchasing scale with contract manufacturers against the giants.
  • R&D scale: Vimian capitalised EUR 6.0m of R&D and software against Zoetis' 7% to 8% of revenue.
  • Group-level cost leverage. The adjusted EBITA margin fell from 26.3% to 24.8% while revenue rose 28% from 2023 to 2025.
  • The research's "Scale needs a bounded market" test applies: within each niche the market is bounded and Vimian leads. Across the group, scale is additive, not multiplicative.

C-44

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

D6047th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: surplus customer utility is high in Vimian's chronic and procedural niches, and inferiority is not demonstrated anywhere in the research. On the framework's standard, which is easier to disprove than prove, Vimian passes at Triaging depth. The evidence is behavioural: retention, organic growth above market, share gains, membership growth. There are no customer surveys.

  • Evidence of high or non-inferior utility, by niche:
  • Orthopaedics. Movora is "expected to continue winning share" from the J&J and Arthrex units (expert). Integrated tooling and training produce "exceptionally high customer retention". The US business grew at low single digits in Q3 2026 despite unchanged market conditions, which is share gain.
  • Allergy. The customised immunotherapy solves a lifelong problem and is diagnosed with the company's own tests. Allergy and dermatology grew double digit in Q3 2026.
  • Dental. A full-suite equipment, imaging, consumables and software platform, plus education-led adoption in 40 markets. Dental demand is "robust".
  • Buying group. Members receive supplier rebates directly, a quantifiable financial utility. Membership grew from 10,948 to 12,400 in under a year, partly by acquisition.
  • Across segments: cross-selling uptake suggests clinics value one supplier for several niche needs.
  • Where utility is weaker:
  • Diagnostics (Indical is not a premium ecosystem player against IDEXX).
  • The independent-clinic proposition of VetFamily against software-integrated alternatives (Covetrus, Chewy). Not yet shown to be inferior, but under test.
  • Group-level proof: organic growth above the animal-health market every year since 2021, and in 2026 at 9% to 12% against peers about 7% and Zoetis -1%. That is consistent with leading utility in the niches.
C-45

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

C+5267th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Vimian has and uses pricing power in nominal terms. Price rises of about 5% are applied, wage inflation is passed through, and there is no evidence of price given back or of customers lost to price. Gross margin is stable at about 69% through two years of input inflation. That is evidence of application. But there is no evidence of real pricing power (above cost inflation) and no disclosure of price within organic growth. The power is demonstrably sufficient to defend margin, not demonstrably sufficient to expand it.

  • Evidence of application:
  • Average price increases of about 5% implemented, and wage inflation fully passed to veterinary customers (Business Foundations).
  • The Specialty Pharma margin rose to 30.3% from 29.5% in Q3 2026 and has been steady at about 30% for two years.
  • The MedTech margin rose on dental gross margins.
  • Gross margin was 68.9% in 2024 and 2025 against input costs up about 13% a year.
  • What is not demonstrated:
  • A price contribution above cost inflation. Gross margin was flat, not rising.
  • A split of organic growth into price, volume and mix. Not disclosed.
  • Resistance testing. Vimian has not priced through a severe customer squeeze in its niches. The clinics' own price rises are the ones under pushback ("clinics out-pricing consumer demand").
  • Relative position:
  • Vimian's pricing is holding while Zoetis gives price back. That is the research's "niche insulation" point (DNB Carnegie, Jefferies): Vimian has superior pricing power relative to blockbuster competitors in a price war.
  • The Wisdom Library entry "Pricing power breaks before demand does" warns that clinics' pricing is the stressed link. Vimian's own pricing would be tested second, through corporate tenders.
  • Erosion of relative utility: none visible. Volumes in the niches are growing.
  • For the model: price about 3% to 5% a year in organic growth, matched by costs. Gross margin flat at about 69%. No assumed real pricing gain.
C-46

To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

B5275th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: scale economies are explicitly shared with customers in one segment only, the VetFamily buying group, whose model is built on passing supplier rebates to members in tiers. Elsewhere there is no stated or de facto sharing. The culture is entrepreneurial and margin-seeking, not cost-leadership. This is a modest, segment-limited form of the "scale economies shared" pattern.

  • VetFamily.
  • It acts as a virtual corporate network for independents. It extracts volume rebates from major suppliers, including from their marketing budgets, and shares them with 12,400 member clinics through a three-tier model: more commitment brings more rebate.
  • That is a textbook shared-scale flywheel. More members bring more rebate power, which brings more value per member, which brings more members.
  • Its evidence is membership growth. Its limit is the shrinking independent pool in mature markets.
  • The rest of the group:
  • No pricing messaging around passing on scale benefits. Specialty Pharma and MedTech take price.
  • Cross-selling is not discounted bundling in the evidence.
  • The direct-sales conversion captures distributor margin for Vimian, not for the customer.
  • Implication: Vimian does not build the deep loyalty that comes from visibly sharing scale (Costco-type). It relies on switching costs and clinical relationships. That is fine for margins, but it means customer loyalty in a squeeze rests on lock-in, not goodwill.
C-47

To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

D5242nd percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: gravitational or mechanical forces that improve competitive advantage with time are present but modest:

  • the trained-surgeon base
  • the immunotherapy patient base
  • the buying group's rebate loop
  • the cross-selling network

These forces do not show up in improving group financial outputs against peers. The adjusted EBITA margin fell 150 basis points in 2023-2025 while revenue grew 28%. The compounding is management- and acquisition-driven, not gravitational.

  • Mechanical forces at work: 1. Every surgeon trained raises future implant demand and switching cost (65,000 professionals a year). 2. Every allergy patient started on immunotherapy is a multi-year recurring revenue stream. 3. Every buying-group member adds rebate power for the others. 4. Every acquired product added to the network can be sold to the existing clinic base without new customer acquisition cost.
  • Why they do not compound the financials:
  • Each force requires continuing spend: training centres, field sales, new-country set-up, acquisitions.
  • The decentralised model gives limited shared-cost leverage (thirteen people at the centre, segment-level autonomy).
  • Margin gains are bought (dental acquisitions at premium margins) rather than earned through scale.
  • Measured against peers: the operating outputs compound (organic growth above peers) but the financial outputs do not (margin flat to down, ROIC 9.4% against IDEXX 41% and Zoetis 18.4%).
  • For the model: do not assume structural margin expansion from scale. Margin gains in 2027-2029 should be modelled segment by segment from mix (Diagnostics recovery, dental, MedTech's US orthopaedic leverage on a fixed field force), not as a group operating-leverage coefficient.

Management is new, acting and commercially able, and it sits inside a governance and incentive system built for scale rather than per-share returns.

How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

C-6358th percentile

  • the dental platform build
  • the pivot from platform deals to bolt-ons
  • distributor-to-direct conversion
  • companion diagnostics
  • cross-selling as an organic engine

  • In a weak consumer market, chronic allergy, dental consumables and the buying group's rebate value hold. Elective orthopaedics suffers, and 2025 showed it.
  • In a strong market, orthopaedics and diagnostics lever up.
  • Acquisitions continue in both, funded by cash and debt.
  • Gaps:
  • The strategy does not say how growth converts to cash and returns.
  • There is no capital-return policy beyond reinvesting.
  • The perimeter is drifting (cremation).
  • The 2030 target has no modellable bridge. The Capital Markets Day is the chance to supply one.

C-63

Explain, with the evidence, the extent to which the company puts shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent its governance is (scaffolding put in place even where it was not needed): what does the evidence show, and why?

F285th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: the evidence says shareholders as a whole come second to scale, and minority shareholders come second to the controlling sponsor's growth strategy. Governance scaffolding is present in form but thin in substance. The reason is structural: a sponsor-controlled roll-up whose incentives, committees and capital policy are set to maximise absolute EBITA growth, with no return discipline and no capital returns. Minorities fund the growth through dilution and bear the governance risk.

Evidence for shareholders first or prudent governance:

  • An independent chair (Magnus Welander, since 2024, holding 109,630 shares and 100,000 call options).
  • Three of five directors independent; average tenure about 2.5 years.
  • The audit committee is chaired by an independent (Pia Marions, a public-market CFO).
  • Fidelio's representatives waive board fees.
  • No poison pills or transfer restrictions.
  • Grant Thornton auditor continuity, no restatements.
  • MSCI AAA (May 2026).
  • The CEO's maximum bonus is capped at 100% of salary.
  • The LTI requires executives to buy "hurdle shares", putting capital at risk.
  • LTIP dilution capped at 3.0%.
  • The CEO's own holding is large.

Evidence against:

  • Dilution. Two equity raises since the 2021 listing at falling valuations, including SEK 1.6bn in 2024 after the USD 70m DePuy Synthes settlement that stemmed from an acquired subsidiary (VOI). Shares rose from about 402m to 529m.
  • No capital returns: no dividend, no buyback, 100% reinvestment.
  • Incentives:
  • 70% absolute adjusted EBITA plus 30% revenue, with no ROIC test, no clawback, and undisclosed hurdles.
  • Adjusted EBITA excludes deal costs, so it rewards buying EBITA.
  • The board retains the right to raise pay limits in "exceptional circumstances".
  • The outgoing chief executive got an EUR 85k bonus for partial organic delivery despite 0% on EBITA, plus EUR 446k of "other remuneration".
  • Sponsor control of capital. Fidelio (58.4%) chairs the M&A committee, was paid SEK 250k a quarter for M&A support in 2024, and backs M&A funding. Majority board independence is described in the research as "optical" given this.
  • Related party. Licensing income from an entity owned by a Specialty Pharma manager (EUR 9.4m in 2024, EUR 9.7m in 2025) is disclosed but unexplained. Transactions with Diagnostics minorities were EUR 4.5m (2024).
  • Controls. No internal audit function; board self-assessment across about 80 countries; 12 whistleblower complaints in 2025.

Why: the sponsor's interest is to build a large platform. Minorities benefit only if growth converts to per-share value, which the incentive system does not test. The Wisdom Library's "founder-led / owner-operator tiger risk" fits: a controlling owner brings stability and optionality, and is "not your friend" on dilution or exit terms.

C-64

To what extent is the company an enlightened capital allocator and a dynamic repurchaser of its shares, not just an operator or generator, with a record of allocating capital at a high return on new invested capital?

F3220th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: Vimian is an allocator by acquisition only. It has done 59 deals at about 7.5x to 9x EBITDA. It has never repurchased shares and pays no dividend. Its record of allocating at a high return on new capital is not demonstrated: ROIC is 9.4%, after-tax adjusted ROCE 5.4% in 2025, one acquisition (VOI) produced a USD 70m settlement, and equity was issued twice at falling prices. It is a disciplined buyer on entry price and an unproven creator of value per share.

  • Allocation record:
  • Disciplined entry multiples relative to peers (16x), sourced bilaterally.
  • A dental platform that worked: margin-accretive, offsetting orthopaedic decline.
  • Norfolk at about 9x with a clear cross-selling rationale.
  • Against:
  • The aggregate return.
  • The equity issuance history.
  • Earn-outs and stay-on bonuses that raise the real price.
  • Perimeter creep (cremation).
  • No buyback even in February 2026 at about SEK 28 (about 16x forward EBIT).
  • An incentive system without a return test.
  • The M&A committee chaired by the sponsor.
  • Not a "dynamic repurchaser": there has been no repurchase at any price, and the policy is 100% reinvestment.
  • For the model: assume continued acquisition spending of about EUR 60m to 120m a year, no buybacks and no dividends through FY2029 (consensus has nil dividends). Assume share count growth of about 0.3% to 0.5% a year from LTIPs, and a non-trivial chance of equity issuance if the 2030 ambition is pursued literally.

Financial profile

Group judgement

Company - Economic strength

D3928th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Vimian's economic strength is a growth engine with weak conversion.

Strengths:

  • Revenue growth is durable: about 15% a year reported over three years, organic 6% to 12%.
  • Gross margin is high and stable at about 69%.
  • Segment margins are attractive (25% to 30%) and supported by moats.
  • There is a long runway to deploy capital.

Weaknesses:

  • The conversion of that growth into shareholder earnings and cash has been poor and is not yet improving:
  • The adjusted EBITA margin has drifted down 150 to 200 basis points in three years, on incremental margins of about 19% to 22%.
  • EPS on the brokers' basis grew about 9% a year against revenue at about 15%.
  • ROIC is about 9% to 11%.
  • Cash conversion is about half of EBITDA year to date, with net working capital stuck near 30% of trailing revenue on FactSet's basis.
  • Adjusted earnings are flattered by recurring "one-offs", capitalised development and an unexplained related-party income stream.
  • About 70% of adjusted EBITA leaks away before reported net income.

The critical modelling insight is that consensus requires a break with history on three fronts at once from FY2027:

  1. Incremental margins jumping to about 27% to 36%.
  2. Financial items falling by about EUR 10m.
  3. Tax normalising towards 27% to 28%.

Each is plausible alone. Together they carry most of consensus's +21% and +26% EPS growth in FY2027-2028.

My base for the Consumer:

  • Revenue +11% to +12% in 2027 (organic about 8% to 9% plus acquisitions, less currency).
  • Adjusted EBITA margin +30 to +60 basis points a year.
  • Financial items EUR 17m to 20m.
  • Tax 29% to 31%.
  • Net working capital at about 26% of revenue (company basis).

That gives EPS growth in the low-to-mid teens, not the twenties. Operating leverage is low and skewed to the downside. Perimeter width widens the forecast error.

C-49

What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?

D4549th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Vimian generates durable revenue growth (about 15% a year reported over FY2022-2025, organic about 6% to 9%) but has converted little of it into EPS. On the brokers' basis, EPS rose from EUR 0.074 (FY2023) to 0.087 (FY2024) to 0.088 (FY2025), about 9% a year, with FY2025 flat while revenue grew 13%. Consensus now expects EPS growth to outrun revenue growth from FY2027 (+21% and +26%). That requires a break with three years of history: margin expansion plus lower financial costs plus tax normalisation. The revenue engine is durable. The EPS conversion is not yet proven.

  • History:
  • Revenue: EUR 281m (FY2022), 332m, 375m, 425m (FY2025). Growth 17.9%, 13.0%, 13.4%. Acquisitions contributed 10 points in FY2025.
  • EPS was held back by three things:
    • the adjusted EBITA margin falling from 26.3% (2023) to 24.8% (2025)
    • net interest of EUR 19m to 25m a year, against adjusted EBITA of EUR 87m to 105m
    • a 12% rise in the share count from the 2024 rights issue (472m to 529m)
  • Reported EPS rose from a very low base (EUR 0.021 to 0.059). Taken alone that flatters, because items affecting comparability fell.
  • Consensus (brokers' basis):
Line FY2026 FY2027 FY2028 FY2029
EPS growth +13.7% +21.0% +25.8% +7.0% (one broker)
Revenue growth +13.8% +11.5% +9.0% +6.5%
  • From FY2026 to FY2028, consensus revenue rises 21%, EBITDA 28%, EBIT 42% and pre-tax profit 77%.
  • The leverage comes from three places:
    • EBIT margin up about 310 basis points in two years (18.3% to 21.4%)
    • the EBIT-to-pre-tax gap (mostly financial items) falling from about EUR 25m to about EUR 14m
    • the tax rate falling from about 33% to about 27% (implied by net income over pre-tax profit)
  • Basis trap. Brokers' FY2026 "adjusted" EPS ranges from EUR 0.089 (UBS) to 0.140 (DNB Carnegie). The gap of about EUR 0.05 is roughly what adding back amortisation of acquired intangibles and items affecting comparability after tax does per share: about EUR 25m to 30m after tax, over 530m shares (my arithmetic). The median of 0.100 is a blend of bases.
  • Durability:
  • Revenue: high. The niches, cross-selling and continued bolt-ons support 10% to 12% total growth.
  • EPS: moderate. It depends on the margin and below-EBITA assumptions tested in C-53, C-54 and C-76.
  • For the model:
  • Rebuild EPS from segment EBITA.
  • Decide explicitly whether to add back amortisation of acquired intangibles.
  • Treat the FY2027 below-EBITA step as a separate, testable assumption.
C-50

How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

D5042nd percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: there is ample immediate scope to deploy capital: bolt-on acquisitions (a 235-target pipeline, about EUR 60m to 120m a year) and operating spend (field sales, new VetFamily countries, companion diagnostics, biologics). Deployment exceeds 20% of sales a year (investing outflows were EUR 113m in 2025, about 27% of revenue). But the return on that new capital is moderate, not high: about 8% to 9% post-tax at entry on acquisitions, rising with acquired units' organic growth. This supports durable growth and a fair multiple near the stock's own historical median. It does not support a compounder premium.

  • Scope:
  • Fragmented niches across dental, orthopaedics, diagnostics, buying groups and allergy, in about 80 markets.
  • About 80% bilateral sourcing.
  • Leverage headroom of about EUR 70m to 135m (to 2.5x to 3.0x).
  • Consensus free cash flow of EUR 58m to 78m a year in FY2027-2028.
  • Operating reinvestment: the US field force, education centres, three new VetFamily countries, I-Vet and companion diagnostics, Nextmune Bio.
  • Returns on new capital, my arithmetic:
  • At 8x to 10x EBITDA, the pre-tax EBITDA yield is 10% to 12.5%, and post-tax EBITA about 7.5% to 9.5%.
  • If acquired units grow organically at 7% to 8% and some cross-selling lands, the yield reaches about 11% to 13% by year five.
  • That is consistent with Jefferies' post-tax ROIC of 10.8% (2026e) rising to 11.1% (2027e), and DNB Carnegie's after-tax adjusted ROCE of 5.4% (2025) rising to 10.2% (2028e).
  • Operating spend returns are unknown. They show up as flat group margins while growth accelerates.
  • What it means for potential and the fair multiple:
  • Mid-term: high-single-digit organic growth plus 2 to 4 points of acquired growth for many years.
  • Long-term: value creation per euro deployed is thin, because entry yields are close to the cost of capital and synergies are revenue-led.
  • Fair multiple: around the stock's own five-year median 24-month-forward P/E of about 21x. The memo used 21.2x, between the core animal-health peers (about 12x to 15.7x EV/EBITA) and the Nordic compounders (about 15.4x to 16x EV/EBITA). The current 25.1x prices in a quality of reinvestment return that has not been shown.
C-51

How high are the returns on capital: post-tax return on new invested capital, on capital employed and on tangible capital?

F3622nd percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: returns on capital are low for the business quality the moats imply. Reported ROIC is 9.4% (2025) on EUR 590m of goodwill, against Zoetis at 18.4% and IDEXX at 41%. Broker estimates rise only slowly, to about 10% to 11% by 2027-2028. Return on tangible capital is likely high, because the business is asset-light apart from working capital. The gap between the two is the price paid for acquisitions. The return on new invested capital is about the cost of capital at entry.

  • Post-tax return on invested capital:
  • 9.4% reported (2025).
  • Jefferies: 10.8% (2026e), 11.1% (2027e).
  • DNB Carnegie after-tax adjusted ROCE: 5.4% (2025), 7.3% (2026e), 8.9% (2027e), 10.2% (2028e).
  • Below the framework's 15% test throughout the forecast period.
  • Return on capital employed: as above. It fails a 15% test.
  • Return on tangible capital (not stated in the research; my estimate with stated assumptions):
  • Tangible capital is mainly net working capital (company basis EUR 114m at Q2 2026) plus modest fixed assets. Capex is about 3.5% of sales, but the fixed-asset balance is not in the files.
  • On EUR 150m to 200m of tangible capital, post-tax adjusted EBITA of about EUR 80m to 90m gives roughly 40% to 55%.
  • The underlying businesses are high-return. The shareholder's return is diluted by goodwill.
  • Return on new invested capital: about 7.5% to 9.5% post-tax at entry for acquisitions at 8x to 10x (C-50), plus net working capital absorbed by growth (about 26% of incremental revenue on the company's basis, about 29% to 30% on FactSet's), which pulls the incremental return down further.
  • Trend: improving slowly, as organic EBITA growth accrues on a fixed goodwill base. New acquisitions at about 9x and rising net working capital dilute it.
  • For the model: ROIC of about 10% to 12% by 2028 is the realistic band. A path to 15% would need margin expansion and working capital release together.

C-54

How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

F2223rd percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: the earnings base is not clean. Adjusted EBITA (EUR 105.3m) exceeded reported EBIT (EUR 66.8m) by EUR 38.5m in 2025. Recurring roll-up costs are classified as one-offs. R&D and software are capitalised. FY2025 cash was flattered by litigation receipts. An unexplained related-party licensing stream sits in the best segment. Below EBITA, financial items, a volatile tax rate and the share count leak heavily: EUR 105.3m of adjusted EBITA became EUR 31.3m of reported net income (30%). The direction is improving, as items affecting comparability shrink, but the level of leakage is high.

  • Between adjusted and reported (2025):
  • Amortisation of acquired intangibles: EUR 23.8m. A real economic cost of buying growth. Adding it back is the brokers' practice, but brokers differ.
  • Items affecting comparability: EUR 14.7m. Acquisition costs, integration, stay-on bonuses (EUR 2.9m to 3.9m in Specialty Pharma), contingent consideration revaluations (-EUR 3.1m for strong dental earnings). In a company doing five or more deals a year these recur. Treat EUR 5m to 10m a year as recurring.
  • Flattering items:
  • EUR 6.0m of R&D and software capitalised (1.41% of revenue). Jefferies: excluding it, the adjusted EBITA margin would be about 25.0% rather than 27.5% on its basis, about 250 basis points.
  • FY2025 operating cash included USD 28.7m of litigation receipts.
  • Pro-forma EBITDA (28.6% margin) is used for leverage.
  • Licensing income from an entity owned by a Specialty Pharma manager: EUR 9.4m (2024), EUR 9.7m (2025). If it carries little cost, it is a material share of the segment's profit. Undisclosed and unexplained.
  • Below EBITA (2025):
  • Net financial items EUR 20.6m (about 20% of adjusted EBITA).
  • Tax rate 29% (44% in 2023, 33% in 2024, 35% in Q1 2026), inflated by non-deductible translation effects and the Swedish parent's structure.
  • The share count up 12% after the 2024 rights issue. LTIP dilution capped at 3.0%.
  • Improving direction: the reported EBIT margin rose from 13.1% to 15.7% to 18.4% (Q2 2026) as items affecting comparability fall. That is the most credible quality signal in the files.
  • For the model:
  • Carry amortisation of acquired intangibles (about EUR 25m to 30m a year, rising with deals) and recurring items affecting comparability (EUR 5m to 10m) explicitly.
  • Model a tax rate of 29% to 31%, not consensus's 27% to 28% by FY2028, unless the translation-driven non-deductibles are shown to fade.
  • Flag the related-party licensing income as a separate line pending disclosure.
C-76

How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

D4526th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: financial leverage is moderate (2.0x pro-forma adjusted EBITDA on the company's measure, 1.7x on FactSet's) and comfortable against a 3.0x ceiling. But its cost is heavy relative to profit: net financial items of about EUR 20m to 25m against EBITA of about EUR 105m to 120m. Consensus leans heavily on that cost falling. About a third of FY2027's consensus pre-tax profit growth is lower financial items. A higher-for-longer rate regime, floating debt and continued debt-funded M&A make that the most fragile assumption in the EPS bridge after the margin.

  • Debt:
  • Company net debt EUR 262.4m at Q3 2026, down from 273.7m at Q2. Pro-forma leverage 2.0x.
  • FactSet: total debt EUR 280m, cash 55.6m, net 225m, 1.71x. This includes leases and appears to exclude earn-outs.
  • A EUR 150m senior unsecured bond, half swapped to fixed (August 2025). The rest of the bank debt is floating on three-month rates.
  • Average interest cost 4.1% (Q1 2026). A 1-point rate rise costs EUR 2.0m of pre-tax profit.
  • Cost relative to profit:
  • Net financial items EUR 20.6m (FY2025), FactSet net interest EUR 21.4m (FY2025), 25.4m (FY2024).
  • The consensus EBIT-to-pre-tax gap is about EUR 25m (FY2026), about EUR 15m (FY2027), about EUR 14m (FY2028).
  • FY2027 consensus pre-tax profit rises EUR 29.8m. EUR 19.5m comes from EBIT and EUR 10.3m from lower financial items (my arithmetic).
  • Is the fall plausible?
  • Pure interest on about EUR 260m to 280m of gross debt at about 4% is about EUR 11m to 12m. Lease interest, earn-out discount unwinding and currency revaluations (EUR 0.9m positive in Q1 2026) make up the rest.
  • A run-rate of EUR 14m to 16m is plausible only if net debt stays flat and currency and revaluation items net to near zero.
  • With M&A stepping up and a fifth of the 2027 step depending on it, model EUR 17m to 20m as the base and EUR 14m to 15m as the bull case.
  • How much it amplifies EPS:
  • At about EUR 120m to 135m of EBITA, a EUR 5m swing in financial items is about 5% to 6% of pre-tax profit.
  • A 1-point rate rise is about 2% to 3% of EPS.
  • Each EUR 100m of debt-funded acquisitions adds about 0.4x leverage and about EUR 4m of interest, against about EUR 11m of acquired EBITDA. That is accretive to EPS but raises risk.
  • Comfort:
  • Comfortable against the 3.0x ceiling. Uncomfortable in a stress case: the Risks report's 20% USD fall plus krona strength pushes leverage past 3.0x.
  • In a "Fragility Double Underweight" market regime, leverage is a share-price risk independent of covenants.

Pricing power

P-04

After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

C+5173rd percentile

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: price will contribute about 3 to 4 points a year to organic growth, about 40% of my 8% to 8.5%, and roughly nothing to margin.

  • What price does: it offsets cost inflation. Raw materials and merchandise grew 13% in H1 2026, and wages are passed through. It holds gross margin at 68.5% to 69.5%.
  • Where the power is: real in the chronic and consumable niches. It has never been shown above cost inflation.
  • Volume and mix, not price, drove 2026. Gross margin was flat while organic growth doubled (MedTech recovery, dental, allergy volumes).
  • The 18-month skew is negative. Price is more likely to contribute less than 3 points in Specialty Pharma (dermatology price war, corporate tenders) than more than 4.
  • Size of the risk: a 2-point shortfall in Specialty Pharma price, with costs unchanged, costs about EUR 4m of EBITA. That is about 3% of group EBITA and about 5% of EPS.
  • Consensus's margin expansion (+170 basis points of EBITDA margin from FY2025 to FY2027) cannot come from price, and price cannot rescue a margin miss.

Record and context:

  • Vimian took 5% to 6% in 2022-23. In 2024 it took low-to-mid single digits and was "cautious" on US MedTech pricing because the surgery market was soft. Wage inflation was "fully passed".
  • Gross margin: 69.2% (2023), 68.9% (2024), 68.9% (2025). That is pass-through with a small lag, not real gain.
  • Peers in 2026:
  • Vetoquinol took "more than 2.5%" in H1 2026 and lifted gross margin to 77.1% (mix and in-housing).
  • Virbac expects about 2%.
  • Zoetis's net price is falling. Morgan Stanley estimates −8% companion-animal net price realisation in H2 2026.
  • Elanco launched Zenrelia at about a 20% discount to Apoquel.
  • Merck backs Numelvi with a client guarantee: 7% of dermatology caseload, which vets expect to reach 12%.
  • Clinics are raising their own prices 10% to 12% a year.
  • Vimian prices below the clinic's own inflation and above the blockbuster price war. That is a defensible middle, not a pricing umbrella.

Line by line over 18 months (price capacity is my judgement):

Product line Segment Price capacity a year Main pressure Exposure
Allergy diagnostics (PAX) and customised immunotherapy Specialty Pharma 2% to 4% Cheaper symptomatic JAK inhibitors and antibodies raise immunotherapy's relative cost. Only about 2% of US pets are insured. Disease-modifying, with lifelong patient lock-in. Moderate
Dermatology OTC and prescription products Specialty Pharma 1% to 3% Promotions by Zoetis, Elanco and Merck. About 40% of US Apoquel sales are online (Morgan Stanley). Moderate to high
US retail nutrition campaigns Specialty Pharma Retailer-set Volume swings of 4 to 5 points of segment growth a quarter Low on price, high on volume
Dental consumables (SANOS sealant) MedTech 3% to 5% Corporate procurement of consumables Low
Dental equipment and imaging (iM3, Clarity XR) MedTech 1% to 3% Capital purchase, deferrable; dental-group procurement Moderate
Orthopaedic implants (Movora) MedTech US 0% to 2%; Europe 2% to 4% Elective deferral; corporate tenders at about 15% discounts (expert); tariffs not passed through in 2025 Moderate
Buying-group services (VetFamily) Veterinary Services Not price-led Value comes from supplier rebates; Covetrus VetSuite (4,000 practices) competes Low on price
Diagnostics reagents and tests (Indical, I-Vet) Diagnostics 0% to 2% Contracts under one year; IDEXX holds 72% of point-of-care Price-taker

The most exposed lines are dermatology and immunotherapy (Specialty Pharma is about 42% of revenue and about 47% of segment EBITA) and US orthopaedics.

  • The immunotherapy risk is relative-price erosion, not a price cut. The disease-modifying therapy keeps its list price, but some cost-sensitive owners choose the discounted symptomatic drug.
  • That would show as Specialty Pharma underlying organic growth slipping below about 8%, before any visible margin change.

Corporate buyers:

  • Corporate groups hold 60% to 80% of UK practices and about 30% of US general practices.
  • They run three-year RFPs on total cost.
  • IVC Evidensia places 70% of its contracted spending with fewer than 20 partners.
  • The result is a slow ratchet against supplier price, mostly in implants, dental consumables and diagnostics. It works against VetFamily's growth pool, while VetFamily's own value to the remaining independents rises.

Margin mechanics in my base:

  • Gross margin is held at about 69%.
  • The segment margin gains in P-01 come from mix: dental consumables, companion diagnostics, allergy within Specialty Pharma.
  • If price falls 1 point short across the group with cost inflation unchanged, about EUR 5m of gross profit is lost (about 1 point of gross margin). That is about 4% of EBITA.
  • The company does not disclose price within organic growth. Gross margin is the only proxy: a quarter below about 68% would be the first price signal.

Currency. A weaker dollar lowers reported prices in euros, but that is translation, not pricing. 38% of revenue is in US dollars and there is no hedging.

Tariffs. If US device tariffs are imposed, the hit is "low single-digit points" of EBITA before mitigation (Jefferies), about EUR 1.5m to 4m. The 2025 behaviour (price held to protect volume) suggests Vimian would absorb part of it.

Further out.

  • Animal-health tariff exemptions end on 20 January 2029.
  • VBX-1000, a pharmacological alternative to periodontal surgery, targets about 2030.
  • Neither changes the 2027-28 price assumption.

Operating leverage

C-7538P-0147
C-75

How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

F3816th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: fixed-cost operating leverage is low by design and, recently, negative. About 31% of revenue is cost of goods, which is variable. About 45% is personnel and external costs, sticky in the short run but grown deliberately by each autonomous unit. The centre is tiny. In a downturn the short-run leverage is meaningful (each 1% revenue shortfall costs about 2.5% to 3% of EBITA for a few quarters). In an upturn the leverage is mostly reinvested. So the range of outcomes is skewed: limited upside from volume, real short-run downside.

  • Cost structure (2025, % of EUR 425m revenue):
  • Raw materials and merchandise: EUR 132.2m (31.1%)
  • Personnel: EUR 108.2m (25.5%)
  • Other external: EUR 81.4m (19.2%)
  • Depreciation and amortisation: EUR 37.9m (8.9%)
  • Thirteen people at group headquarters; the rest in segments.
  • Manufacturing outsourced, so few plant fixed costs. Capex is about 3.5% of sales.
  • Evidence of how margin moves with volume:
  • 2023-2025: revenue +28%, adjusted EBITA margin -150 basis points (incremental about 19%).
  • First nine months of 2026: revenue about +13%, margin flat to slightly down (incremental about 22%).
  • Q3 2026: revenue +15%, adjusted EBITA +14%.
  • The volume recovery in MedTech (organic -4% to +15%) lifted MedTech's margin by only 70 basis points year on year in Q3, though 210 basis points in Q2.
  • The former group controller: the structure "limits large-scale cost takeouts".
  • Sensitivity (my arithmetic, FY2027 scale of about EUR 540m revenue and about EUR 130m to 137m EBITA):
  • A 1-point organic revenue shortfall is about EUR 5.4m of revenue and about EUR 3.7m of lost gross profit. With opex unchanged for two to three quarters, that is about 2.7% to 2.8% of EBITA.
  • After a year of hiring restraint, the effect falls to roughly 1.2% to 1.5% of EBITA.
  • A 3-point organic miss is worth about EUR 10m to 11m of EBITA in year one: -7% to -8%, and roughly -10% to -12% of EPS after financial leverage.
  • Upside is muted, because growth investments absorb incremental gross profit.
  • Where real leverage exists:
  • MedTech North America, where a fixed field force was installed in January 2026 ahead of volume.
  • Diagnostics, where two consolidated sites can absorb companion volume.

Together these are a few million euros of upside if volumes come.

P-01

After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

D4732nd percentile

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: fixed-cost operating leverage will add little to the main lines.

  • Margin path: in my base the adjusted EBITA margin rises from a 2026 trough of 24.5% to 24.8% (2027), 25.1% (2028), 25.4% (2029) and 25.5% (2030). That reverses only about half of the 180 basis-point fall since 2022 (26.1% then 26.3% in 2023), and leaves 2030 below 2023.
  • Incremental margin: the implied incremental EBITA margin is about 27%, against 19% to 22% delivered. Consensus implies a 36% incremental EBITDA margin in FY2027.
  • Where real fixed-cost leverage exists: only in the US orthopaedic field force and the two consolidated Diagnostics sites. Together they are worth EUR 3m to 5m by 2028.
  • Where the rest comes from: segment mix, partly offset by central costs and Veterinary Services normalising.
  • The leverage is asymmetric. A 1-point organic shortfall costs about 2.8% of EBITA for two to three quarters. Upside volume is mostly reinvested.
  • EBITDA margin will rise faster than EBITA margin, because capitalised development has doubled. The monitoring line must be segment adjusted EBITA, not EBITDA.

The cost structure explains the low leverage.

  • FY2025, as % of revenue: raw materials and merchandise 31.1% (variable), personnel 25.5% and other external 19.2% (sticky in the short run, but grown deliberately by each unit), D&A 8.9%.
  • Thirteen people at headquarters. Manufacturing is outsourced.
  • Gross margin held at 68.9% for two years. There is no leverage at gross level, and I hold 68.5% to 69.5% throughout.
  • What is left is opex. In my base, personnel and external costs grow about 10.5% a year against revenue of about 11.5%. The opex ratio falls only about 0.4 points over four years.

The record of incremental margins is consistent and low:

  • 19% over 2023 to 2025.
  • 22% in the first nine months of 2026.
  • Q3 2026: revenue +15%, adjusted EBITA +14%.
  • MedTech's swing from −4% to +15% organic lifted its margin by only 70 basis points in Q3 (210 in Q2).

Peers place Vimian with the decentralised compounders, not with installed-base businesses:

  • AddLife: 7% organic drop-through in Q3 2026.
  • Indutrade: 21%, achieved through pricing.
  • IDEXX: 36% at operating level, from an installed base.

Bridge of the group margin from FY2026 to FY2028 (+62 basis points; my arithmetic):

  • Diagnostics recovery: +29
  • MedTech: +27
  • Central costs falling as a share of revenue (2.4% to 2.2%): +20
  • Specialty Pharma: +8
  • Veterinary Services normalising: −12
  • Segment mix (growth shifts towards lower-margin segments and bolt-ons): about −10

Fixed-cost leverage proper (the field force and Diagnostics sites) is about half of the MedTech and Diagnostics lines. The rest is mix.

The EBITDA trap.

  • Capitalised R&D and software was EUR 7.7m in nine months of 2026 against 3.6m. D&A excluding PPA rises from 3.3% to 3.6% of revenue in 2026.
  • So from FY2025 to FY2026 the adjusted EBITA margin falls 30 basis points (24.8% to 24.5%) while my EBITDA margin rises 20 basis points (26.7% to 26.9%).
  • AlphaSense (T-P1) estimates that expensing 2025's EUR 6.0m would have cut the adjusted EBITA margin from 24.8% to 23.4%.
  • Part of the +170 basis points of EBITDA margin that consensus models for FY2025 to FY2027 can come from capitalisation rather than leverage.
  • For the third set: monitor segment adjusted EBITA. An EBITDA "beat" with a flat EBITA margin is not operating leverage.

Against the last four to six years:

  • Revenue rose 71% from 2022 to 2025 (281 to 425) while the adjusted EBITA margin fell from 26.1% to 24.8%. It falls again to 24.5% in 2026.
  • 2021 to 2025 was a period of negative leverage: growth investment, iM3's lower margin, Diagnostics and a shrinking US orthopaedic business.
  • The next four years turn positive but weakly. Margin contributes about 10% of my EBITA growth from FY2026 to FY2028, against about 60% from organic volume at a constant margin and about 30% from bolt-ons (+EUR 20m, +3m and +10m of the +33m; my arithmetic).

Downside sensitivity (my arithmetic, FY2027 scale):

  • A 1-point organic shortfall is about EUR 5.4m of revenue and EUR 3.7m of gross profit. With opex unchanged for two to three quarters, that is about −2.8% of EBITA and −4% to −5% of EPS.
  • After a year of hiring restraint the effect falls to about −1.3%.
  • About EUR 5m to 10m of discretionary spending (campaigns, events, new-country set-up, hiring) can be flexed, at the cost of growth.

Skew and cone for the FY2027 adjusted EBITA margin:

  • Two-sided. My 80% band is 24.3% to 25.4%.
  • Upside: US orthopaedic volumes recovering on the fixed field force, and share gains while DePuy Synthes separates (EUR 2m to 3m).
  • Downside: central costs, Veterinary Services normalising faster, and price-war spill-over into Specialty Pharma.

Where I differ from the first set. The first set (C-53) put the 2027 margin change at +30 to +60 basis points. My bottom-up gives +30, the low end. Central costs and Veterinary Services normalising absorb most of the Diagnostics recovery. I also put Diagnostics at 12% in 2027, not 12% to 15%.

P-02

After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

D5037th percentile

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: financial leverage is moderate and falls steadily, but it falls through EBITDA growth, not repayment. Cash interest therefore does not fall in euros.

  • Leverage path: net debt to adjusted EBITDA, on FactSet's basis (including leases, excluding earn-outs), falls from about 1.6x in 2026 to about 1.0x in 2030. On the company's pro-forma basis it falls from about 2.0x to about 1.2x. That holds while Vimian spends EUR 70m to 80m a year on bolt-ons, because net debt stays between EUR 220m and 245m while EBITDA grows about 12% a year.
  • Cash interest: EUR 15.4m in 2025 and about EUR 13.5m in 2026, then flat at EUR 14m to 15m.
  • What drives the consensus fall in financial costs. Consensus models the gap between EBIT and pre-tax profit falling from about EUR 25m (FY2026) to about EUR 15m (FY2027). That fall is mostly the disappearance of about EUR 10m of non-cash contingent-consideration and currency charges in 2026, not lower interest. It is plausible, but not assured: those charges recur whenever acquired businesses beat their earn-out targets.
  • EPS effect: net financial items fall from about 20% of adjusted EBITA (2025 and 2026) to about 13% (2028). That adds about 4 points to FY2027 EPS growth on my basis, and 1 to 2 points a year after.
  • Balance sheet: strengthens steadily. Leverage is not the constraint on the case at EUR 60m to 120m a year of deals. It becomes the constraint above about EUR 150m a year, or if the 2030 target is pursued literally.

The base path (FactSet basis net debt, including leases, excluding earn-out liabilities; my model):
