History. FactSet net interest was 6.7 (2021), 11.1, 19.3, 25.4 and 21.4 (2025). It rose with the debt-funded roll-up and the 2022-23 rate rise. Gearing peaked at 4.3x in Q2 2022 and forced a SEK 1.5bn directed issue. The 2024 rights issue (SEK 1.6bn, about 65m shares at SEK 25) followed the USD 70m VOI settlement.

Why consensus has the deleveraging but not the deals.

  • DNB Carnegie forecasts net debt to EBITDA of 1.3x (2027e) and 0.6x (2028e). That implies almost no acquisitions. Consensus revenue also appears to include none.
  • If deals continue (my base), revenue and EBITDA rise against consensus and interest stays roughly flat.
  • On my model basis, EUR 65m a year of deals at 9x EBITDA, funded at an all-in cost of about 6% of net debt, is accretive by only 1% to 2% of EPS a year. It is about neutral after PPA amortisation.
  • The deals do not change the EPS case. They change the revenue line and the capital employed.

The balancing loop inside financial items.

  • iM3's dental business beat its targets, so the earn-out was revalued up: EUR 4.5m in nine months of 2026. The charge is non-deductible, so it also lifted the tax rate to 35% in Q1 and Q3.
  • The better acquired units perform, the higher the financial items and the tax rate. Profit leaks to sellers for two to four years.
  • I carry EUR 4.5m a year of non-cash items from 2027, not zero. Consensus appears to carry close to zero.
  • Disagreement with the first set (C-76): it framed the fall in financial items as rate-sensitive. The fragile part is the assumption that contingent-consideration charges stop, not the interest rate.

Rates and refinancing:

  • Half of the EUR 150m bond is swapped to fixed. About EUR 200m of debt floats. A 1-point rate rise costs about EUR 2.0m of pre-tax profit, about 1.8% of FY2027 model-basis pre-tax profit.
  • The bond is a bullet due 13 May 2028, with no extension option (T-P4). Refinancing at 25 to 50 basis points wider would cost EUR 0.4m to 0.75m a year. That is small at leverage near 1.5x.
  • The bank facility (EUR 330m) runs to May 2030.

Currency stress: disagreement with the first set. The first set (C-76, C-81) cited the Risks report: a 20% USD fall plus krona strength pushes leverage past 3.0x. I cannot reproduce that at today's leverage.

  • A 20% USD fall removes about EUR 4.6m of EBIT and about EUR 5m to 6m of EBITDA (10% moves EBIT by EUR 2.3m).
  • That takes company-basis leverage from about 1.9x to about 2.0x, far from 3.0x.
  • The stress was relevant to the 2022 structure, not today's.

The balance sheet:

  • Equity is about EUR 742m (consensus FY2026) and rises EUR 55m to 90m a year through retained earnings. There are no dividends.
  • Goodwill (EUR 590m in 2025) rises with each deal.
  • ROIC on my definition (NOPAT on adjusted EBITA, over equity plus net debt plus earn-outs) rises from about 8.5% (2026) to about 10.5% (2030). It moves slowly, because deals at 9x keep adding capital at about the cost of capital.

Equity issuance. Not in the base. I judge the chance of an equity raise over four years at about 15% to 20%. Triggers would be a platform deal, a literal pursuit of the 2030 target (about EUR 300m a year of deals; C-13), or a second VOI-type liability. The company states the target needs "no further capital injections".

Judgement: little of the cost base is cuttable in large cuts without breaking the decentralised model. What runs the other way:

  • Capitalised R&D and software: EUR 7.7m in nine months against 3.6m. The step-up keeps about EUR 4m to 5m a year out of 2026 opex. Amortisation catches up over three to five years. I raise D&A excluding PPA from EUR 14.1m (2025) to 17.5m (2026), 19.5m (2027) and 21.5m (2028), against consensus of 16.8m, 17.9m and 18.8m.
  • Recurring roll-up costs. About EUR 6m a year of IAC is a real operating cost of the model: stay-on bonuses of EUR 3.8m (2024) and 3.9m (2025) in Specialty Pharma alone, plus deal costs on five or more deals a year. I keep it inside my EBITDA.

The IAC path on the reported basis:

  • EUR 6.2m (2024)
  • EUR 14.7m (2025): 9.0 acquisition-related, 1.2 systems, 1.3 restructuring, the rest litigation and other
  • About EUR 11m (2026E; about 8m in nine months, derived)
  • EUR 9.0m (2027E), 8.5m (2028E), 8.5m (2029E), 9.0m (2030E)

The fall from 2025 is the end of VOI litigation costs and of the MedTech restructuring. It is not a cultural change.

Peer precedents show why Vimian's lever is small.

  • Vetoquinol: lifted gross margin 280 basis points to 74.8% in 2025, and to 77.1% in H1 2026. It used range simplification plus in-housing of an acquired parasiticide portfolio. That needed its own manufacturing. Vimian outsources most production, so the in-housing lever is largely absent outside Diagnostics.
  • Genus: its Value Acceleration Programme delivered £21m over two phases, on a centralised model.
  • Swedencare: lifted gross margin 380 basis points to 60.4% in Q2 2026, yet its operational EBITDA margin fell to 20.5% in Q3 on marketing costs. A gross gain was spent on opex.
  • The Nordic decentralised acquirers (Lifco, Addtech, Indutrade, AddLife): do not run central cost programmes either. Their margins move on price and mix. AddLife's organic drop-through was about 7% in Q3 2026.

Downturn flex. Campaigns, education events, travel, consultants, new-country set-up (Japan, Portugal), companion diagnostics and hiring can be slowed by EUR 5m to 10m a year (my judgement; no figure is disclosed). That roughly halves the short-run downside leverage in P-01 after two to three quarters, at the cost of the organic growth story.

For the model: no separate cost-saving line. The savings sit inside the segment margins in P-01. Treat any Capital Markets Day cost programme as upside of EUR 3m to 6m by 2028 only if it is sized and dated.

5e

Reinforcing feedback loops

2 answers
C-4752P-0552
C-47To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?D5242nd percentilefull answer under 4a Business quality
P-05

After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

C-5255th percentile

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: in this business, commercial feedback loops reinforce revenue while financial loops balance earnings and cash.

Reinforcing loops running now:

  • Cross-selling fed by acquisitions. Each bolt-on adds a product to the 17,000-clinic network. Cross-selling is about 18% of sales and about a third of Specialty Pharma's organic growth, with eight new initiatives in 2026.
  • Training-led adoption in MedTech.
  • The VetFamily rebate loop, extended by geography.
  • Effect: they support organic growth of 8% to 9% in 2027 and 7.5% to 8% after, without a market recovery. That is 2 to 3 points above the market every year, and it makes revenue the most predictable line in the model.

Balancing loops running now:

  1. The earn-out loop. The better acquired businesses perform, the more profit leaks to their sellers: through non-cash, non-deductible contingent-consideration revaluations (EUR 4.5m in nine months of 2026; tax rate 35% in Q1 and Q3), then through cash payments.
  2. The direct-sales working-capital loop. Converting distributors to direct sales raises gross margin, but ties up about 28 to 30 cents of working capital per euro of revenue. That keeps free cash flow at about 30% to 45% of EBITDA.
  3. The clinic price-volume loop, which caps US orthopaedics.
  4. Consolidation and software ecosystems shrinking VetFamily's pool in mature markets.
  5. Staff turnover paid for with recurring stay-on bonuses.

The key question: acquisition funding and working capital. At the current pace of EUR 70m to 80m a year, they are in equilibrium. They neither reinforce nor undermine each other.

  • Free cash flow of EUR 55m to 80m a year (2027-29) roughly matches acquisition cash.
  • Net debt stays flat in euros, and leverage falls only through EBITDA growth.
  • They start to undermine each other only if the deal pace steps up towards what the 2030 target needs, or if working capital rises another 2 to 3 points of revenue (EUR 11m to 16m) in a year.

The loops one by one.

1. Cross-selling and acquisitions (reinforcing; running; high persistence).

  • How it works: a bolt-on brings a product. Vimian sells it into the existing clinic base with no new customer-acquisition cost. The acquired unit grows organically. The return on the deal rises, which justifies the next deal.
  • Evidence: cross-selling supported about a third of Specialty Pharma's organic growth in 2024 and 2025 (Q4 2025 call). It is about 18% of group sales (Q3 2026). Eight new initiatives in 2026. Norfolk was bought explicitly to sell soft-tissue products to Movora's surgeons.
  • Precedent: the Nordic decentralised acquirers (Lifco, Addtech) compound through organic growth in acquired units.
  • What would stall it: the "loose collection of assets" critique (Jefferies, September 2026). Penetration per clinic is not disclosed.
  • Effect on the lines: I attribute about 2 to 3 points of annual organic growth to it. It keeps group organic growth near 8% when the market grows about 4% to 6% in value.

2. Training and installed instruments (reinforcing; running; slow).

  • How it works: trained surgeons stay on Movora's instrument sets. Consignment inventory fills clinics. Dental equipment pulls SANOS and other consumable reorders.
  • Evidence: 48,000 professionals educated in 2025 (annual report; the "65,000" figure used in the first set is unsupported). 1,300 veterinarians in Q3 2026. High retention and low churn in US orthopaedics through the downturn (2025 annual report).
  • Precedent: IDEXX's installed-base loop is the strong form, with about 80% recurring revenue. Vimian's is weaker, because implant volume depends on the owner's decision to operate.
  • Costs: near-term margin. DNB Carnegie (January 2025) flagged education and exhibitions as a MedTech margin drag.
  • Effect on the lines: MedTech organic growth of 7.5% to 8% a year in my base, with US orthopaedics flat to low single digit.

3. The VetFamily rebate loop (reinforcing in new markets, balancing in mature ones).

  • How it works: more members bring more supplier rebates, which means more value per member, which brings more members.
  • Evidence: membership went from 10,948 (2025) to 12,400 (Q3 2026), helped by Ireland (Vetsave, 150 clinics), Japan and Portugal.
  • What works against it in mature markets:
  • Corporate groups own 60% to 80% of UK practices, and acquired clinics leave independent buying groups.
  • Covetrus VetSuite has passed 4,000 practices with USD 150m of cumulative benefits, combining software with pharmacy fulfilment.
  • Chewy bought Modern Animal (USD 400m, May 2026) and aims at about 60 clinics.
  • Independents resisting corporate buyers (Jefferies) and the UK CMA transparency regime both help.
  • Effect on the lines: Veterinary Services organic growth of 9.5% (2027), easing to 8% by 2030. The segment margin normalises towards 28.5% to 29% as new countries consume margin.

4. The earn-out loop (balancing; running now).

  • How it works: a bolt-on is bought with an earn-out. If it beats its targets, the liability is revalued up through financial items. That charge is non-deductible, so the tax rate rises, and the cash goes to the seller.
  • Evidence:
  • 2025: EUR 9.8m of discounting and revaluation in financial items.
  • Nine months of 2026: discounting 3.2m plus probability adjustments 4.5m (dental).
  • Tax rate 35% in Q1 and Q3 2026.
  • The maximum payout rose from EUR 113.2m to EUR 142.1m. iM3's exceptional tranche can reach EUR 50m.
  • Effect on the lines:
  • EUR 4.5m a year of non-cash financial items in my base from 2027.
  • The adjusted tax rate stays 1 to 2 points above a clean rate.
  • A tail of cash outflow up to the cap.
  • Where it bites: EPS. The loop strengthens with deal pace and with the success of acquired dental businesses.

5. Direct sales and working capital (balancing; running now).

  • How it works: direct selling to thousands of clinics, broad implant and dental inventories, consignment stock, and acquisitions that bring their own working capital. Each euro of new revenue ties up more capital than under distribution.
  • Evidence:
  • Company-basis net working capital: EUR 96.6m (end-2025, 23% of revenue) to 114.1m (Q2) to 116.4m (Q3, 25%).
  • FactSet basis: 27.6% to 29.5% of trailing revenue.
  • Inventory EUR 98.3m, receivables EUR 67.5m.
  • Days sales outstanding 49 to 51.
  • The "normalise in Q3" promise was not met.
  • Effect on the lines:
  • At 28.5% (FactSet basis, year-end), working capital absorbs EUR 15m to 21m a year in my base.
  • Free cash flow is 30% of my EBITDA in 2026 and rises only to 44% by 2030.
  • A Q2-2026-sized build (EUR 20m) costs about 0.15x of leverage and EUR 1.2m of interest a year.

6. Clinic price-volume (balancing; slow; running).

  • How it works: clinics raise prices 10% to 12%. Visits fall (18 consecutive negative US quarters to Q3 2026). Elective surgery is deferred: 85% of professionals see owners decline care costing USD 1,000 or less (AAHA, September 2026). Implant volumes fall.
  • Effect on the lines: I assume no US orthopaedic market recovery before 2028, so MedTech organic growth stays at 7% to 8%. A recovery would be upside of EUR 2m to 3m of EBITA a year.

7. Price-war spill-over (balancing; latent). See P-04. It acts through Specialty Pharma volume first, then price.

8. Talent (balancing; running).

  • Staff turnover was 19% in 2025 (12.6% in 2021).
  • Stay-on bonuses (EUR 3.9m in Specialty Pharma) recur.
  • A former controller says founder departures after earn-outs are "natural".
  • This is why I treat about EUR 6m a year of IAC as recurring operating cost.

9. Acquisition capacity (reinforcing; running at the current pace).

  • How it works: EBITDA growth lowers leverage, which creates capacity, which allows more deals and more EBITDA.
  • How it reverses: when organic growth misses and leverage is already high. Swedencare reached 3.0x in 2026 with organic misses and a CEO exit. Vimian itself was at 4.3x in 2022, followed by a SEK 1.5bn directed issue, and raised SEK 1.6bn in 2024 after VOI.

The funding equilibrium in numbers (my base, EUR m):

Line FY2026E FY2027E FY2028E FY2029E FY2030E
Free cash flow (before acquisitions) 38.5 54.8 65.4 78.6 92.1
Acquisitions and earn-outs, cash (in the model, net of acquired working capital) about 85 (80) about 77 (70) about 77 (70) about 77 (70) about 77 (70)
Working capital absorbed 19.9 15.6 18.7 20.1 21.3
Net debt, FactSet basis 222 237 242 233 211

The loops balance at this pace. To reach the 2030 ambition, the deal pace would need to roughly triple (C-13). That would break the equilibrium within two years: leverage would rise about 0.5x a year with no change in cash conversion.

Net effect on the main lines:

  • Revenue: positive skew from the reinforcing commercial loops.
  • EBITA: neutral.
  • EPS: dampened by the earn-out and talent loops.
  • Free cash flow: dampened by the working-capital loop.

C-71

How conservative is current guidance for the next one to three years against the last one to five years, adjusted for the difference in input change forces versus the past?

F141st percentile

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: the only explicit guidance, the 2030 target, is far from conservative. It implies adjusted EBITA growth of about 23% a year from 2025 (about 26% from 2026), against about 10% a year delivered in 2023-2025. That is in an external environment less supportive than 2021-2022 (clinic volumes down eighteen quarters, against organic growth of 16.5% in 2021) and with internal change forces stronger but not strong enough to double the growth rate. Near-term informal guidance is conservative in tone. Taken together, guidance is aggressive on the mid-term and modestly conservative on the next two quarters.

  • Historical rate (company adjusted EBITA): about EUR 87m (2023), 95.2m (2024), 105.3m (2025). About 10% a year (DNB Carnegie also says 10%). Adjusted EBITA margin down 150 basis points over the period.
  • Change-force adjustment:
  • External forces are weaker than in the period when the target was set (C-10). Volume is down and competition is more promotional.
  • Internal forces are stronger: new management, MedTech fixed, an active M&A pipeline. They have lifted organic growth by about 3 to 5 points.
  • Even granting that, the organic contribution to EBITA growth is about 9% to 11% a year (8% to 9% organic revenue plus 30 to 60 basis points of margin).
  • The remaining 12 to 15 points a year would have to come from acquisitions at a pace about three times the current one (C-13).
  • Near term: US orthopaedics at "low single digit" is conservative relative to the momentum MedTech has shown. The Veterinary Services margin at "about 28.5%" is realistic. The Diagnostics improvement "from 2027" is reasonable. No figure to beat beyond tone.

Case simplicity

7 answers
Judgement52C-7925D-0276D-0342D-0450D-0562D-0646
Group judgement

Risks

D5246th percentile

Layer 3 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The financial forecasts are predictable where it matters most to the operating case and unpredictable where it matters most to the share price. Revenue and adjusted EBITA can be forecast within about ±4% and ±7% for the next 18 months, because growth is diffuse, demand is chronic and paid in cash, the base case uses only strengths the company has demonstrated, and industry shocks reach the numbers weakly and late. EPS and free cash flow carry about ±12% and ±25%, because a small cluster of items below EBITA, most of them tied to acquisition accounting and to one business (dental), stands between EBITA and the shareholder, and the company offers no forward view of any of them.

How the seven answers fit together:

  1. The earlier work is sound in substance (D-01). Its conclusions agree. Its presentation would mislead in three places, the most serious being the model's return of +45%, which is +15% to +34% on a single earnings basis.
  2. Our base asks nothing new of the company (D-02). It needs organic growth near 8%, bolt-ons at the present pace and a margin that edges up. Consensus needs operating leverage the company has never shown. The 2030 target needs capabilities the company does not have.
  3. The level of the forecasts rests on many drivers; the improvement on a few (D-03). Dental links growth, inventory, earn-out charges and tax, and pulls EBITA one way and EPS and cash the other within the 18-month window.
  4. The cone widens down the profit and loss account, and a damper narrows it at the bottom (D-04). Earn-out charges rise when acquired businesses beat and reverse when they miss, so reported EPS is a poor guide to operating health in both directions.
  5. Almost everything can be tracked (D-05). Three reporting events fall in the next six months, and each of our differences with consensus can be checked against a published number on 11 February 2027.
  6. The company's own statements trace to segment EBITA and no further (D-06). What it says is worth about 1% of EPS; what it does not say is worth 5% to 10%.
  7. The share price is the company's own (D-07). Nothing in the sector will carry it or cushion it.

The steer to the Consumer.

  • Rest the memo's forecast confidence on adjusted EBITA. It is the line that is both predictable and uncontaminated: EBITDA is flattered by capitalised development, and EPS is distorted by basis and by earn-out accounting.
  • State every EPS figure with its basis, and correct the return calculation for basis before quoting it.
  • Describe the risk as one of conversion and of price, not of the business. The plausible operating outcomes for FY2027 lie in a narrow band. The plausible share-price outcomes do not, because the multiple has risen about a third in six months on the company's own news, with earnings estimates unchanged, and our base sits below the consensus margin that the multiple anticipates.
  • Use the dated tests. The Capital Markets Day (24 November 2026) could narrow the EPS and cash cone if it brings a working-capital or return target and a view of the lines below EBITA. The fourth-quarter report (11 February 2027) tests inventory, the year-end earn-out reassessment and the full-year cash conversion. The first-quarter report (late April 2027) tests the Diagnostics margin and the tax rate against demanding comparisons.

What would raise or lower the score. It would rise with a cash and returns discipline adopted publicly, a fall in inventory, and a second year of the new team's delivery. It would fall with a large or equity-funded acquisition, a second quarter of cash flow like Q2 2026, or any disturbance to the licensing income.

How narrow is the business perimeter (number of countries and business lines), how is it changing, and what does that mean for predictability?

F259th percentile

Judgement: the perimeter is wide and widening. It covers four segments with very different economics (Diagnostics at 7% to 14% margins, Specialty Pharma at 30%), about 80 markets, 59 acquisitions since 2015, over 17,000 clinics, and new adjacencies in 2026 (soft-tissue surgery, Italian pet cremation, Japan, Portugal). For predictability this is negative at group level: many small moving parts, campaign and outbreak volatility, currency translation, and an acquisition stream that changes the base each quarter. Segment disclosure is good enough to model, but forecast error at the EPS line will be wide.

  • Specialty Pharma: US nutrition campaign phasing can swing quarterly organic growth by about 4 to 5 points (14% against 9% underlying in Q2; 7% against 11% in Q3).
  • Diagnostics: swings on livestock disease outbreaks.
  • MedTech: has the US elective cycle.
  • Currency: moves reported growth 1 to 5 points a quarter.
  • Acquisitions: add 4 to 10 points a year and an earn-out stream.
  • Reported EPS: carries four bases and recurring adjustments.
  • Brokers' FY2026 adjusted EPS spans 0.089 to 0.140.
  • Mitigant: diversification lowers the chance of a single-point catastrophe. No customer is above 10%. Segment disclosure every quarter (revenue, organic growth, adjusted EBITA by segment) lets the case be tracked.
  • For the model:
  • Build by segment.
  • Separate organic, acquired and currency growth.
  • Use annual rather than quarterly precision.
  • Widen the EPS confidence band to about ±10% for FY2027.
D-02

To what extent does past and present fundamental delivery make the expected change plausible, and to what extent does the thesis rely only on strengths the business demonstrably has, rather than on strengths it would need to acquire?

C+7667th percentile

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: our base forecast relies almost entirely on strengths Vimian has already demonstrated, and that is the main reason it is plausible. Consensus relies on one strength the company has not demonstrated in any year since listing (operating leverage). The company's own 2030 target relies on strengths it would need to acquire (a deal pace three times the present one, or equity). The plausibility of the expected change therefore falls in a clear order: our base, then consensus, then the target.

The expected change, stated plainly. Against 2021 to 2025, the base case expects four things over the next 18 months to four years:

  1. Organic growth staying near 8% without a market recovery.
  2. The adjusted EBITA margin ceasing to fall and rising about 30 basis points a year.
  3. Less profit lost between EBITA and net income (reported net income rising from 30% of adjusted EBITA to above 40%).
  4. Bolt-ons funded from cash flow and debt, with no new equity.

Each element tested against demonstrated delivery:

Element of the expected change Demonstrated? Evidence Reliance on a strength still to be acquired
Organic growth of about 8% without market help Yes, repeatedly Above the market every year since 2021; 9%, 12% and 11% in Q1 to Q3 2026 while US visits fell 2% to 3%; about 8% a year on a two-year view None. Movora holds about 40% of the North American veterinary orthopaedic implant market, twice its nearest rival (management, May 2025), and grew through a falling market
Immunotherapy holding against cheaper itch drugs Yes, so far Specialty Pharma grew 11% underlying in Q3 2026 with its margin up to 30.3%; vets prescribe a fast-acting drug and start allergy testing together; 29% of vets decline to prescribe Zenrelia because of its label (Morgan Stanley, October 2026) Small. Large in consensus
Diagnostics margin from about 9% to 12% to 14% Not demonstrated 13.8% in Q1 2026 fell to 7.2% in Q3; the companion-animal model competes with IDEXX's installed base Yes: scale in companion testing, which Vimian does not have. Worth about 30 basis points of group margin by 2028
Lower leakage below EBITA Partly IAC are falling as litigation ends. Earn-out charges and the tax rate have not improved (tax 31% in nine months of 2026) Moderate. It needs earn-out charges to fall, which I now assume only in part (D-01)
Self-funded bolt-ons at about 9x EBITDA Yes About EUR 80m to 95m a year in 2024 to 2026 with leverage steady at about 2.0x against a 3.0x ceiling; Norfolk at about 9x None at the present pace. The earlier equity raises (2022, 2024) followed leverage of 4.3x and the VOI settlement, neither of which is in the base
Cash conversion improving No Operating cash flow about half of adjusted EBITDA in nine months of 2026; inventory +EUR 18m None, because the base assumes no release of working capital
The new team delivering Yes, on revenue only Three consecutive beats; MedTech from -4% to +15% organic in five quarters. Four quarters is a short record, and the two segment heads have 1 and 7 months in role Margin and cash delivery are unproven

The decisive comparison is with the decentralised acquirers that have done what Vimian is now expected to do. The research question was whether any decentralised roll-up has lifted margin and cash conversion while stepping up acquisitions, and what distinguished those that did.

  • Those that did (Lifco, Indutrade, Addtech) share one feature Vimian lacks: an explicit return-on-working-capital discipline imposed on every subsidiary. Addtech reported a return on working capital of 82% (twelve months to September 2026). Indutrade's working capital turns 4.8 times a year with cash conversion above 130%. Vimian's net working capital is 25% of revenue on its own basis, and no return or working-capital measure appears in its incentives (70% absolute adjusted EBITA, 30% revenue).
  • Those that did not: AddLife (a working-capital drain in 2026, and organic EBITA growth of only SEK 5m on SEK 75m of organic sales in Q3 2026) and Swedencare (organic misses, leverage of about 3x, and its chief executive leaving three months after its first Capital Markets Day).
  • The animal-health margin success, Vetoquinol, was achieved by bringing manufacturing in-house, a lever Vimian largely lacks because it outsources production.
  • The inference. The strength Vimian would need to acquire to deliver consensus margins and a cash release together is a central cash-and-returns discipline. The first sign of one is the chief financial officer's release from his second role in October 2026. The Capital Markets Day is the first occasion on which a working-capital or return target could be announced. If one is, it would be the single most important change to this answer.

What makes the base case robust to this gap. The second set deliberately built the base without the undemonstrated strengths: no working-capital release, no market recovery, a margin gain of which fixed-cost leverage is about half, and Diagnostics recovering more slowly than management says. About 60% of the growth in EBITA from FY2026 to FY2028 is organic volume at a constant margin, about 30% is bolt-ons and about 10% is margin. The two largest parts are the two things the company has always delivered.

Where I think the earlier sets were too generous or too harsh:

  • Too harsh on receivables and on the "normalise in Q3" statement (D-01, item 5).
  • Slightly too generous on the Diagnostics margin. The comparison in Q1 2027 is with 13.8%, a quarter helped by livestock disease testing (bluetongue and avian influenza). A year-on-year fall in that quarter is more likely than not, in the first quarter in which management has said the margin improves. The full-year path of 12% is still reasonable.
D-03In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?D4237th percentilefull answer under 2a Internal risks
D-04

How wide is the cone of plausible outcomes over the next 18 months, how skewed is it, and so how predictable is the case?

C-5053rd percentile

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the cone is narrow at the top of the profit and loss account and widens steadily down it. Over the next 18 months I put revenue within about ±4% of our base, adjusted EBITA within about ±7%, model-basis and reported EPS within about ±12%, and free cash flow within about ±25%, each as a band I expect to hold about two times in three. The skew is positive on revenue, slightly negative on EBITA, and negative on free cash flow. The range of reported EPS is narrower than the lines above it would suggest, because the earn-out charge moves against operating surprises. The case is therefore predictable as an operating business and only moderately predictable as an earnings and cash stream.

The cone for FY2027, the year that sits inside the 18-month window (base after my input changes; bands are my judgement):

Line Base FY2027 Band I expect to hold about two times in three Skew What sets the width
Revenue EUR 536m ±4% (about EUR 515m to 557m) Positive Timing and size of bolt-ons (each EUR 65m of deals is about EUR 25m of annual revenue); currency (a 10% move in the US dollar is EUR 15.7m)
Organic growth 8.5% 6.5% to 10% Slightly negative Laps 12% and 11%; US orthopaedics; the allergy franchise against cheaper and longer-acting itch drugs
Adjusted EBITA EUR 133m (24.8%) ±7% (about EUR 124m to 142m) Slightly negative One point of organic growth is about 2.8% of EBITA for two to three quarters; Diagnostics; central costs; licensing income
EPS, model basis 0.141 ±12% (about 0.124 to 0.158) Slightly negative The lines above, plus earn-out charges (EUR 10.4m for a 10% change in the underlying measures), the tax rate (one point is about 1.4% of EPS) and interest (one point of rates is about EUR 2.0m)
EPS, after-PPA basis (closest to consensus) 0.111 about -8% to +6% Balanced Narrower than the model basis, because this basis adds back the non-cash charges
Free cash flow before acquisitions EUR 55m ±25% (about EUR 41m to 69m) Negative, with one positive option One point of revenue held in working capital is about EUR 5.4m, a tenth of the year's free cash flow

Why the cone is narrow at the top.

  • Demand is chronic, consumable and paid in cash, and has grown through 18 consecutive quarters of falling US clinic visits.
  • The three sources of quarterly noise (retail campaign phasing, livestock disease outbreaks, the quarterly timing of licensing income) wash out within a year.
  • Acquisitions are within management's control, and the company has never failed to deploy capital.

Why it widens below EBITA, and the one feature that narrows it again.

  • Four EPS bases exist, and brokers' FY2026 "adjusted" EPS runs from 0.089 to 0.140. A reader who does not fix the basis faces a range of 57% before any forecasting risk.
  • The history is of revenue arriving and EPS not: FY2026 consensus EPS fell 15.1% in twelve months during three consecutive revenue and EBITA beats. FY2027 consensus sales rose 7% while EPS fell 7%.
  • The earn-out mechanism is a damper. A 10% beat by acquired businesses against their earn-out measures adds EUR 10.4m to the liability, charged through financial items with no tax relief. A 10% miss reverses it, as income. A broad 5% beat therefore costs about EUR 5m, roughly 7% of model-basis net income, in the year it is recognised (my arithmetic). The effect is that reported and model-basis EPS move less than EBITA in both directions. AddLife showed the reverse case in Q3 2026: a SEK 32m write-back lifted profit after tax 56% while trading was only in line.
  • Two consequences follow. First, reported EPS is a poor signal of operating health in either direction: a strong EPS print can mean acquired businesses are missing. Second, the true dispersion of the business is better read from EBITA and from the after-PPA EPS.

The skew, line by line.

  • Revenue: positive. Consensus appears to contain no unannounced deals (Jefferies says so explicitly, 28 October 2026). Deals add to revenue when announced.
  • Organic growth: slightly negative. Three things can only subtract in 2027: the comparison with 12% and 11%; a US orthopaedic market that management says has not changed; and a new competitor to immunotherapy's convenience, Zoetis' 75-day Cytopoint Plus (licensed 11 November 2026, after the first set was written). One thing can add: US orthopaedic volume on the field force installed in January 2026.
  • EBITA margin: slightly negative. Short-run operating leverage is asymmetric: a shortfall costs margin at once, while extra volume is mostly reinvested. Against that, EUR 5m to 10m of discretionary spending can be held back within two to three quarters.
  • Free cash flow: negative, with one positive option. Inventory is still rising, Norfolk adds working capital from November 2026, and year-end earn-out payments are irregular (EUR 29.4m was paid in nine months of 2026). The option is a release of dental and orthopaedic stock: EUR 8m was released in one quarter in 2023.

The tails: events that sit outside the band. None is likely in 18 months. Each would move EPS by more than the band.

Event Effect My view of likelihood over 18 months
Loss or regulatory disruption of the Bova licensing income About -EUR 6m to -8m of EBITA; about -8% of EPS Low (it has run through three years and a change of segment head)
A large or equity-funded acquisition in pursuit of the 2030 target Changes leverage, share count and the nature of the case Low to moderate (about one in seven)
A second acquired liability of the VOI kind Cash and equity; VOI cost USD 70m before recovery Low
US tariffs on imported medical devices About EUR 1.5m to 4m of EBITA before mitigation Low to moderate
A fall of 10% in the US dollar against the euro About -EUR 15.7m of revenue and -EUR 2.3m of EBIT; unhedged Moderate
Full payment of iM3's optional earn-out tranche Up to EUR 50m of cash, about 0.3x of leverage; payable only on exceptional growth Low within 18 months
A write-down of dental or discontinued orthopaedic inventory A few million euros, taken as IAC Low to moderate

How the past dispersion compares. The wide outcomes of 2021 to 2025 came from single events: the collapse of organic growth from 16.5% to 3.7% in 2022 (Diagnostics fell 25% as pandemic testing ended), the USD 70m settlement in 2023, the US orthopaedic decline of 2025 and a chief executive removed. The base contains no such event, the business is larger and more spread, and leverage is half its 2022 peak. I therefore judge the operating cone for the next 18 months to be narrower than the record of the last five years implies. Peers show that single shocks still reach diversified acquirers: Lifco lost a customer worth 5% of group revenue (October 2026), and Zoetis cut its 2026 revenue guidance in August 2026.

Our base against consensus within the cone (my judgement). Consensus FY2027 EBITDA of EUR 153m sits about 4% above our base including unannounced deals and about 6% above it without them, near the upper edge of our band. I put the chance that FY2027 EBITDA on the brokers' basis comes in below today's consensus at about 60% to 65%.

The share price cone is wider than the earnings cone, and this is where predictability of the case, as opposed to the forecasts, is weakest. The first set put the fall to a fair trough multiple at about 27%, and about 40% in a company-specific problem (C-83). On one consistent basis the return at the guideline fair multiple is +15% to +34% (D-01). The stock moves almost entirely on its own news (D-07).

D-05

How helpful is the company's disclosure on operating momentum and key drivers of the thesis to monitor the case over about the next six months?

B6280th percentile

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: disclosure is good enough to monitor the operating case closely over the next six months, and better than the earlier sets allowed for the balance sheet and the below-EBITA lines. The company reports segment revenue, organic growth and adjusted EBITA every quarter, and also discloses, quarterly, the items that matter most to our differences with consensus: working capital by component, the split of financial items, contingent consideration, capitalised development, central costs and the related-party licensing income. Three things it does not disclose limit the monitoring: price and volume within organic growth, orthopaedics separately from dental, and the cost attached to the licensing income. Three reporting events fall inside the six months, so the case will be tested early and often.

The calendar to mid-May 2027:

Date Event What it can settle
24 November 2026 First Capital Markets Day Status of the 2030 target; segment margin and growth ambitions; an acquisition envelope; whether a cash-conversion, working-capital or return target is adopted
11 February 2027 Q4 and full-year 2026 First full year under the new team; the year-end balance sheet; the year-end reassessment of earn-outs; full-year cash conversion
About mid-March 2027 2026 annual report The notes: related-party transactions, the maturity and sensitivity of contingent consideration, capitalised development and useful lives, the tax reconciliation
Late April 2027 Q1 2027 Diagnostics margin against 13.8%; organic growth against 9%; the tax rate; licensing income against a high Q1 2026

Peers report before or alongside Vimian each quarter (IDEXX and Zoetis in early February and early May; Swedencare within days), and Vetsource and Morgan Stanley publish clinic-visit and veterinary survey data in between. Vimian itself gives no monthly data and no numerical guidance.

What to monitor, where it is disclosed, and the reading that would change the forecast:

Thesis driver Disclosed Frequency Reading in the next six months that confirms the base Reading that would break it
Group organic growth Yes, by segment Quarterly Q4 2026 about 10%; Q1 2027 at least 9% Below 7% in either quarter

Buying-group membership Yes Quarterly Above 12,400 A fall

What is missing, and how much it matters:

  • Price, volume and mix within organic growth (matters a good deal). Without it, the first sign of pressure from corporate buyers or from the dermatology price war would be a fall in gross margin, a quarter or two late.
  • Orthopaedics separately from dental, in revenue, margin and inventory (matters a good deal). It prevents anyone from telling whether the field sales force is earning its cost, and whether the inventory is dental growth stock or unsold orthopaedic items.
  • The cost attached to the licensing income, and the contract's term (matters as a tail risk).
  • The targets behind the 25 earn-outs (matters for cash timing). Only the total, the maximum and the sensitivity are given.
  • Companion-animal against livestock diagnostics (matters for one margin).
  • The currency effect in some quarters (not given for Q3 2026) and the incentive plan's hurdles.
  • A consistent cash-conversion measure. The company has used three definitions (C-60). Use operating cash flow over adjusted EBITDA.

Where I differ from the first set. The first set (C-60) called communication "unhelpful below EBITA". For monitoring purposes that is too harsh. The split of financial items, the contingent-consideration roll-forward and its sensitivity, the related-party amount and the working-capital components are all published quarterly, which is more than Swedencare or AddLife give. What is weak is explanation and guidance, not data. The practical point for the Consumer: every one of our differences with consensus can be checked against a published number on 11 February 2027.

A caution on how to read the next two prints. Because of the dental effect (D-03) and the earn-out damper (D-04), judge each quarter in this order: segment organic growth; adjusted EBITA (not EBITDA, which capitalised development flatters); inventory and operating cash flow; and only then EPS, on a stated basis.

D-06

How clearly does what the company says it will achieve trace into specific operational drivers, onto identifiable revenue, cost and margin lines (the key fundamental milestones and measures), and through to earnings per share; and how clear is the external transmission from value-chain dynamics to earnings per share?

D4628th percentile

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the trace from what the company says to earnings per share is clear for the first two-thirds of its length and breaks in the last third. What management says maps cleanly onto operational drivers and onto segment revenue and adjusted EBITA, which are reported every quarter. From segment EBITA to EPS the trace is poor: eight lines intervene, the company says almost nothing about them, and EPS is quoted on four bases. The more important weakness is one of proportion. Each thing the company does say is worth about 1% of EPS. The things worth 5% to 10% of EPS have no company statement behind them at all. The external transmission, from the value chain to EPS, is weak and slow for industry dynamics and direct and mechanical for currency and interest rates.

The internal trace, statement by statement (EPS effects are my arithmetic, on FY2027 model-basis EPS of about 0.141):

What the company says Operational driver Line it lands on Milestone that can be measured Approximate effect on FY2027 EPS
Diagnostics margin improves "from 2027" Companion-animal test volumes on two consolidated sites Diagnostics adjusted EBITA margin, 9.4% to 12% on about EUR 35m of revenue Segment margin each quarter About +EUR 1.3m of EBITA; about +1%
Veterinary Services margin normalises "around 28.5%" Set-up costs in Japan, Portugal and the US Segment margin, 29.4% to 28.8% Segment margin each quarter About -EUR 0.5m; under -0.5%
US orthopaedics at "low single digits"; field sales force US implant volumes on a fixed sales cost MedTech margin, 25.2% to 25.6% Segment margin; commentary only on US orthopaedics About +EUR 0.8m; about +0.5%
"Central cost increases" Long-term incentive cost (about EUR 0.6m a quarter, non-cash), group functions Central costs, EUR 11.6m to 12.4m Reported quarterly About -EUR 0.8m; about -0.5%
Norfolk "enhancing earnings from 2027" Cross-selling to Movora's surgeons About EUR 6m of MedTech revenue at a margin below the group's Not separately reported Under +1%
Capex "about 4% of revenue" Capitalised development and software Cash flow; depreciation later Reported quarterly None on EPS; about EUR 8m of cash against consensus capex
An "active" acquisition phase Bolt-ons at about 9x EBITDA Revenue (+EUR 25m for each EUR 65m of deals), EBITDA, PPA, interest, earn-outs Announced deal by deal +1% to +2% on the model basis; about nil after PPA
"Maintaining double-digit organic growth" Not broken down by price, volume or segment Revenue; EBITA Reported; our base is 8.5% Each point is about 2.8% of EBITA at first, about 1.3% once costs adjust
Adjusted EBITA above EUR 300m by 2030 None given None None Cannot be traced

What the table shows.

  • Down to segment EBITA the trace is good. Every near-term statement has a segment line and a quarterly number against which it can be checked. The key fundamental measures are identifiable: segment organic growth, segment adjusted EBITA margin, central costs, buying-group membership.
  • The statements are small. Taken together, the specific statements in the first six rows move FY2027 EPS by about 1% net. They describe the margin bridge accurately, and the margin bridge is not what drives EPS.
  • The large items have no statement. Four things move EPS by 5% or more and are covered by no company statement other than an "ambition":
  • the rate of organic growth itself (two points is about 5% of EBITA in the short run);
  • the contingent-consideration charge (EUR 10.4m for a 10% change in the underlying measures; about 7% of net income for a 5% change);
  • the tax rate (29% in 2025, 31% in nine months of 2026; each point is about 1.4% of EPS);
  • the licensing income (about 8% of EPS if lost).
  • The one numerical target has no bridge. The 2030 target has three qualifiers and no building blocks. On the first set's arithmetic it needs about three times the present deal pace or new equity. It cannot be traced to any driver, and both earlier sets rightly treat it as non-information.

Where the trace breaks: segment EBITA to EPS. Between the segment totals and EPS sit central costs; the difference between EBITDA and EBITA, which capitalised development widens (EUR 7.7m capitalised in nine months of 2026 against EUR 3.6m, amortised over five to fifteen years); PPA amortisation (about EUR 26m); IAC (about EUR 11m, of which about EUR 6m recurs); net financial items, of which up to half is non-cash; a tax rate distorted by non-deductible items; minorities; and the share count. The company explains each after the event and gives a forward view of none. The sell-side fills the gap with assumptions of its own, which is why FY2026 "adjusted" EPS runs from 0.089 to 0.140 across six brokers.

Two features make the break worse than a lack of guidance.

  • The lines are not independent of operations. The better the acquired businesses trade, the higher the financial charge and the tax rate (D-03, D-04). A reader cannot simply apply a constant deduction to EBITA.
  • EBITDA and EBITA are diverging. From FY2025 to FY2026 the adjusted EBITA margin falls about 30 basis points while the EBITDA margin on the brokers' basis rises about 20. Consensus is expressed in EBITDA. A statement by the company about "margin" will be about adjusted EBITA.

The external transmission, from value-chain dynamics to EPS:

External dynamic Where it reaches Vimian Size Clarity and speed
US clinic visits falling (3.2% in Q3 2026) and clinic prices rising 10% to 12% a year US orthopaedic implant volumes only, about an eighth of revenue Group organic growth has run 11 to 14 points above visit growth; a recovery is worth EUR 2m to 3m of EBITA a year Weak and lagged. In 2008 to 2011, same-store clinic volumes took eleven quarters to turn positive
Price war in itch drugs (Zoetis, Elanco, Merck) and longer-acting injectables The relative cost and convenience of immunotherapy, so Specialty Pharma's volume first and price second Not visible so far (11% underlying growth). A two-point price shortfall in Specialty Pharma is about EUR 4m of EBITA, about 5% of EPS Indirect and slow. Would show first as underlying growth below 8%
Corporate clinic groups and central tenders Price of implants, dental consumables and diagnostics; the pool of independent clinics for VetFamily A slow ratchet; tender discounts of about 15% on implants (expert) Slow; not measurable from disclosure
Online pharmacy and practice software (Chewy, Covetrus) VetFamily's members more than Vimian's own products Small in 18 months Slow
US dollar against the euro (38% of revenue; unhedged) Reported revenue, and EBIT to a lesser degree 10% is EUR 15.7m of revenue and EUR 2.3m of EBIT, about 2% of EPS Direct, mechanical, immediate
Euro interest rates (about EUR 200m of floating debt) Financing cost One point is about EUR 2.0m, under 2% of EPS Direct, mechanical
US tariffs on imported medical devices MedTech cost of goods About EUR 1.5m to 4m before mitigation Clear in size; uncertain in timing
Swedish krona against the euro The share price, not the earnings One for one on the SEK value of euro earnings Direct

What the external table shows. Industry dynamics reach Vimian's earnings weakly and late, through one product line and through relative prices. That is the financial expression of the first set's finding that the niches are insulated, and it helps predictability: an industry shock of the Zoetis kind does not pass straight into Vimian's numbers. The two macro variables that do pass straight through, the dollar and interest rates, are easy to model and small (about 2% of EPS each for a plausible move). The exception to "small" is a dollar move for the top line: it can take two to five points off reported growth in a quarter, as in Q1 2026.

Where I differ from the first set. The first set (C-07) judged that macro "bites hardest at company level ... on EPS (interest and tax)". On the new detail I would put it differently: the interest-rate effect on EPS is small, and the larger below-EBITA swings come from the company's own earn-out accounting, not from macro. The second set (P-02) made the same correction, and I agree with it.

What would raise this score. A Capital Markets Day that gives a margin corridor by segment, an acquisition envelope, a cash-conversion or working-capital target, and a view of the tax rate and of earn-out payments would close most of the break. The invitation promises segment roadmaps. Nothing indicates that the below-EBITA lines will be covered.

What perception- or sentiment-driven risks are there that are sudden and high-magnitude, including events elsewhere in the sector, theme or market that could hit the share price whatever the company's own fundamentals?

F1813th percentile

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: perception-driven sudden risks are high and structurally likely:

  • a 58.4% controlling holder over a 40.3% float, with average daily trading of about USD 2.3m to 2.5m
  • no listed options
  • a share price up 44.5% in twelve months, at the 94th relative-strength percentile and the 75th percentile of its own five-year multiple range
  • a sector that has moved 21.5% in a day on a peer's print
  • a style ("complex roll-ups") the market sells first in risk-off phases
  • a market regime with Fragility at Double Underweight

The Capital Markets Day on 24 November 2026 is a dated perception event in either direction.

  • Controlling holder. A Fidelio placing or exit (it has lent shares; the ten largest hold 80%) would hit a thin float. So would the former Specialty Pharma head's 2.6m shares, or any governance headline.
  • Read-across:
  • Zoetis (-21.5% in a day, May 2026; Q3 organic -1%).
  • Swedencare (Q3 organic 2%, North America -4%, CEO leaving).
  • IDEXX's clinic-visit outlook.
  • "Sector capital outflows" took Vimian down 17% to February 2026 with nothing company-specific.
  • Positioning: after +40% in six months, holders are sitting on gains. The FfF notes "momentum underweight" and that breadth recoveries led by defensives are fragile. A rotation out of healthcare, or a small-cap risk-off leg, would hit Vimian disproportionately.
  • Capital Markets Day:
  • A re-based or re-dated 2030 target, or a signal of equity-funded growth, would be read negatively.
  • A credible margin and cash bridge would be read positively.
  • Mean target price (EUR 3.72) sits below the price (EUR 3.80). The median (EUR 4.05) is about 7% above.
  • Short vulnerability: none so far (short interest below 0.5%). The profile fits typical roll-up short theses: adjusted earnings, related-party items, a working capital build.

C-70

Beyond meeting or beating, what is the record, nature and behaviour of the company and its chief executive in upgrading guidance?

F188th percentile

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: the company has no record of upgrading guidance. Its multi-year record is of mid-term ambitions set high, missed and replaced:

  • a 35% margin
  • a 30% sales CAGR
  • EUR 200m adjusted EBITA by 2025, replaced by EUR 300m by 2030

Under the new chief executive, the behaviour has changed to conservative framing followed by beats. That is the "sandbagging precision" archetype, not the "upgrader" one. There is no formal guidance to raise, and the only target was reaffirmed, not raised.

  • Old pattern:
  • The IPO-era targets lapsed.
  • The EUR 200m 2025 target was abandoned in March 2024 (2025 actual EUR 105.3m).
  • The research calls management historically an "over-promiser archetype".
  • Margins missed implied targets by about 200 basis points in FY2023.
  • New pattern (since November 2025):
  • Q1 2026 framed "transitional", then adjusted EBITA beat by about 3%.
  • Q2 2026: revenue and adjusted EBITA each 4% ahead of consensus.
  • Q3 2026: organic growth 11% against an 8% consensus; adjusted EBITA 4% ahead of EUR 28.1m.
  • Tone stays cautious (US orthopaedics, Japan).
  • Nature: the chief executive manages the next quarter down and lets results speak. He has not yet confronted the long-term target. The Capital Markets Day is the first test of how he handles it.

  • broker cuts of 4% to 7% in mid-2025
  • FY2026 consensus EPS down about 15% over twelve months

Over five years it is poor: organic growth collapsed from 16.5% to 3.7% in 2022, and the IPO-era ambitions and the 2025 target were abandoned. In comparable conditions (a weak US end-market), the old team missed in 2025 and the new team has beaten in 2026. The change in the record coincides with the change in management, on a four-quarter sample.

  • One year (Q4 2025 to Q3 2026):
  • Q4 2025: organic 6% to 7%; DNB Carnegie previewed "muted growth momentum into year-end". About in line.
  • Q1 2026: adjusted EBITA growth 3% ahead after "transitional" framing. Organic 9% outpaced the market.
  • Q2 2026: revenue EUR 121.6m against EUR 117m (+4%); adjusted EBITA EUR 29.7m against 28.5m (+4%); organic 12% against 9%.
  • Q3 2026: organic 11% against 8%; adjusted EBITA EUR 29.2m against 28.1m (+4%).
  • Cash conversion missed badly in Q2 and recovered only partly in Q3. The beats are on the profit and loss account, not on cash.
  • Three years:
  • Q1 2024 net sales missed by 3% (MedTech reorganisation).
  • FY2024 organic 9%, above peers (6%).
  • FY2025: organic 6%; EPS flat; the adjusted EBITA target missed (0% bonus payout on that component). DNB Carnegie cut adjusted EBITA 4% to 5% and moved to Hold (July 2025). Jefferies cut EPS 7% and moved to Hold.
  • The Change Forces report says historical deliveries "frequently surpassed prior consensus on the top line", while interest diluted EPS.
  • Five years: organic growth of 16.5% (2021) then 3.7% (2022); a 35% margin ambition and a 30% sales CAGR dropped; the EUR 200m 2025 target missed by about half.
  • Pattern for the model: beat on revenue more than on EPS; beat recently on segment EBITA; miss on cash and below-EBITA items. Lean forecasts towards revenue upside and EPS and cash caution.

After assessing the track record of delivery in greater detail, how much weight should the forecasts for the next 18 months to four years carry, and in which direction should they be leaned?

F4017th percentile

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: weight the revenue forecasts heavily and the margin, EPS and cash forecasts lightly. Lean revenue slightly up, and margin, EPS and free cash flow down, against both consensus and management.

What the record supports:

  • Revenue: deliverable. Organic growth has beaten the market every year since 2021. 2026 brought three straight organic beats (Q2: 12% against 9%; Q3: 11% against 8%). Acquisitions, which management controls, add 2 to 5 points.
  • Margin: has not been delivered. The adjusted EBITA margin fell three years running. Three mid-term ambitions were dropped (a 35% margin, a 30% sales CAGR, EUR 200m of EBITA by 2025 against an actual 105.3m). FY2026 consensus EPS fell 15.1% over twelve months.
  • The new team's four quarters of beats are on revenue and segment EBITA, measured against consensus that had already been cut.
  • Cash: has repeatedly undershot. Operating cash flow was EUR 3.8m in Q2 2026, and the promise that working capital would "normalise in Q3" was not met. FY2025's 101% conversion leaned on USD 28.7m of litigation receipts.

My base already leans this way:

  • Revenue in line with consensus (above it from FY2028 only on unannounced deals).
  • EBITDA −4% against consensus in FY2027 (−6% excluding unannounced deals).
  • EPS like for like −4% (FY2027) and about −10% (FY2028).

Confidence I attach to my own FY2027 base (my judgement):

  • About 70% that revenue lands within ±4% of EUR 536m.
  • About 60% that adjusted EBITA lands within ±7% of EUR 133m.
  • About 50% that model-basis EPS lands within ±12% of EUR 0.144.
  • Free cash flow within about ±25% of EUR 55m.
  • Direction of risk around my base: revenue balanced to slightly up (deal timing); EBITA margin balanced; free cash flow slightly down (working capital); EPS balanced.

How to weight the old record against the new team:

Where the record argues for leaning the other way:

Group judgement

Financial forecasts

B6078th percentile

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The base case is credible on the revenue line and modest everywhere below it.

  • Vimian should keep compounding revenue at 11% to 12% a year: about 8% organic, as the reinforcing cross-selling, training and buying-group loops outrun a shrinking clinic-visit market, plus 4 to 5 points from self-funded bolt-ons.
  • The four forces that would turn that growth into EPS are each weak:
  • Fixed-cost operating leverage is low by design and mostly reinvested.
  • Cost elimination is small and offset by central costs and capitalised development.
  • Pricing offsets inflation but does not expand margin.
  • Financial leverage falls through EBITDA growth, not cheaper debt.
  • The result is a margin that repairs only about 100 basis points by 2030, still below 2023.

Where the EPS growth comes from: volume, plus the fading of below-EBITA drags that were unusually heavy in 2026. Those drags were non-cash earn-out charges, a 34% tax rate and IAC. EPS compounds about 18% a year from a depressed FY2026 base, with free cash flow more than doubling.

The central insight for the Consumer. The case's growth rate is not the problem. Its level against consensus is.

  • Consensus needs about 140 to 170 basis points of EBITDA margin expansion that has no precedent in the company's record. Part of it is flattered by the doubling of capitalised development.
  • Consensus also has the deleveraging benefit without the deals that the company will do.
  • My EBITDA is 6% to 8% below consensus excluding deals. My like-for-like EPS is about 4% (FY2027) to 10% (FY2028) below.
  • Revenue and EBITA will be the reliable lines. Margin, working capital, the earn-out loop and the related-party licensing income are where the forecasts can break.

For the third set: test organic growth and margin separately, and test EPS on one declared basis. The four EPS bases spread FY2026 consensus from EUR 0.089 to 0.140, and that spread is larger than any forecasting disagreement.

For the fourth set: expect consensus revenue to rise (deals), consensus EBITDA to drift down 3% to 6%, and consensus EPS to fall less than EBITDA. Consensus EPS growth already embeds some below-EBITA caution. The FY2027 revision pattern shows it: sales +7%, EBITDA +4%, EPS −7% over twelve months.

Tax.

  • The statutory blend across Sweden, the US, Germany, France, Italy, the Netherlands and the UK is about 24% to 25%. That is the company's "normalised" rate, and DNB Carnegie uses 25%.
  • The gap to the reported rate has three sources:
  • non-deductible contingent-consideration revaluations
  • unrecognised losses in start-ups (VetFamily Japan and Portugal, Nextmune Bio)
  • Swedish parent costs and interest limits
  • Transfer-pricing exposure is a further upside risk to tax (2025 annual report).
  • I close only about half the gap by 2028.
  • Consensus implies about 29% (FY2027) and 27% (FY2028), measured as net income over pre-tax profit. On a like-for-like basis that is about 1 to 2 points below me.

Specific items, with what each could do to the forecasts:

  • Related-party licensing income (EUR 9.4m in 2024, EUR 9.7m in 2025; EUR 4.9m in an interim 2026 period). It is in the base as recurring. If it stopped at a 60% to 80% margin, about EUR 6m to 8m of EBITA would go: 4% to 6% of group EBITA, and about 8% of FY2027 EPS. T-P4's licensing-rule explanation lowers the governance risk, not the continuity risk. A new segment head took over on 1 October 2026. This is the largest single swing factor below the segment line.
  • Earn-outs. EUR 31.6m carried, maximum EUR 142.1m. iM3's exceptional tranche is up to EUR 50m and is payable only on exceptional growth and cash flow. Cash payments sit in my acquisitions line (about EUR 12m to 15m a year). A full iM3 tranche would be a one-off outflow of up to EUR 50m, about 0.3x of leverage.
  • VOI receivable. USD 6.2m (about EUR 5.3m), carried at zero after the final Delaware ruling (24 September 2026). Upside to cash and reported profit, not in the base.
  • Currency. Unhedged. A 10% USD move is worth EUR 15.7m of revenue and EUR 2.3m of EBIT. A 10% move in sterling is worth EUR 5.0m and EUR 1.3m. I assume a −1.5 point translation drag on 2027 revenue (EUR/USD forecasts of 1.20 to 1.23 from Deutsche Bank and Morgan Stanley), which costs about EUR 1.5m of EBITA.
  • Capex. EUR 15.1m (2025, 3.6% of sales) and EUR 15.7m in nine months of 2026. I use EUR 21.5m (FY2026; capitalised development has doubled), then about 4.4% to 3.7% of revenue, against consensus of EUR 18m, 15m and 19m. FY2027 consensus capex looks EUR 8m too low against the company's "about 4%".
  • Refinancing. The EUR 150m bond is due May 2028. Cost effect is small (P-02).
  • Equity. No issuance in the base. "Without further capital injections" holds at my deal pace.

Against consensus, the below-EBITA gaps for FY2027 (my judgement):

  • Financial items: consensus's EBIT-to-pre-tax gap is about EUR 15m; mine is 18.3m. The EUR 3m to 4m difference is my continuing non-cash earn-out charge.
  • Tax: about 1 to 2 points higher in mine.
  • PPA: EUR 1m to 3m higher in mine (deals).
  • D&A excluding PPA: EUR 1.5m to 2.5m higher in mine (capitalised development).

Together these explain about EUR 6m to 9m of pre-tax profit, about 6% to 9% of FY2027 EPS. That is as large as the EBITDA gap.

Beyond cutting costs, how much plausible scope is there to improve the financial outputs over the mid term; do peer precedents show it is achievable; would it come from a few big areas or many small ones; and is it congruent with the stated strategy?

F5515th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: beyond cost cutting, there is plausible mid-term scope to improve financial outputs in a few big areas that are congruent with the stated strategy:

  • Diagnostics margin recovery
  • MedTech's US orthopaedic volume on a fixed sales force, plus dental mix
  • Direct-sales conversion in Specialty Pharma and dental
  • Working capital release
  • Below-EBITA normalisation (items affecting comparability, financial items, tax)

Together these could add about 100 to 200 basis points of group adjusted EBITA margin over three years and EUR 15m to 25m of one-off cash, on my arithmetic. Peer precedents show margin self-help is achievable in animal health. None of the levers is sized by the company, and two (US orthopaedic volume, working capital) have not yet moved.

The few big areas (my arithmetic, approximate):

  1. Diagnostics: from about 7% to 9% towards a companion-testing margin "closer to the group average" (CFO), from 2027. On EUR 35m to 38m of revenue at 15%, about +EUR 2.5m to 3m of EBITA (+40 to 50 basis points of group margin).
  2. MedTech: US orthopaedic volume recovery on the January 2026 field force, plus dental consumables and software mix (Clarity XR), plus Norfolk cross-selling. A segment margin from about 24% to 26% towards 27% to 28% adds about EUR 4m to 6m on EUR 200m to 215m (+70 to 100 basis points of group margin).
  3. Direct-sales conversion in Specialty Pharma and dental: DNB Carnegie raised estimates 3% to 4% for the iM3 conversion alone.
  4. Working capital: from 26% to 23% of revenue (company basis) releases about EUR 15m. To 21%, about EUR 25m. One-off, not recurring.
  5. Below EBITA: items affecting comparability from EUR 14.7m towards EUR 5m to 10m; tax from about 33% towards 29% to 30%; financial items towards EUR 17m to 20m.

Many small areas: SKU rationalisation, site consolidation, distributor terminations country by country, cross-selling clinic by clinic. This is the decentralised model's natural way of improving, slow and incremental.

Peer precedents:

  • Vetoquinol reached its 20% EBITA margin target through mix and in-housing (FY2025 21.7%).
  • Genus's ABS VAP lifted bovine margins through structured self-help.
  • Swedencare's route back to about 26% EBITDA margins after channel costs is cited as the nearest precedent.
  • IDEXX's 41% ROIC on a recurring consumables model is an aspiration, not a precedent.

Congruence: every item is on the chief executive's stated agenda ("accelerate what is working well and address the areas we need to improve"). The Capital Markets Day is where they could be sized.

C-80

To what extent has the core growth engine decelerated over the last twelve months: is the revenue growth rate running below the company's own normal rate, and is the input change force itself slowing?

B7279th percentile

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: the core growth engine has not decelerated over the last twelve months. It has accelerated. Organic growth rose from 5% (Q2 and Q3 2025) to 9%, 12% and 11% (Q1 to Q3 2026), above the company's own normal rate of about 6% to 9%. The input change forces are all running: cross-selling, dental, buying-group membership, bolt-ons and the MedTech reorganisation. The forward risk is a base-driven deceleration in 2027, not a failing engine. On a two-year view the engine runs at about 8% a year.

  • Revenue growth against normal:
  • Organic growth over the last three quarters (9%, 12%, 11%) is above the 2023-2025 range (about 6% to 9%).
  • Reported growth of 15% to 17% in Q2-Q3 2026 is at the top of the three-year range.
  • Inputs:
  • Cross-selling is about 18% of sales.
  • VetFamily members grew from 11,400 to 12,400.
  • Dental is "robust".
  • US orthopaedics is growing again (low single digit).
  • The M&A pipeline is "more active".
  • 75 new products.
  • None of these is slowing.
  • Caveats:
  • The twelve months include Specialty Pharma's campaign effects (Q2 +5 points, Q3 -4 points) and a livestock PCR-driven Diagnostics quarter.
  • MedTech's 15% is against a weak base (FY2025 -1%).
  • Two-year stacked group organic growth is about 8% a year.
  • Expect 2027 organic growth around 8% to 9% as MedTech laps its recovery. The memo's alarm level is Specialty Pharma underlying growth below 8%; its invalidation level is below 6% for two quarters.
C-67

How do the sell-side's forecasts for the next one to three years compare with the last one to five years, for the main financial statement line items in particular and the granular working up to them, and how credible is the gap given the change forces and foundations found above?

F309th percentile

Layer 1 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement: on revenue, the sell-side's forecasts are a credible deceleration from the past. Revenue growth goes from 13% to 18% (FY2023-2025) to 13.8%, 11.5% and 9.0% (FY2026-2028), in line with about 8% to 9% organic growth plus acquisition carry-over. Consensus may even be light if acquisitions continue at EUR 80m to 100m a year, because it appears to include little unannounced M&A.

Below revenue, consensus departs sharply from the past, and that gap is only partly credible:

  • The EBITDA margin rises 220 basis points (26.7% to 28.9%) after three flat-to-down years.
  • Depreciation and amortisation are held flat in euros while revenue grows 30%.
  • Financial items fall by about 40%.
  • The tax rate falls from about 33% to about 27%.
  • Capex dips below the company's own indication.

The combination produces EPS growth of +21% and +26% in FY2027-2028 against about 9% a year delivered. I judge the revenue line credible, the margin line optimistic by roughly 30 to 60 basis points a year, and the below-EBITA lines optimistic by about EUR 5m to 8m of pre-tax profit by FY2028.

After assessing the other questions/items in the set in greater detail, what is the net likely impact to the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

B6281st percentile

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: the next four years look like a better-converting version of the last four, not a different business.

The main lines in my base:

  • Revenue: compounds about 11.6% a year from FY2026 to FY2030, against 13% to 18% in 2023-25. Organic growth is 7.5% to 8.5% a year and bolt-ons add 4 to 5 points.
  • Adjusted EBITA margin: recovers about 100 basis points from the 2026 trough (24.5% to 25.5%), after a 180 basis-point fall since 2022. EBITA compounds about 12.7% a year.
  • EPS: compounds about 18% a year on the model basis and after-PPA basis, and about 25% on the reported basis. Below-EBITA leakage shrinks.
  • Free cash flow: rises from EUR 38m to 92m.

Where the improvement sits: mostly in the below-EBITA lines, which consensus already assumes. Operating margin gives less. That is where consensus needs a break with history: +170 basis points of EBITDA margin from FY2025 to FY2027.

Net against consensus:

  • Revenue: in line (above it from FY2028 only on unannounced deals).
  • EBITDA: −2.6% (FY2026), −4.2% (FY2027), −2.2% (FY2028). Excluding my unannounced deals: about −6% (FY2027) and −8% (FY2028).
  • EPS like for like: about −4% (FY2027) and about −10% (FY2028).
  • Free cash flow: below FactSet's 58 and 78 for FY2027-28 by about 5% and 16%, but that definition is unclear.

Where the change comes from (FY2026 to FY2028, model basis; my arithmetic):

  • Pre-tax profit rises EUR 37.1m.
  • EBITA contributes +33.2m:
  • organic volume at a constant margin: +20m (about 60%)
  • bolt-ons: +10m (about 30%)
  • margin: +3m (about 10%)
  • Lower financial items contribute +4.4m.
  • A lower tax rate adds about 3 points to EPS growth.

The main driver is segment volume, which is the most reliable line. Margin, the least reliable line, carries little of my base. This is the opposite of consensus. Consensus's EBITDA growth of +37m from FY2026 to FY2028 has no deals in it, and about 22% of it is margin (+140 basis points on EUR 588m).

What this means for the third set:

  • My forecast is concentrated in two drivers: organic volume (Specialty Pharma and MedTech, about 77% of revenue) and the bolt-on pace.
  • Consensus is concentrated in organic volume plus margin.
  • Testing consensus therefore means testing the margin.
  • Testing my base means testing organic growth and the deal pace.

Forecasts and upgrades

8

Probably upgrades this financial year to

D3231st percentile

Memo topic 7, Scope for upgrades over 6 to 12 months: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

The fourth set as a whole

F3220th percentile

Layer 4 · The fourth set as a whole

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Judgement: our assessment is reflected in guidance, estimates and the narrative on everything that is good about Vimian, and is not reflected on the things that are less good. That is the wrong way round for an investment case that looks to upgrades. On the test the memo must apply, whether guidance, sell-side estimates and market expectations fail to capture the probable developments of the next 6 and 6 to 18 months, the answer is that they capture the favourable developments in full and run ahead of the probable outcome on margin and EPS. The business is sound and its operating forecasts are predictable. The gap between our view and the market's is small, and where it exists it is against the shares.

The three answers together:

  1. Guidance (I-01). There is none to raise. The quarterly habit of cautious framing and a beat should continue for two more quarters at the operating level. The one number the company has given is about 58% above our 2030 forecast and will have to change, most likely after the Capital Markets Day, not at it.
  2. Estimates (I-02). Consensus revenue is right only if acquisitions arrive; before deals it embeds double-digit organic growth and is about 3% above us. Consensus EBITDA is about 2% to 8% above us, depending on the year and on whether unannounced deals are counted, and consensus EPS about 4% (FY2027) to 10% (FY2028) above us, like for like. Revisions are mildly positive today and more likely to turn negative on profit lines from mid-2027.
  3. Narrative (I-03). The positives are known and priced; the lines below EBITA, the licensing income, the EPS basis and the organic growth embedded in consensus are not articulated, and most of them are unfavourable in the 18-month window.

The pattern that joins them, and joins them to the third set. In each of the three, the dividing line is adjusted EBITA.

  • Above it, our view, the company's signals, consensus and the narrative agree, the forecasts are predictable (D-04), and the share price has already responded.
  • Below it, the company says nothing (D-06), the brokers assume normalisation, the narrative is silent, and our forecast is lower. The reason is a single mechanism that the market treats as noise: the cost of buying growth (PPA, earn-out charges that rise with the success of what was bought, the tax those charges attract, the inventory that dental growth needs) is recurring, and it is largest when the operating news is best.

What this implies for the memo's topics.

  • Scope for upgrades over 6 to 12 months (topic #7): limited to revenue and EBITDA on announced acquisitions. Not EPS.
  • Current momentum (topic #8): price momentum strong (94th percentile of relative strength; +40% in six months); estimate momentum middling (EPS for FY2027 unchanged over six months; the dashboard's rating C); the nearest peers weak. The price has run well ahead of the estimates.
  • The stage of the stock (topic #10): past the turn and past the broadening of the thesis; in a reinforcing stage that now needs proof of conversion to continue, and with a first half of 2027 that offers slowing headline growth before any such proof can arrive. I would describe the risk as stalling, not collapsing: the operating case is too well spread and too well evidenced for the latter.
  • The fair multiple and the return (topics #11 and #12): the guideline multiple of 27.07x is close to the ten-year 90th percentile. It is a multiple for a stock in peak delivery, with upgrades. Vimian is delivering on revenue and not yet on earnings. Applied on one earnings basis it gives a return of about +15% to +34% to March 2028, not +45% (D-01). At the five-year median multiple (21.19x) the value is about SEK 35 on consensus earnings and about SEK 30 on our after-PPA earnings (my arithmetic).

What would turn the gap favourable.

  • A lower price. Around SEK 30 to 35, the market would be paying a median multiple for a business that should grow organically at about 8%, compound EBITA at about 12% to 13% a year and roughly double its free cash flow by 2030. At that level the expectations would under-capture the operating case, and the unarticulated negatives would be in the price.
  • Or evidence that the mechanism below EBITA is being managed: a public cash-conversion, working-capital or return target (possible on 24 November 2026); a fall in MedTech inventory (visible on 11 February 2027); and a first full year in which the adjusted EBITA margin rises (reported February 2028).
  • Or a faster organic rate than we assume, through a recovery in US orthopaedic volumes, which would lift EBITA by EUR 2m to 3m a year and is in neither our base nor, explicitly, in consensus.

The two things most likely to make this judgement wrong. First, the company's record under its present chief executive is of beating, and three of our differences with consensus (margin, organic growth in 2027, and the charges below EBITA) each rest on a sample of three or four quarters. Second, we have not seen the research of three of the six brokers, including the only Sell. The market may know more of what we call unknown than the files show, in which case the gap is narrower still, and neither favourable nor unfavourable.
