I-01

How likely is company guidance to be raised, met or reduced at the next few earnings events (all such events over the next 18 months), and what would drive each outcome? Secondarily, how likely is any guidance, mid-term target or ambition for 18 months to three years out to be raised, met or reduced?

F3817th percentile

Layer 4 · Guidance

Judgement: there is almost nothing to raise. Vimian gives no annual numerical guidance, so the usual route by which a company lifts expectations does not exist here. What exists is one long-dated target and a set of qualitative signals. Over the next 18 months I expect the qualitative signals to be met, with one exception: the "double-digit organic growth" ambition will be visibly missed in 2027 and quietly re-framed. I expect the 2030 target to be reaffirmed at the Capital Markets Day on 24 November 2026 (about four chances in five) and, over 18 months to three years, to be re-dated, re-based or dropped (slightly better than even). In place of guidance upgrades, the company's working method is to frame the next quarter cautiously and then beat the consensus it collects. That method is likely to keep working on revenue and segment EBITA, and it does not reach EPS or cash.

What counts as guidance, and how each part compares with our assessment:

Guidance or signal Our assessment Gap
Adjusted EBITA above EUR 300m by 2030, "over half organic", "without further capital injections", leverage below 3.0x (March 2024; reaffirmed 22 October 2026) About EUR 190m in 2030 Guidance is about 58% above us. DNB Carnegie puts the required growth at about 27% a year from 2026
"Maintaining double-digit organic growth" (an ambition) 8.5% in 2027, falling to 7.5% Above us by about two points
Veterinary Services margin normalising around 28.5% to 29% 28.8% In line
Diagnostics margin improving "from 2027" 12% in 2027 In line for the year; at risk in the first quarter
US orthopaedics at low single digits; market "not structurally changed" No recovery In line; cautious
Capex about 4% of revenue; no dividend; "active" acquisitions; Norfolk adding to earnings from 2027 The same In line

The Capital Markets Day, 24 November 2026 (probabilities are my judgement):

Outcome for the 2030 target Probability Effect on the case
Raised Under 3% Not credible on the arithmetic
Reaffirmed, with segment roadmaps and a general acquisition ambition, but no cash, working-capital or return target About 45% Neutral to mildly negative. It is what brokers expect at a minimum, and it leaves the EPS and cash questions open
Reaffirmed, and supported by building blocks that can be modelled: segment growth and margin corridors, an acquisition envelope, a cash-conversion or working-capital target About 25% The best outcome. It would narrow the EPS and cash cone (D-04) and would be the first sign of a cash and returns discipline (D-02)
Re-dated, re-based, or replaced by rate targets of the kind Nordic acquirers use (annual EBITA growth, a margin, leverage) About 20% Healthiest for the long run; probably negative for the shares at first
Reaffirmed with a signal of larger acquisitions or openness to equity About 10% Negative for minority shareholders, given two earlier issues at falling prices

Why I put reaffirmation at about four in five.

  • The target was reaffirmed in the Q3 report one month before the event.
  • The new chief executive has not yet addressed it in his own words, and the board's mandate to him was "greater urgency, not a strategy shift".
  • The controlling owner, Fidelio Capital (58.4%), is a growth sponsor and chairs the acquisitions committee.
  • Restating a target downward at a first Capital Markets Day, while the share price is at a 52-week high, is a choice few managements make.

Why I still give one chance in five to a re-framing. The chief executive's own style is to frame cautiously (Q1 2026 as "transitional"; Japan revenue projections as "premature"). Keeping a target that needs about three times the present deal pace is out of character for him. Swedencare's experience is fresh: it set five-year targets at its first Capital Markets Day on 2 June 2026, missed in the following quarter and announced its chief executive's departure in September.

What the event is likely to do to expectations.

  • The invitation promises roadmaps for the four segments. I expect segment ambitions for growth and margin and little or nothing on tax, earn-outs or the lines below EBITA (D-06).
  • If an acquisition envelope is given, some brokers will start to include deals. AddLife is the precedent: explicit acquisition programmes led Redeye to raise 2027 to 2028 adjusted EBITA by 4% to 7% (October 2026).
  • The expectations going in are raised. The share price has gained about 40% in six months, target prices have risen 6% in a month, and DNB Carnegie calls the event "a crucial strategic milestone for rebuilding long-term investor confidence". The most likely outcome (the second row) is therefore more likely to disappoint a little than to please.

The quarterly events to May 2028: Q4 2026 (11 February 2027), Q1, Q2 and Q3 2027, Q4 2027 (February 2028) and Q1 2028. With no numbers to raise, the question becomes whether each qualitative signal is upheld, strengthened or withdrawn (probabilities are my judgement):

Signal Upheld Strengthened Weakened or withdrawn What decides it
"Double-digit organic growth" 20% under 5% about 75% The comparisons: 12% in Q2 2026 and 11% in Q3. Our Q2 2027 is about 7%. I expect the wording to move to "outgrowing the market" during 2027
Veterinary Services margin about 28.5% to 29% 80% 10% 10% The pace of new-country spending
Diagnostics improving from 2027 65% for the year 10% 25% Companion-test volume. Q1 2027 compares with 13.8%, so the first reading may show a fall
US orthopaedics at low single digits 70% 15% 15% Elective demand; the field sales force
Leverage below 3.0x; no new equity 85% not applicable 15% The size of acquisitions
Working capital and cash conversion improving 50% 20% (a formal target) 30% MedTech inventory

The practical substitute for guidance: the quarterly beat. Under the present chief executive the company has beaten the consensus it collects on organic growth and adjusted EBITA in three consecutive quarters, by about 4% on EBITA in Q2 and Q3 2026. I expect that pattern to continue more often than not at the operating level for the next two quarters, because the quarterly bar is set by the same cautious tone. It is weaker from Q2 2027, when the comparisons harden. It does not extend below EBITA: full-year FY2026 consensus EPS fell 15% over the twelve months in which those beats were delivered.

Secondarily: 18 months to three years out.

  • The 2030 target. By the FY2028 report (February 2029) our adjusted EBITA is about EUR 151m, half the target, with two years left. The LTIP 2026/2029 vests on FY2028 adjusted EBITA, so the board will have to state, at least to itself, what a realistic FY2028 is. The company has already dropped three sets of mid-term ambitions (a 35% margin, a 30% sales growth rate, EUR 200m of adjusted EBITA by 2025). My judgement for the period to late 2029: re-dated, re-based or dropped about 55%; maintained about 40%, of which about 15 points by pursuing it through a large or equity-funded deal; raised under 5%.
  • Any new mid-term targets set at the Capital Markets Day (segment margins, a group margin corridor) would, if set in line with management's recent caution, be the first Vimian targets with a fair chance of being met.

How far guidance reflects our assessment.

  • Near term: almost fully, and slightly conservatively. Our inputs match the qualitative signals line by line. The gap is small and mildly favourable.
  • Mid term: not at all. The only number is far above our view. That is the opposite of conservative guidance, and it carries a credibility cost that has still to be paid.
  • Net. This is not a company with conservative guidance and a record of raising it. It is a company with no guidance, a recent habit of beating the quarter, and one target that will have to be changed.

I-02

How likely are sell-side consensus forecasts, and the net breadth of revisions, to move higher or lower over the next few earnings events (over the next 18 months): to be upgraded, cut or held, and why? Secondarily, how likely are the consensus forecasts for 18 months to three years out to be upgraded, cut or held?

D3537th percentile

Layer 4 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement: over the next 18 months I expect consensus revenue to hold and then rise as acquisitions are announced, consensus EBITDA to be cut by about 3% to 6%, and consensus EPS to be cut by about 4% for FY2027 and about 10% for FY2028 on a like-for-like basis. Net breadth of revisions, which is mildly positive today after the Q3 beat, is more likely to turn negative on profit lines during 2027 than to strengthen. The cuts are most likely to come at the Q2 2027 report (July 2027) and at the FY2027 report (February 2028), not in the next quarter. One correction to the earlier sets matters here: consensus revenue for FY2027 already assumes organic growth of about 10% to 11%. Without new acquisitions it is not light. It is about 3% too high.

Where consensus stands today (FactSet, 10 November 2026):

  • Revisions to FY2027 over one, three, six and twelve months: sales +3%, +3%, +4%, +7%; EBITDA +1%, +3%, +3%, +4%; EPS 0%, +4%, 0%, -7%. Target prices +6%, +7%, +11% (to six months). The share of buy ratings is 57%, unchanged. The dashboard's rating of sell-side earnings momentum is C.
  • Six brokers: two Hold (UBS, Jefferies), one Sell (Barclays), three Buy (Nordea, DNB Carnegie and one unnamed). The mean target price (EUR 3.72) is below the share price (EUR 3.80).

Our forecast against consensus (the model's figures, with the unannounced bolt-ons separated; my arithmetic):

Line FY2027 consensus Ours, with unannounced deals Ours, without them FY2028 consensus Ours, with unannounced deals Ours, without them
Revenue 539 536 (-0.6%) about 524 (-2.9%) 588 602 (+2.4%) about 563 (-4.2%)
EBITDA, brokers' basis 153 147 (-4.0%) about 144 (-6%) 170 166 (-2.3%) about 156 (-8%)
EPS, like for like 0.121 about -4% 0.152 about -10%

The correction on revenue. The first set (C-67, C-85) read consensus revenue growth of 11.5% for FY2027 as implying organic growth of 8% to 9%, and called the revenue line "credible to slightly conservative". The second set's segment work shows otherwise. The carry-over from 2026 acquisitions is only about 1.7 points (about EUR 8m), and consensus contains no unannounced deals. Consensus organic growth for FY2027 is therefore about 10% to 11%, or a little under 10% if brokers assume no currency effect. That is management's "double-digit" ambition, not a discount to it. Our 8.5% leaves us about 3% below consensus revenue before new deals (2.5% below in Specialty Pharma and 4.6% below in MedTech). Revenue upgrades therefore depend on acquisitions being announced, not on organic growth beating. Brokers add deals only when announced (DNB Carnegie did so on 30 April 2026; Jefferies states that its estimates exclude unannounced deals).

Why EBITDA and EPS are more likely to be cut than raised.

  • Margin. Consensus has the EBITDA margin rising from 26.7% (FY2025) to 28.4% (FY2027) and 28.9% (FY2028). Ours is 27.4% and 27.6%. Jefferies, which has the longest coverage, is 50 basis points below consensus on the 2027 adjusted EBITA margin and 70 below on 2028. The nine months of 2026 produced an incremental margin of about 22% against the 36% that consensus needs in FY2027.
  • Lines below EBITDA. Consensus depreciation is flat while capitalised development has doubled. Consensus PPA amortisation is flat while deals continue. Consensus capex for FY2027 (EUR 15m) is about EUR 8m below the company's "about 4% of revenue".
  • Organic growth in 2027. At about 8.5% against a consensus near 10% to 11%, the shortfall would be about EUR 15m of revenue and about EUR 4m to 5m of EBITDA.

Why measured EPS revisions are an unreliable signal for this stock. Consensus EPS is an average across at least two definitions. UBS (0.089 for FY2026) and Nordea (0.090) deduct PPA; DNB Carnegie (0.140) adds it back and uses a 25% tax rate. A broker joining, leaving or changing basis moves the mean without any change of view: the FactSet mean for FY2026 fell 3.4% in the last month, the month in which UBS's estimate (dated 4 November), the lowest of the six, was added or updated. Watch EBITDA and sales revisions, and individual brokers' changes, not the consensus EPS line. For the same reason, the twelve-month pattern (sales +7%, EBITDA +4%, EPS -7%) should be read with care. DNB Carnegie attributed its EPS cuts of 2025 to interest on the new bond. The larger recurring cause, in my view, is the contingent-consideration charge and its effect on tax, which brokers adjust out after the event and do not forecast.

FY2027, February 2028 The verdict on the margin. FY2028 EBITDA and EPS cut if the adjusted EBITA margin is near our 24.8% and not the 25.4% consensus implies The first full-year test of "margins the next test"
Acquisitions, at any time Revenue and EBITDA raised on announcement: each EUR 65m of deals adds about EUR 25m of annual revenue and about EUR 7m of EBITDA The only source of upgrades that our base contains and consensus does not. Worth 1% to 2% of EPS on a pre-PPA basis and about nil after PPA

Probabilities for the level of today's consensus 12 to 18 months from now (my judgement):

Consensus line Higher by more than 2% Within 2% Lower by more than 2%
FY2027 revenue (EUR 539m) 35% 40% 25%
FY2027 EBITDA (EUR 153m) 20% 30% 50%

FY2028 EBITDA (EUR 170m) 25% 30% 45%
FY2028 EPS (0.152), like for like 15% 25% 60%

The case against my view, which I weight at about 35% to 40%.

  • The company has beaten quarterly EBITA consensus by 3% to 4% three times running, and DNB Carnegie raised 2027 to 2028 EBITA by 3% after Q3.
  • Estimates are rising now: FY2028 EPS is up 6% in three months.
  • A recovery in US orthopaedic volumes on the fixed field sales force, or a faster Diagnostics recovery, would add EUR 2m to 5m of EBITA.
  • A larger flow of deals than we assume would lift revenue and EBITDA estimates by more than organic shortfalls lower them.

Net breadth. Today: mildly positive (EBITDA +3% in three months, EPS +4%, targets rising, no change in ratings). My expectation: positive on revenue on deal announcements; flat to negative on EBITDA from mid-2027; negative on EPS for FY2028. Ratings are more likely to move from Buy to Hold than the reverse while the share price is above the mean target. I do not expect the dashboard's rating of sell-side momentum to rise above C during 2027.

Secondarily: 18 months to three years out.

  • FY2029 rests on one broker (revenue EUR 626m, EBITDA EUR 181m, EPS 0.163). Its growth of 6.5% is an artefact of thin coverage, as the first set said.
  • As more brokers publish FY2029 and as deals are announced, I expect consensus revenue and EBITDA for FY2029 to rise (ours are 7.4% and 3.5% higher, wholly because of unannounced deals). I expect EPS to be held or cut a little: our after-PPA EPS for FY2029 is 0.155.
  • The longer-dated estimates are the one place where the direction of revisions is likely to be upward, and the reason is acquisitions, which add revenue and EBITDA and add little to EPS after PPA.

How far consensus reflects our assessment. On revenue it does, and it will reflect more as deals arrive. On the margin and on EPS it runs ahead of our assessment, by about 100 basis points of EBITDA margin in FY2027 and about 130 in FY2028, and by about 10% of FY2028 EPS. For a case that is looked to for upgrades over the next six to twelve months, that is an unfavourable gap. It is softened by two things: the company's habit of beating the quarter, and the fact that half the brokers, and the mean target price, are already cautious.

D-0762
D-07

To what extent do the share price and the multiple move with sector and industry peers rather than idiosyncratically (on a rolling six-month basis), and so how far is the multiple company-driven rather than industry-driven?

A6293rd percentile

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the share price moves almost entirely on the company's own news. Over the latest six-month window the peer basket explains 7% of the daily variance of Vimian's price, and 93% is its own. That figure has stayed between 4% and 18% in every six-month window for two years. The stock's beta to the basket has fallen from about 1.0 in mid-2025 to 0.45 as it re-rated. The multiple is company-driven to the same degree: Vimian's has expanded by roughly a third in six months, with consensus earnings barely changed, while the nearest animal-health peers sit near the bottom of their own ten-year ranges. The implication is two-sided. The case will be decided by Vimian's own prints, which we can analyse. And nothing in the sector will cushion a company-specific disappointment.

The evidence from the co-movement table (daily returns, six-month windows, against 33 European pharmaceutical and biotechnology peers with price history):

  • Latest window (21 May to 13 November 2026): correlation 0.26; beta 0.45; share of variance explained 7%. Vimian returned +41.5% against +14.1% for the basket.
  • Over 24 months: the share explained was never above 18%. It was highest, at 14% to 18% with a beta of about 1.0, in the windows ending April to September 2025, which include the market fall of spring 2025. It has been 4% to 9% in every window since October 2025.
  • Against the animal-health names in the basket: Vetoquinol 0.09, Genus 0.18, Virbac 0.21. These are no higher than the correlations with large pharmaceutical companies (GSK and Sanofi 0.26). The highest single readings are bioMerieux (0.31) and Galderma (0.29). There is no animal-health factor visible in the price.

The multiple (from the financials block and the dashboard; the co-movement table covers prices only):

  • Vimian's 24-month-forward multiple is 25.14x, at the 75th percentile of its five-year range. Its trailing-basis multiple is at the 57th percentile of its ten-year range.
  • The animal-health peers are at the 1st (Vetoquinol), 5th (Virbac) and 12th (Genus) percentiles of their own ten-year ranges.
  • Over six months the share price rose about 40%. Consensus EPS for FY2027 changed by 0% over the same six months and FY2028 by about +3%. Almost the whole six-month rise is an expansion of the multiple, and it happened to Vimian alone. The news behind it was the company's: three consecutive beats, a permanent chief executive, MedTech's recovery.

Three qualifications to the headline number.

  1. The basket is the dashboard's sector, not the economic peer group. Swedencare, Zoetis, IDEXX, Elanco and the Nordic serial acquirers are not in it. The sell-side values Vimian against exactly those names (DNB Carnegie: about 17x 2027 EV/EBITA against 15x to 16x for core animal-health peers and Nordic compounders). Co-movement with them is not measured here and may be higher. The first set records a 17% fall to February 2026 that it attributed to "sector capital outflows"; the table shows no rise in co-movement with this basket in that period (5% to 9%), so if that fall was a sector effect it was a sector this basket does not capture.
  2. Thin trading lowers daily correlation mechanically. Value traded is about USD 2.3m to 2.5m a day and the free float is about one third to 40%. A thinly traded stock responds to sector news with a delay, which daily data understate. The gap in six-month returns (41.5% against 14.1%) confirms the conclusion all the same.
  3. Independence is conditional. In the one stressed period in the series the beta was about 1.0 and the share explained more than doubled. In a broad sell-off Vimian has moved with the market, and its low present beta is a feature of a stock rising on its own news, not a defensive property. A small, leveraged, acquisition-led company with a controlling holder is the kind the first set expected to be sold first in such a period.

What this means for the case.

  • The multiple is company-driven, at roughly nine-tenths on this evidence. It will respond to the Capital Markets Day and to the February and April prints, not to Zoetis or to the sector's direction. A sector re-rating would help little, because the peers' low multiples have not held Vimian's down.
  • The multiple now carries an expectation that estimates have not yet met. A re-rating with flat earnings estimates is a statement by the market that margin and cash conversion will follow. Our forecast is that revenue will arrive and that EBITDA will fall 3% to 6% short of consensus (I-02). A company-driven multiple gives no protection against that.
  • For predictability, the finding is helpful. The share price depends on variables this work can forecast and the company discloses (D-05), not on sector flows that cannot be forecast. That is the sense in which I score it above 50. It would score higher if our own view of those variables were above what the price implies, and it is not.

Peers compared. Vetoquinol and Virbac have delivered their margin plans and trade at the bottom of their ranges because their organic growth is low. Vimian has delivered growth without margin and trades in the top quarter of its range. The market is paying for growth and, in Vimian's case alone, paying in advance for conversion.

Outside the eleven ratings

Answers not placed under any of the eleven ratings

The table the ratings map uses places these nowhere; each is shown with the reason the table records.

Why it is not placed It asks whether the stock fits the sector's historical patterns of share price and financial outperformance, a cross-check across many topics rather than one of the eleven.

C-84

What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

D4434th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: in animal health and in the "serial acquirer / compounder" style, share-price outperformance has come from three things together. The first is sustained organic growth above the market. The second is visible conversion of that growth into earnings and cash: proven upgrades plus cash conversion. The third is a re-rating once the market believes the first two.

Underperformance has come from organic deceleration, an innovation or pricing shock in a core franchise, cash conversion that diverged from reported profit, and complex roll-ups trading on adjusted earnings in risk-off tapes.

Vimian today fits the outperformance pattern on organic growth and early estimate revisions. It fits the underperformance pattern on cash conversion, earnings quality and complexity. The price (+40% in six months, 75th percentile multiple) already reflects the first set.

  • Precedents in animal health:
  • IDEXX: the durable winner. A recurring consumable and installed-base model, 41% ROIC, double-digit earnings growth "insulated from price wars". The template for valuation premium: recurring revenue plus returns, not growth alone.
  • Zoetis: outperformed on blockbuster innovation (Apoquel, Simparica, Librela). It de-rated sharply when portfolios reached parity and pricing broke: -21.5% in a day (May 2026), guidance cuts, and Morgan Stanley cutting its target from USD 160 to 115.
  • Swedencare: the nearest listed Swedish peer. Fell on North American execution and channel destocking, leverage of about 3x and a CEO exit. Shows how a roll-up with retail exposure and leverage de-rates on organic misses.
  • Vetoquinol: delivered its 20% EBITDA margin target through mix and in-housing, yet sits at the 1st percentile of its own valuation range because organic growth is low single digit. Margin delivery without growth is not rewarded.
  • Virbac: organic +7.4% with margin expansion led to an upgrade to Outperform, but it is at the 5th percentile of its range. Sector flows dominate.
  • CVS Group: a roll-up with weak organic growth (like-for-like 2.1%), reliant on R&D credits and Australian M&A, sits at about 8x EBITDA after a -22% three-year TSR. Shows the market's discount for acquisition-led growth without organic growth.
  • Vimian's own history:
  • It listed in 2021 near a 58x forward multiple during 16.5% organic growth.
  • It de-rated through 2022-2025 as organic growth fell to 3.7% (2022), the VOI litigation cost USD 70m and forced a discounted rights issue, MedTech shrank, and targets were abandoned.
  • It re-rated in 2026 as organic growth rose to 9-12%. The shares are up about 50% from the February 2026 low of SEK 27.90 to SEK 41.80.
  • Rules of thumb from the research:
  • "Headline beats in European pharma and animal health often rest on accruals, stocking, receivables or one-off cash, so cash conversion is the test" (Researcher, 30 September 2026). Vimian's Q2 2026 was cited as an example.
  • "Slow growth is punished twice" (earnings and multiple).
  • "A crisp setup fit is often a late one."
  • "The market chooses its defensives, and the leveraged one de-rates first."
  • Fit: Vimian is in the favourable phase of the organic-acceleration pattern. Remaining outperformance requires the second leg (cash and margin conversion). The precedents say the market will not pay a compounder multiple without it, and will de-rate quickly if organic growth fades.

Why it is not placed The table holds this question in reserve for Riskiness sub-items that are not yet built, so it is not yet placed.

C-83

How far could the shares fall to a fair trough multiple?

D4648th percentile

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: with earnings unchanged, the shares could fall about 27% to a fair trough multiple: the five-year 25th percentile of 18.4x 24-month-forward earnings, about SEK 30.5. In a company-specific de-rating to the 10th percentile (about 15.7x), or the Selector's "crash" band (15.0x), the fall would be about 38% to 40% (about SEK 25 to 26). The sell-side's bear values corroborate this: Jefferies' downside of SEK 25 (14x 2027 EV/EBIT) and Barclays' target of EUR 2.61 (about SEK 29). With a modest earnings cut as well, a fair trough is about -35%.

My arithmetic at SEK 41.80 and 25.14x, which implies 24-month-forward EPS of about SEK 1.66:

Multiple (24-month-forward P/E) Value (SEK) Change
Five-year median 21.19x 35.2 -16%
Ten-year 35th percentile, "conservative" band, 18.4x 30.6 -27%
Five-year 25th percentile 18.36x 30.5 -27%
Five-year 10th percentile 15.66x 26.0 -38%
Ten-year 10th percentile, "crash" band, 15.0x 24.9 -40%
Five-year low 11.13x 18.5 -56%
18.4x on EPS 10% lower (the below-EBITA relief not arriving) 27.5 -34%
  • The fair trough for this business (organic growth about 8%, ROIC about 10%, leverage 2x, no dividend, a sponsor overhang) is the 25th percentile in an ordinary sector de-rating: about -27%. Note the 2021 listing at a 58x peak distorts the upper bands.
  • The 10th percentile applies in a company problem: a second cash miss, a governance event, or a Capital Markets Day disappointment. That is about -38% to -40%.
  • Context: the shares traded at SEK 27.90 in February 2026 and at a 52-week low of SEK 24.18 to 25.20. The trough is a known recent place, not a theoretical one.

Why it is not placed It rates the staircase of news the market can look forward to, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or the catalysts as such.

Group judgement

Stock market narrative

D4027th percentile

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The narrative has already moved through its first and most rewarding stage: from "broken roll-up with a leadership vacuum" (February 2026, SEK 27.90) to "turnaround with proven top line" (now SEK 41.80, +40% to 50%, 75th percentile multiple). The sell-side debate has shifted, in Jefferies' words, from "top-line delivery to the pace of margin conversion".

The next stage of the staircase is about earnings and cash conversion:

  • the Capital Markets Day bridge
  • FY2026 cash
  • Diagnostics and MedTech margins
  • FY2027 margin expansion

These are exactly the outputs where the company's record is weakest. The steps are dated and trackable, which is good for a modeller. But they are confirmations of what consensus already assumes, not novel surprises. The one novel near-term event (the Capital Markets Day) has a two-sided outcome tied to a target that cannot be met on its stated terms.

For the Consumer:

  • Calendar the steps above as the checkpoints for the model's margin and cash assumptions.
  • Expect the narrative to reward a credible, modest margin bridge and to punish any sign that growth needs equity or that cash conversion has not recovered.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the earlier answers agree with each other, which is a check on the analysis rather than on the company.

D-01

How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets: to what extent do they support and align with each other, rather than contradict or jar?

–68

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the earlier answers are congruent in substance and jar in presentation. Both sets reach the same core view by independent routes: revenue is the reliable line; the conversion of revenue into margin, cash and EPS is the weak one; consensus is about right on revenue and too high on margin. The second set's model implements the first set's judgements faithfully. The places where the work jars are five, and four of them are matters of basis or internal consistency, not of disagreement. One of them, the basis of the return calculation, is large enough to mislead the memo if it is not corrected.

Where the answers support each other (high congruence):

  • The growth engine. The first set's organic growth of about 8% to 9% (C-06, C-80), the second set's 8.5% falling to 7.5% (P-10), the market evidence (US clinic visits down 3.2% in Q3 2026; IDEXX, 2 November 2026) and the precedent research (T-D1: organic growth "validated") all agree.
  • Weak conversion. The first set's incremental margin of 19% to 22% (C-53), the second set's margin path of +30 basis points a year (P-01), Jefferies' 24.9% for 2027 and the peer evidence (AddLife's organic drop-through of about 7%) agree.
  • Capital allocation. Both sets, and every broker, assume continuous bolt-ons, no dividend and no buyback.
  • The scores. The first set's group scores (43 to 57) and the second set's (42 to 52) describe the same case: a sound business with an unproven earnings conversion, priced after a 40% rise.

Where the answers jar, and my resolution of each:

1. The model's return against the Analysts' caution (the important one).

  • The first and second sets score the case in the 40s and 50s. The model shows a total return of +45% in about 16 months (31% annualised). These cannot both be right.
  • The cause is a basis mismatch, on my arithmetic:
  • The model's two-year-forward multiple today is 23.2x, on model-basis EPS of about EUR 0.164 (price EUR 3.80).
  • FactSet's is 25.14x, on consensus EPS of about EUR 0.151. The guideline fair multiple of 27.07x is drawn from the history of this FactSet series. It sits at the ten-year 90th percentile (27.10x, the Selector's "mania" band).
  • On the second set's after-PPA basis, the one closest to consensus, two-year-forward EPS is about EUR 0.128. On our own like-for-like forecast the shares therefore already trade at about 28x to 30x, above the guideline fair multiple.
  • The return on one consistent basis, on my arithmetic:
Basis Multiple today Multiple at 31 March 2028 From the multiple From earnings Return
The model as it stands (model-basis EPS; multiple from consensus history) 23.2x 27.07x +16.8% +24.1% +45%
Consensus-basis multiple today; the model's earnings growth 25.14x 27.07x +7.7% +24.1% about +34%
After-PPA EPS for both the multiple and the earnings about 29.6x 27.07x about -8.5% about +26% about +15%
  • The third row uses after-PPA EPS of about EUR 0.161 for the year to March 2030 (0.155 for FY2029 and 0.179 for FY2030, weighted three to one). That gives a value of about SEK 48 against the model's SEK 60.6. The three-year return falls in the same way, from +74% to about +40%.
  • The Financial Analyst should state the multiple today and the guideline fair multiple on the same EPS basis as the earnings to which the multiple is applied, and should show the return on the after-PPA basis beside the model-basis return. Until then the Consumer should treat +15% to +34% as the range, and should say in the memo that about 17 points of the +45% is a change in the multiple that is not a re-rating.
  • This also explains the dashboard's note that the TSR/Valuation pin (F) and the re-based return "disagree by two grades or more".

2. The model's FY2026 EPS growth of +47% is a break in basis, not growth. The model's history is on the Viewforth definition, which is after PPA (FY2025: 0.083). Its forecasts exclude PPA of about EUR 26m. On the second set's model basis, FY2025 was 0.107 and FY2026 is 0.115: growth of about 7%. The same break makes the model's "EBITA" 26% above consensus EBIT and its net income 53% above consensus in FY2026. None of these is a forecast difference.

3. The cross-check gap the model summary flags (FY2026 EPS: the Analyst's 0.115 against the model's 0.122).

  • The gap is EUR 0.0077 a share, about EUR 4.1m of net income (my arithmetic). It has two parts:
  • Minority interests, about EUR 1.5m (0.003 a share). The Analyst deducts them; the model does not model them (Richard's ruling of 16 October 2026). This same amount explains the whole gap in FY2027 to FY2030 (for example FY2027: 78.5 against 76.8 plus 1.5).
  • The three reported quarters of 2026, about EUR 2.6m (0.005 a share). For reported periods the model holds FactSet's figures on the Viewforth definition: FactSet's operating EBITDA (EUR 99.6m for nine months against EUR 96.3m on the Analyst's basis) and reported tax adjusted for deferred tax, not the Analyst's 29.5% on model-basis profit.
  • The same cause produces the phasing note in the model summary. To reach the Analyst's full-year EBITDA margin of 26.9% from FactSet's higher nine-month figure, the model lowered the fourth-quarter margin by 2.7 points, to about 24.2%. The model's fourth quarter of 2026 is therefore understated by about EUR 3.4m of EBITDA, while the year is as the Analyst intended. The Consumer should not read the model's Q4 2026 quarter as a forecast of a weak quarter.
  • The gap does not show that any forecast input is wrong. It is confined to minorities and to reported periods. I change no input because of it.
  • Free cash flow (the Analyst's 38.5 against the model's 36.1, and EUR 1.4m to 2.6m in later years). The model treats all net financial items as cash. The Analyst adds back the non-cash part (about EUR 10m in 2026, EUR 4.5m a year after) and deducts one-off IAC paid in cash. The Analyst's figure is the better estimate of cash. No input is wrong.

4. Earn-out charges: an inconsistency inside the second set's base, which I correct.

  • The base assumes that acquired dental businesses keep growing at double digits and that EUR 65m a year of new bolt-ons, with earn-outs, continue. It also assumes the non-cash contingent-consideration charge falls by more than half, from about EUR 10m in 2025 and in 2026 to EUR 4.5m a year from 2027. These two assumptions pull against each other.
  • The new evidence (OWN-2, from the Q3 2026 report) points to the charge persisting:
  • The maximum payout rose from EUR 113.2m to EUR 142.1m during 2026, while the number of obligations fell from 27 to 25.
  • A 10% rise in the underlying performance measures now raises the liability by EUR 10.4m, against EUR 8.1m at end-2025.
  • New deals added EUR 8.5m of liability in nine months.
  • Probability adjustments were EUR 4.5m in 2025 and EUR 4.5m in the first nine months of 2026, including EUR 2.2m in Q3 alone.
  • Against this, the carried liability fell from EUR 46.4m to EUR 31.6m after EUR 29.4m of payments, so the discounting charge falls (EUR 5.3m in 2025; EUR 3.2m in nine months of 2026).
  • My change: non-cash charges of EUR 6.5m a year from 2027 (about EUR 3m of discounting and about EUR 3.5m of probability adjustments), in place of EUR 4.5m. Net financial items become -20.3, -21.1, -21.2 and -20.3 for FY2027 to FY2030. For FY2026 I use -24.5 in place of -23.5: the earn-outs are reassessed at the year-end, and Q4 2025 carried EUR 3.1m of probability adjustment.
  • Because these charges are not tax-deductible, I raise the adjusted tax rate so that the tax charge in euros is unchanged: 29.8%, 28.5%, 28.0%, 27.4% and 27.3%.
  • Effect (my arithmetic): model-basis EPS falls about 2.5% in FY2027 (the Analyst's 0.144 becomes 0.141) and about 2% in later years (0.167 becomes 0.163). Reported EPS falls from 0.099 to about 0.096 in FY2027. After-PPA EPS (0.091, 0.111, 0.131) does not change, because that basis adds the non-cash items back. The second set's like-for-like gap to consensus (about -4% in FY2027 and -10% in FY2028) therefore stands.
  • One consequence for reading the updated model: its equity free cash flow will fall by about EUR 2m a year, because the model treats the charge as cash. Real cash is unchanged. The Analyst-basis free cash flow (38.5, 54.8, 65.4) stands.

5. Working capital: the first set's explanation does not fit the Q3 balance sheet.

  • The first set (C-33, C-52) explained the build as a structural cost of selling directly to thousands of clinics. The second set kept the level (28.5% of revenue on FactSet's basis) and added that the stock "moved up" in Q3.
  • The Q3 detail (OWN-6) shows something narrower, on my arithmetic:
Line (company basis) End-2025 30 September 2026 Change Of which acquired
Inventory 80.1 98.3 +18.2 1.4
Trade receivables 57.3 67.5 +10.2 8.7 (trade and other receivables)
Trade payables 20.1 28.1 +8.0 not disclosed
Net working capital 96.6 116.4 +19.8
  • Receivables grew organically by only about EUR 1.5m while organic revenue grew about 11%. Days outstanding fell on an organic basis. Most of the acquired receivables came with the Veterinary Services deals (EUR 6.9m; Favna and VetSave).
  • The organic build is inventory of about EUR 17m, and the company attributes it to MedTech, mainly dental stocking. The cash flow statement shows EUR 16.5m absorbed by inventory in nine months.
  • It follows that the company's Q2 statement that the receivables build would "normalise in Q3" was, on the evidence, largely kept: Q3 working-capital cash flow was +EUR 0.3m while inventory rose, so receivables must have fallen by a similar amount (my inference; medium confidence). I disagree with the first and second sets' reading that the promise was simply broken. The receivables normalised and a dental inventory build took their place.
  • The level in the model does not change. What changes is the judgement about it: the problem is one segment's inventory, which is visible each quarter, and which the company has reduced before (EUR 8.0m in Q4 2023 through supply-chain action). It is more fixable, and more trackable, than a structural receivables problem. It is also more exposed to a write-down if the stocked dental products or the remaining discontinued orthopaedic items do not sell.

Smaller points of disagreement between the sets, on which I side as follows:

  • Related-party licensing income. The first set treated it as a governance alarm; the second as a structural dependency. The new research supports the second set. The counterparty is the entity of Nick Bova, the founder of the Bova compounding business bought in 2022, which holds the pharmacy licences that a company cannot hold directly in some jurisdictions. The income continued after the change of segment head (EUR 9.4m in 2024, EUR 9.7m in 2025, about EUR 7.7m in nine months of 2026). The risk is continuity and regulation (the FDA's August 2026 draft guidance on animal-drug compounding), not misconduct. I add one point neither set made: the income is lumpy by quarter (about EUR 4.0m in Q1 2026, EUR 0.9m in Q2 and EUR 2.8m in Q3; EUR 2.2m in Q1 2025). If it carries little cost, it moves Specialty Pharma's quarterly margin by one to two points without any change in trading.
  • Currency stress. The first set repeated a claim that a 20% fall in the US dollar pushes leverage past 3.0x. The second set could not reproduce it. I agree with the second set.
  • Free float. The first set uses 40.3%; Jefferies (October 2026) uses 33.8%. Use "about one third to 40%".
  • Training. Use 48,000 professionals (2025 annual report), as the second set says.

Why the score is not higher. The substance is consistent, but the Consumer works from these documents alone, and three of the five items above (the return basis, the FY2026 growth rate and the model's Q4 2026 quarter) would produce wrong statements in a memo if read at face value.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the sell-side and investors already articulate the case, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or how much of the case is known.

I-03

How comprehensively are these dynamics articulated in the current narrative of (i) sell-side commentary and (ii) investor commentary (for example on Twitter): how well known is each of them to investors who own or cover the company or its sector, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to three years out?

F3522nd percentile

Layer 4 · Stock market narrative

Judgement: the positive dynamics of the case are fully articulated and priced. The central open question, whether growth converts into margin, is also fully articulated, and the sell-side frames it as a test it expects the company to pass. What is not articulated is almost everything between EBITA and the shareholder: the contingent-consideration charge and its link to the tax rate, the related-party licensing income, the spread of EPS definitions, the composition of the working-capital build, and the fact that consensus revenue already assumes double-digit organic growth. These unarticulated dynamics are, on balance, negative for the next 18 months. The narrative therefore reflects the good news completely and the unfavourable detail hardly at all, which is an unfavourable gap. Two things soften it: the sell-side is not euphoric (two Holds, one Sell, and a mean target below the price), and a few of the unknowns cut in the company's favour.

The evidence base, and its limits.

  • Sell-side. Six brokers contribute to FactSet. The AlphaSense narrative report rests mainly on two of them, Jefferies and DNB Carnegie. It does not hold the research of UBS, Barclays, Nordea or the unnamed broker. Barclays' Sell case (target EUR 2.61, and the lowest FY2028 EPS of the six at 0.135) and UBS's Hold (target EUR 3.19) are unseen. Either may already make some of the points listed below as unknown. I therefore describe a dynamic as "not discussed" only in the sense of "not discussed by Jefferies or DNB Carnegie".
  • Investor commentary, including social media. The files contain none. I cannot assess it and do not guess. The structural facts suggest it is thin: a market value of about EUR 2.0bn, a free float of about one third to 40%, ten holders owning about 80%, and value traded of about USD 2.4m a day.

How well known each dynamic is (sell-side, per the files):

Dental as one driver behind growth, inventory, earn-out charges and tax By no one as a single point Dental seen simply as good news Good for EBITA; a drag on cash and reported EPS in the window (D-03) Unfavourable in the window; favourable beyond it
The earn-out charge as a damper on EPS By no one Not in view Narrows the EPS range; makes EPS a poor signal (D-04) Neutral
VOI: final ruling for Vimian (24 September 2026); USD 6.2m receivable carried at nil Barely Closed A possible one-off of about EUR 5m Favourable, small

Reading the table.

  • Known and priced: every positive operating dynamic. The narrative moved from "broken roll-up with a leadership vacuum" in February 2026 to "growth proven" by October, and the price moved with it.
  • Known and debated: the margin, cash conversion, US orthopaedics and the 2030 target. On these the sell-side's stance is balanced. Jefferies calls the risk and reward "balanced" and says the shares "already price in a recovery". DNB Carnegie argues for a premium.
  • Unknown or unarticulated: the lines below EBITA, the licensing income, the basis of EPS, the organic growth embedded in consensus revenue, and the comparisons that fall due in the first half of 2027. Of the eight rows of the table from the contingent-consideration charges downward, six point against the case over the next 18 months, one is neutral and one is favourable. This is the asymmetry that matters: a reader who knew only the narrative would be more optimistic on EPS than our work supports, and would find little in it to make them more optimistic than the price already is.

Why the gap is less unfavourable than that count suggests.

  • The sell-side has not capitulated to the story. Three of six ratings are Hold or Sell. The mean target (EUR 3.72) is below the price. The most recent estimate to enter (UBS, 4 November 2026) is the lowest.
  • Some of the unknowns are smaller than they sound. The licensing arrangement has a regulatory reason and has run undisturbed for at least three years. The contingent-consideration charges are non-cash until paid, and brokers who adjust them out are not wrong about cash.
  • Two unknowns are favourable. Receivables normalised as the company said they would, and the inventory that replaced them sits in one segment that has released stock before. And acquisitions will add to revenue and EBITDA estimates when announced.

Primarily the next 18 months. The narrative is at the stage where confirmation is expected and already paid for. The next items it will absorb are, in order, the Capital Markets Day; the fourth-quarter balance sheet; and then a first half of 2027 in which headline organic growth slows from 12% to about 7% and the Diagnostics margin falls year on year in its first test. None of these breaks the case as we model it. Each is a moment at which a narrative built on "double-digit growth, margins next" meets a fact that is weaker than its wording. The dynamics most likely to enter the narrative as new negatives are the organic slowdown against comparisons (certain to be noticed), the reported EPS and tax drag from earn-outs (likely to be noticed at the full-year report), and the licensing income (noticed only if something draws attention to it).

Secondarily, 18 months to three years out. The longer-dated positives are barely discussed and barely founded: the ageing of the pets acquired in 2020 to 2022 into the years of chronic care; the dental platform's possible share of the global companion-animal dental market; Nextmune's biologics unit; a share gain while DePuy Synthes is separated from Johnson & Johnson (clinicians interviewed in September 2026 said the separation would not change their choice of supplier). These are unpriced, and they are also undated and unsized, so I give them little weight. The longer-dated negative that is discussed is the 2030 target. The one that is not is the ending of US tariff exemptions for animal health on 20 January 2029.

Where I differ from the first set. The first set's group judgement on the narrative (score 54) described the remaining steps as "confirmations of what consensus already assumes". I agree, and go one step further. The new research shows that the items the narrative omits are specific, recurring and mostly unfavourable. The staircase ahead does not only lack positive surprises. It contains two or three small negative ones that are not yet in anyone's note.

Each layer's own material

What each layer was asked, what its scores mean, and the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices

Layer 1: Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

Written 10 November 2026, 20:44 to 21:07 UK, Opus, ricardinho login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Answers to the first set of questions: Vimian Group AB (VIMIAN-SE), as at 2026-11-10

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Reading notes for the Consumer (context, not a question)

1. Source check. The latest Earnings Review covers the third quarter of 2026: the period ending 30 September 2026, reported on 22 October 2026 (its §4 says so, and the FactSet financials block's last period is 09/2026). It can be relied on as the latest print. The earlier review covers Q2 2026 (reported 17 July 2026). The Risks, Business Foundations and Leadership digests (June 2026) pre-date both the Q2 and the Q3 prints. The Change Forces digest (21 September 2026) pre-dates Q3. The latest memo (24 September 2026, re-rated 1 October 2026) pre-dates Q3. It set three Q3 tests, which I score below. The price has since risen from SEK 35.7 (memo) to SEK 41.80 (10 November 2026).

2. Earnings bases. Never mix them. For FY2025 earnings per share:

  • reported basic EUR 0.059 (the company rounds this to "flat at EUR 0.06")
  • FactSet adjusted diluted EUR 0.075
  • brokers' adjusted basis (the base for consensus) EUR 0.088
  • Viewforth definition EUR 0.083
  • company "adjusted" about EUR 0.13

Brokers' FY2026 "adjusted" estimates range from EUR 0.089 (UBS) to EUR 0.140 (DNB Carnegie), a 57% spread for one year. That spread is a definitional problem, not a forecasting disagreement. Build the profit and loss account from the company's segment adjusted EBITA downwards and choose one EPS basis explicitly. Do not reconcile to the consensus EPS median.

3. Profit bases for FY2025:

  • company adjusted EBITA EUR 105.3m (24.8% margin)
  • reported EBIT EUR 66.8m (15.7%)
  • FactSet operating EBITDA EUR 110m (25.9%)
  • brokers' EBITDA EUR 114m (26.7%)
  • company pro-forma adjusted EBITDA margin 28.6%

Consensus EBITDA margins (27.5%, 28.4%, 28.9% for FY2026 to FY2028) are on the brokers' basis. Compare them with 26.7%, not with the FactSet history.

4. Known conflicts and defects in the files:

  • The Q2 Earnings Review digest's quarterly revenue and EBITA "series" are garbled (it gives Q3 2025 revenue as 96.6, which is a net working capital figure). Use FactSet's quarterly table and the Q3 review's prior-year comparatives instead: Q3 2025 revenue EUR 104.6m, adjusted EBITA EUR 25.5m.
  • FactSet's gross margin line (17.0%) is defective. The company's gross margin is 68.9%.
  • The FactSet segment consensus has a meaningless "Other" line. Speciality Pharma, MedTech, Veterinary Services and Diagnostics sum to the revenue consensus without it.
  • FactSet's FY2026 net income median (EUR 42.4m) does not reconcile to its EPS median (EUR 0.100). The broker subsets and bases differ.
  • FactSet's market capitalisation (22,331) is in SEK, not EUR. At EUR 3.80 a share and 530.4m diluted shares, the market capitalisation is about EUR 2.0bn. Enterprise value is about EUR 2.24bn to 2.28bn, depending on whether FactSet's net debt (EUR 225m) or the company's (EUR 262.4m) is used.
  • The employee net promoter score is either -36 or +30 for 2025. Rest nothing on it.
  • The Leadership digest's "EUR 300m by 2030 is a 7% CAGR" is an arithmetic error. The true figure is about 23% a year from 2025, or about 26% from 2026.
  • The acquisition count is given as 58, 59 or 62 since 2015.

5. Anchor facts (company basis unless stated; EUR m):

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 2

Research requests for the next set

Company: Vimian Group AB (VIMIAN-SE), a Stockholm-listed companion-animal health group reporting in euros. It has four segments:

  • Specialty Pharma: Nextmune, covering allergy diagnostics and allergen-specific immunotherapy, dermatology and specialised nutrition.
  • MedTech: Movora veterinary orthopaedics, iM3 veterinary dentistry and Norfolk Medical Products soft-tissue surgery.
  • Veterinary Services: the VetFamily buying group for independent clinics, plus co-owned clinics.
  • Diagnostics: Indical Bioscience and I-Vet.

Request: T-P1

Peers:

  • Swedencare: the closest Stockholm-listed companion-animal products roll-up.
  • Virbac (VIRP-FR): a European animal-health pharmaceutical group with a long published margin history.
  • Vetoquinol (VETO-FR): a companion-animal pharmaceutical group that delivered a margin plan through mix, in-housing and range simplification.
  • Zoetis and Elanco: scale benchmarks for animal-health cost structures, commercial spending and promotional costs.
  • IDEXX Laboratories: installed-base operating leverage in veterinary diagnostics, the benchmark for Indical's cost structure.
  • Lifco, Addtech, AddLife and Indutrade: decentralised Nordic serial acquirers, for how decentralised structures behave on central costs and operating leverage in up-cycles and down-cycles.
  • Arthrex Vet Systems and the DePuy Synthes veterinary business: the main orthopaedic implant competitors to Movora.
  • Covetrus and Patterson Companies: the distribution economics Vimian is replacing with direct sales.

Inputs:

  • What share of Vimian's operating costs is fixed in the short term in each segment? Costs in the 2025 annual report:
  • raw materials and merchandise EUR 132.2m
  • personnel EUR 108.2m
  • other external expenses EUR 81.4m
  • depreciation and amortisation EUR 37.9m

How quickly can each segment flex personnel and external costs when organic growth slows?

  • How did segment adjusted EBITA margins move with organic growth in each of these periods?
  • 2022, when group organic growth was 3.7%.
  • 2025, when MedTech organic growth was about -1%.
  • The first three quarters of 2026, when MedTech organic growth was 6%, 11% and 15%.

What explains the group adjusted EBITA margin falling from 26.3% in 2023 to 24.8% in 2025 while revenue rose?

  • What fixed cost did the January 2026 move of US orthopaedics from inside sales to a field sales force add? What volume is needed to absorb it, and what margin effect followed?
  • What did consolidating Diagnostics production from four sites to two save? What margin does management expect from companion-animal testing as it scales from 2027 (Q3 2026 earnings call, 22 Oct 2026)?
  • What did discontinuing more than 4,000 of 22,000 MedTech SKUs in Q4 2025 deliver in cost, gross margin and inventory?
  • How large are central costs, which the company described as increasing in Q3 2026? What do the thirteen-person group headquarters and other group functions cost, and how fast are they growing?
  • Under Vimian's decentralised, incentive-based integration model, which costs fall after a bolt-on acquisition (procurement, distribution, laboratory internalisation) and which do not? What cost synergies have been disclosed for iM3, AllAccem, Dental Focus, I-Vet or Norfolk Medical Products?
  • How much growth investment sits in the 2026 cost base and could be slowed in a downturn, and at what cost to growth? The candidates:
  • VetFamily entries in Japan, Portugal and Ireland
  • companion diagnostics
  • US field sales
  • Nextmune Bio
  • education centres
  • What did a former Group Business Controller mean in saying the decentralised structure limits "large-scale cost takeouts" (expert call, 13 Oct 2025)? Do decentralised Nordic acquirers show different margin behaviour in down-cycles from centralised peers?
  • How much R&D and software does Vimian capitalise (EUR 6.0m in 2025)? How would expensing it change segment margins?
  • What cost programmes have the named peers announced? What did each deliver against what was promised, and over what period? Examples: Vetoquinol's range simplification and in-housing; Swedencare's channel and manufacturing changes.

Focus: How much of a change in organic revenue reaches adjusted EBITA in Vimian's decentralised, outsourced model, both in the first year and after costs adjust. Which segments, if any, carry genuine fixed-cost operating leverage that could lift the group margin over the next 18 months to four years?

Request: T-P2

Peers:

  • Zoetis and Elanco: pricing behaviour in companion-animal dermatology and parasiticides, including Zoetis' 2026 promotional and gross-to-net reset.
  • Merck Animal Health: Numelvi against Apoquel.
  • Virbac and Vetoquinol: European mid-sized companion-animal pricing. Vetoquinol reported price increases above 2.5% in H1 2026.
  • Swedencare: dental and supplement pricing through retail and online channels.
  • ALK-Abelló: allergen immunotherapy pricing and uptake precedents in human medicine.
  • Arthrex Vet Systems, DePuy Synthes Vet and KYON: veterinary orthopaedic implant pricing.
  • Henry Schein and Patterson Companies: dental consumables and equipment pricing to clinics.
  • Mars Veterinary Health (VCA, Banfield), IVC Evidensia, VetPartners and CVS Group: corporate clinic buyers and their procurement power.

Operating conditions:

  • US clinic visits falling from 2022 to 2026: eighteen consecutive negative quarters to Q3 2026 (Vetsource and Morgan Stanley, October 2026).
  • Input-cost and wage inflation in 2022-2023.
  • The 2026 companion-animal price war: Zoetis promotions and gross-to-net rebates, and Elanco's launches of Zenrelia and Credelio Quattro.
  • Corporate clinic consolidation and centralised tendering in the US, UK and Nordics.
  • The UK CMA transparency regime: final decision 24 Mar 2026, binding order 22 Sep 2026.
  • Online prescription migration to Chewy and others.
  • US tariff episodes on imported medical devices and components in 2025-2026.
  • A weaker US dollar against the euro in 2025-2026.

Inputs:

  • What price increases has Vimian implemented by segment and by product line from 2023 to 2026? The research cites average increases of about 5%. How much of organic growth came from price and how much from volume and mix? Has any price been given back?
  • How did group gross margin move relative to input-cost inflation? Gross margin was 69.2% in 2023 and 68.9% in 2024 and 2025. What does that imply about pass-through, and with what lags?
  • How have allergen-specific immunotherapy prices and uptake behaved when symptomatic allergy drugs (Apoquel, Cytopoint, Zenrelia, Numelvi) were discounted or newly launched? What do veterinary dermatologists say about choosing between them?
  • How do corporate clinic groups negotiate with suppliers, and what discounts do they obtain against independent clinics? The product lines in question:
  • orthopaedic implant systems
  • dental equipment and consumables, such as SANOS sealant
  • allergy diagnostics and immunotherapy
  • How did veterinary orthopaedic implant prices and volumes behave in North America from summer 2024 to 2026, while owners deferred elective surgery?
  • Did US tariffs on devices or components lead Vimian or its competitors to raise implant prices? Were the increases held?
  • What are the contract structures, pass-through clauses and switching costs in each segment?
  • Specialty Pharma: framework agreements with performance-linked volume discounts.
  • VetFamily: supplier rebates shared with members in tiers.
  • MedTech: instrument sets, training and consignment.
  • Diagnostics: contracts generally shorter than one year.
  • How did moving from distributors to direct sales change realised prices and margins? Examples: Specialty Pharma in France, Belgium and the Netherlands; iM3's distributor conversions.
  • What do customers, competitors and the covering brokers (Jefferies, DNB Carnegie, Barclays, UBS, Nordea) say about Vimian's pricing now?

Focus: Can Vimian keep raising prices in allergy, dental and orthopaedics at or above cost inflation while its clinic customers are squeezed and consolidating and the industry leader is cutting net prices? Which of its product lines are most exposed to price pressure?

Request: T-P3

Why requested: Vimian's growth, margins and cash depend on several interacting dynamics, and their direction decides whether revenue growth converts into earnings and cash:

  • cross-selling into an expanding clinic base
  • the buying group's rebate flywheel
  • acquisitions funded from cash and debt
  • working capital absorbed by direct selling

Peers:

  • Lifco, Addtech, AddLife and Indutrade: decentralised serial acquirers whose acquisition-funding dynamics have been tested through cycles.
  • Swedencare: a leveraged animal-health roll-up whose growth and funding dynamics weakened in 2024-2026.
  • CVS Group: a veterinary consolidator with buying-scale and laboratory-internalisation dynamics.
  • IDEXX Laboratories: the installed-base and consumables dynamic in veterinary diagnostics.
  • Covetrus and Chewy: software and fulfilment dynamics that can route clinic purchasing around buying groups.
  • Zoetis: the price-volume dynamic in companion-animal therapeutics in 2025-2026.

Inputs:

  • Cross-selling dynamic. Cross-selling is reported at about 18% of group sales and about a third of Specialty Pharma's organic growth. Is penetration per clinic rising? Does it lift segment margins, or require extra selling cost?
  • Buying-group dynamic. VetFamily membership was 10,948 in 2025, 11,900 in Q2 2026 and 12,400 in Q3 2026. Do more members bring better supplier rebates and higher retention? Is corporate clinic consolidation shrinking the pool of independent clinics in mature markets?
  • Acquisition-funding dynamic. Net assets acquired were EUR 80.9m in 2024 and EUR 93.3m in 2025. Leverage was 2.0x net debt to pro-forma adjusted EBITDA at Q3 2026 against a 3.0x ceiling, and equity has been raised twice since the 2021 listing. Can cash conversion sustain the acquisition pace without equity?
  • Working-capital dynamic. Net working capital was EUR 96.6m at end-2025 and EUR 114.1m at Q2 2026, and the Q3 2026 net working capital cash flow was EUR 0.3m. Does direct selling raise receivables and inventory per euro of revenue, leaving less cash for acquisitions?
  • Training dynamic. 65,000 professionals were trained in 2025 and 1,300 veterinarians by Movora in Q3 2026. Does training raise implant and dental demand and switching costs over time?
  • Price-volume dynamic at clinics. Veterinary costs are rising 10% to 12% a year (Aon expert call, 28 Oct 2026). Do clinic price rises reduce visits and elective procedures, and does that feed back into suppliers' volumes?
  • Talent dynamic. Staff turnover was 19% in 2025. Do founders or key staff leave acquired units after earn-outs end, and does performance then fall?
  • For each dynamic: how it works step by step, the dated evidence that it is operating now, past precedents at the named peers (how far it ran, how long, and what stopped it), and what would strengthen, stall or reverse it.

Focus: Which of these dynamics are running now at Vimian, and in which direction? In particular, are the acquisition-funding and working-capital dynamics reinforcing or undermining each other over the next two to three years?

Request: T-P4

Why requested: Vimian's path from segment adjusted EBITA to earnings per share runs through large and variable items, so its EPS outcome depends on these inputs as much as on operations:

  • amortisation of acquired intangibles
  • items affecting comparability
  • financial items
  • a volatile tax rate
  • related-party income
  • broker EPS definitions that differ widely

Peers:

  • Swedencare: an acquisitive animal-health company with earn-outs and adjusted measures.
  • AddLife and Lifco: Nordic acquirers reporting adjusted EBITA with large amortisation of acquired intangibles.
  • CVS Group: adjusted EPS practice, R&D credits and contingent consideration.
  • Virbac and Vetoquinol: animal-health tax rates and financial items.
  • Dechra Pharmaceuticals (formerly listed): an acquisitive veterinary pharmaceutical company with an adjusted-earnings practice.

Inputs:

  • Amortisation of acquired intangibles (EUR 23.8m in 2025). What is its expected path with continued acquisitions? How does each covering broker treat it in "adjusted" EPS? FactSet showed FY2026 broker EPS estimates from EUR 0.089 to EUR 0.140 on 10 Nov 2026.
  • Items affecting comparability (EUR 14.7m in 2025). What is their composition (acquisition costs, stay-on bonuses, integration costs, contingent consideration revaluations), and how much recurs?
  • Net financial items (EUR 20.6m in 2025). The components:
  • interest on the EUR 150m senior unsecured bond, half swapped to fixed in August 2025
  • interest on floating bank debt (average interest cost 4.1% in Q1 2026)
  • lease interest
  • earn-out discount unwinding
  • currency revaluations

When do the bond and bank facilities mature, and on what terms might they be refinanced?

  • Tax rate (44% in 2023, 33% in 2024, 29% in 2025, 35% in Q1 2026). What role do non-deductible currency translation effects and the group structure play? What normal rate is achievable?
  • Licensing income invoiced by Specialty Pharma to an entity owned by a Specialty Pharma manager (EUR 9.4m in 2024 and EUR 9.7m in 2025, annual reports). What is licensed? What cost and margin attach to it? Is it at arm's length? Does it continue after the 2026 change in segment leadership?
  • Contingent consideration and earn-outs. What liabilities are outstanding, and what is the payment schedule?
  • Share count. What is the effect of LTIP 2025 and LTIP 2026/2029 dilution (capped at 3.0%) and of Class C, D and E share issues and repurchases? Do the 2030 ambition's funding terms ("without further capital injections") hold, and has the company commented on equity funding?
  • Capex. What path do capitalised R&D and software (EUR 6.0m in 2025) and capital expenditure follow, against the company's indication of about 4% of revenue for operational capex?
  • The Delaware appeal on the VOI indemnification award, and recovery of the USD 6.2m receivable classed as doubtful.
  • Currency translation sensitivity with no hedging: a 10% USD move worth EUR 15.7m of revenue and EUR 2.3m of EBIT (annual report). What is the effect of krona movements on the parent's costs?
  • For each input: what management guides, what each covering broker assumes, and how far apart they are.

Focus: Which inputs between segment adjusted EBITA and EPS will move most over FY2027 to FY2029? How far apart are management, each broker and the evidence on financial items, tax, amortisation and items affecting comparability, and how much of current consensus EPS growth rests on those lines rather than on operating profit?

Layer 2: Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

Written 11 November 2026, 20:24 to 20:49 UK, Opus, ricky login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Answers to the second set of questions: Vimian Group AB (VIMIAN-SE), as at 2026-11-11

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Reading notes for the Consumer (context, not a question)

1. What this set does. It turns the first set's judgements into one base case, built segment by segment from the nine-month 2026 actuals. The full inputs are in forecast-inputs.md. Money is in EUR millions. Figures are on the company's basis unless stated. "My arithmetic" or "derived" marks figures I calculated rather than found.

2. The three bases used in this set. Read these before comparing any figure with consensus.

  • EBITDA = company adjusted EBITDA less about EUR 6m a year of recurring roll-up costs. These are stay-on bonuses (EUR 3.9m in Specialty Pharma alone in 2025) and deal costs, which the company puts in items affecting comparability (IAC). For FY2025 this gives 119.4 − 6.0 = 113.4 (26.7% margin). FactSet's brokers'-basis FY2025 actual is EUR 114m (26.7%). My EBITDA margins can therefore be read directly against consensus EBITDA margins (27.5%, 28.4%, 28.9%). The match is my inference; the company publishes no such measure.
  • D&A excludes amortisation of acquired intangibles (PPA). That was EUR 14.1m in FY2025 (37.9 − 23.8) and EUR 12.7m in the first nine months of 2026.
  • EPS on three bases:
  • Model basis: excludes PPA and one-off IAC. Includes the recurring roll-up costs and all net financial items, the non-cash ones too. The model and my cross-check use this basis.
  • After-PPA basis: deducts PPA after tax and adds back the non-cash contingent-consideration and currency items. This is closest to UBS (0.089 for FY2026) and Nordea (0.090).
  • Reported (IFRS).
  • Correction to the AlphaSense EPS report (T-P4). That report says brokers "uniformly exclude" PPA amortisation. FactSet's FY2025 brokers'-basis actual of EUR 0.088 does not fit that. It reconciles to reported net income of 31.3, plus IAC of 14.7, plus about 9.8 of non-cash contingent-consideration items, with little tax relief: about 46.6m over 529m shares (my reconciliation). So most houses deduct PPA. DNB Carnegie adds it back and uses a 25% tax rate (0.140 for FY2026), which makes it the outlier.
  • Revenue growth includes unannounced bolt-ons. I assume EUR 65m of enterprise value a year at about 9x EBITDA, which is about EUR 25m of revenue a year. Consensus appears to include none. I show organic growth, acquisitions and currency separately so the Consumer can strip the deals out.

3. Facts that are new since the first set, or that change it:

  • Net financial items for the first nine months of 2026 were EUR 18.2m. Financing costs were 10.5 (average rate about 4%). Discounting of contingent consideration was 3.2. Probability adjustments were 4.5, because dental earn-outs were revalued up. Currency effects were 1.2. About EUR 9m of the nine-month charge is non-cash.
  • Adjusted EBITDA was 33.2, 33.7 and 33.9 in Q1 to Q3 2026: 100.8 for nine months, a 28.2% margin. Adjusted EBITA was 88.1 (24.6%).
  • Net working capital on the company's basis rose again in Q3, from EUR 114.1m (Q2) to EUR 116.4m (25% of revenue), "driven by higher inventories in MedTech". Inventory was EUR 98.3m and trade receivables EUR 67.5m (Q3 report, via T-P3). The first set had FactSet's basis flat (137 to 138). The company's basis shows the stock did not normalise and moved up. The SKU cull has not released inventory yet.
  • Capitalised R&D and software was EUR 7.7m in nine months, against 3.6m a year earlier. It has doubled.
  • Central costs were EUR 3.0m in Q3 against 1.8m a year earlier (+67%), of which long-term incentive costs were 0.6m.
  • Debt maturities: the EUR 150m senior unsecured bond is a bullet due 13 May 2028. The EUR 330m bank facility runs to May 2030.
  • Contingent consideration was carried at EUR 31.6m at Q3 2026, down from 46.4m at end-2025. The maximum payout is EUR 142.1m across 25 obligations (Q3), against EUR 113.2m across 27 in the 2025 annual report. The cap has risen. iM3's optional tranche can reach EUR 50m.
  • VOI litigation: the Delaware Supreme Court ruled finally for Vimian on 24 September 2026 (T-P4). The USD 6.2m receivable is still carried at zero. The first set said the outcome was unreported. It is now a possible one-off of about EUR 5m of cash, timing unknown.
  • Training: the 2025 annual report says 48,000 professionals were educated, not 65,000 (T-P3). Use 48,000.
  • Related-party licensing income (EUR 9.7m in 2025). T-P4 reports that the arrangement exists because of local pharmacy-licensing rules: Specialty Pharma licenses formulas and the manager's entity distributes. It also reports that management describes the pricing as arm's length. I hold this at low-to-medium confidence, because I could not check the wording. If it is right, the item is a structural dependency more than a governance alarm: EUR 9.7m of high-margin revenue rests on one person's licensed entity. I partly revise the first set's C-86. The risk is continuity and concentration, not misconduct. It remains the largest unexplained profit item.
  • IAC for the first nine months of 2026 were about EUR 8m (derived; medium confidence): adjusted EBITA 88.1, less PPA of about 19.3, less implied reported EBIT of about 61. The implied EBIT comes from net income of 27.1, tax of about 34%, minorities of about 1 and net financial items of 18.2.

Organic growth comes from niche penetration, about 3 to 4 points of price matched by cost inflation, and cross-selling. No market-volume recovery is assumed: US clinic visits have fallen for 18 consecutive quarters. US orthopaedics is flat to low single digit. The bolt-ons are EUR 65m of enterprise value a year at about 9x EBITDA, about EUR 25m of revenue a year, half in the year of purchase.

EBITDA margin basis. EBITDA is company adjusted EBITDA less about EUR 6m to 7m a year of recurring roll-up costs (stay-on bonuses, deal costs) that the company reports in items affecting comparability. FY2025 on this basis: 113.4 (26.7%), equal to FactSet's brokers'-basis actual of EUR 114m (26.7%). The margin path is built from the segment margins above.

  • Gross margin held at about 69%.
  • Central costs grow 7% a year.
  • EBITDA margin rises faster than the EBITA margin because D&A excluding PPA rises: capitalised development and software was EUR 7.7m in the first nine months of 2026, against 3.6m.

Other inputs:

  • D&A excludes PPA amortisation. PPA is about EUR 26m (2026) rising to 32.5m (2030) with deals; it is excluded from the model, as instructed.

From 2027: cash interest at about 6% of average FactSet-basis net debt (all-in, including fees and cash drag; the average rate on gross debt is about 4%), plus EUR 4.5m a year of non-cash earn-out charges. The bond is refinanced in 2028 at slightly higher cost.
  • Tax is the adjusted rate on model-basis pre-tax profit. It corresponds to a reported effective rate of about 33.7% (2026), 30.4%, 29.4%, 28.5% and 28.4%.
  • Shares. About 0.4% a year of incentive-plan dilution. EUR 3.4m of warrant-exercise cash in 2026, about 1.1m shares.
  • Working capital is FactSet's basis: receivables plus inventories less payables, at year-end, over trailing revenue. It was 27.6% at end-2025 and 29.5% at Q3 2026. It is held at 28.5%, with no release.
  • Lease payments are my estimate. They are not disclosed in the files; lease interest of EUR 0.5m in 2025 implies small lease liabilities.
  • Acquisitions less disposals is entered net of the working capital the acquired businesses bring, about EUR 5m to 7m a year, because the model's working-capital line already charges it on total revenue. Gross cash is about EUR 85m (2026) and EUR 77m a year after, including earn-out settlements: EUR 31.6m carried at Q3 2026, maximum EUR 142.1m.
